Modeling Discrete Data with High-Order Vector Potts Models
Aaron De Clercq, Merijn Moody, Clélia de Mulatier

Abstract
Modeling high-dimensional data is challenging, yet essential to understanding many complex systems. Maximum entropy models such as Ising and Potts models have been used extensively to capture pairwise interactions from correlation patterns in data, allowing to infer graphical representations of complex systems from observations (e.g., from protein sequences, or neural population activity). Recently, there has been growing interest in modeling higher-order correlation patterns involving simultaneously three or more variables. While progress has been made in binary data with high-order Ising models, we extend this framework to the more general case of discrete data. We introduce q q -state spin models, a complete family of maximum entropy models that generalize the vector Potts model to include long-range and arbitrary high-order interactions. Even in the pairwise case, compared to the standard vector Potts model, our models allow for more diverse interaction types. We discuss their statistical interpretation through examples and relate these models to discrete Fourier analysis. Using a loop expansion of the partition function, we show that the statistical properties of q q -state spin models are fully captured by the algebraic structure of their interactions. We define gauge transformations under which this structure, and therefore the partition function, remains invariant. Models equivalent under gauge transformations can thus be seen as different representations of the same abstract statistical model, despite potentially having interactions of different orders, extending results from the binary case. For practical application of this framework to data analysis, we focus on a subset of models known, in the binary case, as Minimally Complex Models, generalizing them to discrete data. We obtain a closed-form expression for the marginal likelihood of these models, enabling fast model selection. We illustrate their use with simple real-world examples.



Keywords
statistical inference
\cdot

discrete data
\cdot

Potts model
\cdot

\cdot

1
Introduction

The analysis of high-dimensional data has become a central challenge in modern science, driven by the explosion of high-throughput experiments. Whether analyzing the firing patterns of neural populations, the sequence variations in protein families, or gene regulatory networks, the primary goal is often the same: to infer the microscopic laws or structural constraints governing the system
based on macroscopic observations obtained from experimental data.
From a statistical modeling standpoint, this problem is frequently addressed using the principle of maximum entropy
25
, which enables the construction of the least biased probability distribution consistent with measured observables.
Over the last three decades, this approach has been successful at reconstructing graphical models of microscopic interactions in complex systems from discrete data, specifically pairwise Ising and Potts models.
More generally, physicists have reformulated the task of extracting information from large, high-dimensional data as an inverse statistical problem
39
. This research has found broad application across the biological sciences, such as the reconstruction of neural functional connectivity from population activity
48
;
13
, the inference of gene regulatory networks from sequencing data
33
, and the determination of three-dimensional protein structures from sequence variations
53
;
35
;
17
;
6
;
24
;
46
.

Many complex systems however have high-order interactions between their components, which are simultaneous interactions between three or more components, and can exhibit behaviors that are not well explained by pairwise models. In data, this can appear for instance as high-order correlation patterns between the states of the components (i.e., correlations between three or more variables) that cannot be explained by the pairwise correlation structure of the data.
This general problem has led to a recent surge of interest for models with high-order interactions in many fields of complex systems
8
;
7
.
From a data modeling perspective, finding which high-order interactions are most relevant to explain the data (if any) is particularly challenging due to the combinatorial explosion of the number of possible interactions.

Recent progress has been made in addressing this challenge for
binary data
. To model high-order statistical patterns, References
34
;
9
proposed a generalization of Ising-like maximum entropy models to include interactions of arbitrarily high order, which the authors generally call “spin models” (also known in the literature as “high-order Ising models” or “generalized Ising models”
52
).
Consider a binary dataset composed of multiple observations of the state
\boldsymbol{s}=(s_{1},\,\cdots,\,s_{n})
of
n
binary variables
s_{i}
taking values in
\{-1,+1\}
(called “spin” variables).
A spin model is a parametric family of probability distributions of the form
9
:

p(\boldsymbol{s}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g},\mathcal{M})=\frac{1}{Z_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})}\,\exp\left({\;\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}}g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})}\right)\,,

(1)

characterized by the choice of a set
\mathcal{M}=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(1)},\,\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(K)}\}
of
K
interactions of arbitrary order between the spins. Each interaction is represented by an
n
-dimensional binary vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}
that indicates which spins are interacting,
and the functions
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})
define how the variables interact, taking the form of product operators:

\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}{s_{i}}^{\mu_{i}}\,,\qquad\forall\boldsymbol{\mu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{n}\,.

(2)

For example, in a five-spin system, a three-body interaction between
s_{1}
,
s_{3}
and
s_{4}
is represented by the binary vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(1,0,1,1,0)
and corresponds to the operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})=s_{1}s_{3}s_{4}
.
Ising-like (pairwise) models only have operators of the form
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})=s_{i}
and
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})=s_{i}s_{j}
between pairs of spins.
The vector
\boldsymbol{g}=(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}}
is a vector of real parameters, and the normalization factor
Z_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})
, known in physics as the partition function of the model, is given by:

\displaystyle Z_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})=\sum_{\boldsymbol{s}}\,\exp\left({\;\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}}g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})}\right)\,.

(3)

The spin models (
1
) are maximum entropy models constrained to produce chosen expectation values of the operators in
\mathcal{M}
.
These values correspond to (high-order) moments of the multivariate systems that are considered as the relevant macroscopic observables by the model.
The coefficients
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
are Lagrange multipliers, which are usually fitted so that the model average
\langle\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\rangle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{s}}p(\boldsymbol{s}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g},\mathcal{M})\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})
of each operator in
\mathcal{M}
matches its empirical average in the data.
The family of spin models is thus able to capture any possible patterns of binary data (i.e., any choice of the multivariate moments). In particular, the complete model with all
(2^{n}-1)
interactions can fit all high-order correlation patterns.
In this context, identifying which patterns of the data are relevant is equivalent to finding
the subset of interactions that best models the data,
which is challenging due to the super-exponential number of possible choices.

An interesting property of this family of models, with important implications for modeling, is that it is closed under changes of representation of the spin variables, called
gauge transformations
by
9
.
A gauge transformation is a bijective map of the form
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\boldsymbol{b}(\boldsymbol{s})
, which defines a new basis of spin variables
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{1},\cdots,\sigma_{n})
in terms of the original variables
\boldsymbol{s}
using a set of independent operators
\boldsymbol{b}(\boldsymbol{s})=(\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}(\boldsymbol{s}),\dots,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}}(\boldsymbol{s}))
(see example in Fig.
1
). The probability distribution (
1
) of any spin model can be rewritten in the new basis
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
by re-expressing each spin operator in terms of the new variables. In the new basis, the operators still have the form of spin operators,
and the model probability distribution thus remains that of a spin model
1
1

1

Under a gauge transformation, any spin operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})
can be transformed by re-expressing it in terms of the new basis variables
\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{b}(\boldsymbol{s})
. Using the inverse transformation
\boldsymbol{s}=\boldsymbol{b}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})
, the transformed operator is given by:

\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}](\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})\doteq\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{b}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}))\,.

(4)

This yields a transformed model
\mathcal{T}_{b}[\mathcal{M}]
, which is obtained by transforming each operator within
\mathcal{M}
:

\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}[\mathcal{M}]\doteq\{\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}]\mid\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\mathcal{M}\}\,.

(5)

Consequently, the probability of finding the system in state
\boldsymbol{s}
under model
\mathcal{M}
is identical to the probability of finding the system in the transformed state
\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}
under the transformed model
\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}[\mathcal{M}]
:

p(\boldsymbol{s}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g},\mathcal{M})=p(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}[\mathcal{M}])\,,

(6)

where the parameter vector
\boldsymbol{g}^{\prime}
is a permutation of the original parameters
\boldsymbol{g}
, ensuring that the transformed operator in
\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}[\mathcal{M}]
is coupled to the same parameter as the original operator in
\mathcal{M}
.

9
.
We denote
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}[\mathcal{M}]
the spin model resulting from the gauge transformation
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\boldsymbol{b}(\boldsymbol{s})
of a model
\mathcal{M}
.
Crucially, models related by a gauge transformation can have interactions of different orders. In particular, standard pairwise models can map to models containing high-order interactions. Moreover, models related by gauge transformations have the same partition function
Z_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})

9
,
and can thus be understood as different representations of the same abstract statistical model.

This gives rise to the concept of
equivariant model selection
, as illustrated in Figure
1
. Suppose we have a binary dataset
\boldsymbol{\hat{s}}
written in terms of the variables
\boldsymbol{s}
(Bob’s dataset) and our model selection procedure yields an optimal model
\mathcal{M}
. If we rewrite the dataset in terms of the new basis variables
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\boldsymbol{b}(\boldsymbol{s})
using a gauge transformation and perform the exact same model selection procedure to the new dataset
\boldsymbol{\hat{\sigma}}
(Alice’s dataset), it will yield a model
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
that is a priori different from
\mathcal{M}
.
One can expect an unbiased model selection procedure among all spin models to yield models that are consistent across different representations of the data. In this example, this would mean that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is related to
\mathcal{M}
by the same gauge transformation,
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}[\mathcal{M}]
.
Such a model selection procedure is called equivariant.
Because the family of pairwise models is not closed under gauge transformations, equivariant model selection is fundamentally impossible unless we encompass high-order interactions within our model selection framework. This underscores the importance of developing a comprehensive high-order framework for statistical inference.

Figure 1:
Example illustrating equivariant model selection
for binary datasets
\boldsymbol{\hat{s}}=\{\boldsymbol{s}^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N}
, where each datapoint
\boldsymbol{s}^{(i)}
is a state of a three-spin system
\boldsymbol{s}=(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})\in\{-1,+1\}^{3}
. A model selection procedure is equivariant if, for a basis transformation of the dataset
\mathcal{T}\colon\boldsymbol{s}\to\boldsymbol{\sigma}
, the models obtained from the model selection procedure on
\boldsymbol{\hat{s}}
(Bob’s data) and
\boldsymbol{\hat{\sigma}}
(Alice’s data) are related by the same transformation
\mathcal{T}
.
The left panel shows an example of an equivariant model selection procedure, for which the spin model found in Alice’s data representation corresponds to the model found in Bob’s representation rewritten in Alice’s basis,
i.e.
\mathcal{M}_{\rm Alice}=\mathcal{T}[\mathcal{M}_{\rm Bob}]
.
In the right panel, Alice and Bob have the same data as in the left panel, but they can only select among pairwise models. In this case, Bob will find the same model as in the left panel because his model is already pairwise, but Alice’s model cannot be the same because her model has a three body interaction. Instead, Alice will find a pairwise model that likely has similarities with her left-panel model (such as the projection of the three-body interaction onto pairwise interactions), but which cannot be consistent with Bob’s findings anymore. This gives an example where it is clear that pairwise model selection is not equivariant.

While this framework is well-established for binary variables, a comprehensive framework for
discrete data

(where variables can take
q
different values with
q\geq 2
) remains under-explored. In this paper, we extend this framework to the more general case of discrete data.
Taking inspiration from the binary case,
we define a complete family of maximum entropy models able to reproduce arbitrary multivariate moments of the discrete data.
This family of models, which we call
q
-state spin models, generalizes the vector Potts model to include arbitrary high-order interactions, and reduces to the models in (
1
) when
q=2
.
The complete
q
-state spin models, with all
2^{q^{n}-1}
parameters, can model any patterns of discrete data. More specifically, we can relate this model to a Fourier decomposition of the log-probability.

Similarly to the binary case, we identified changes of representation of the discrete system (gauge transformations) that transform a
q
-state spin model into another
q
-state spin model, with interactions that can be of different orders.
Using a loop expansion of the partition function, we show that these models have the same partition function, and thus correspond to different representations of the same abstract statistical model. This defines equivalent classes of
q
-state spin models, which, in particular, allows for the development of equivariant model selection. We introduce a linear algebra representation of spin models, which provides a comprehensive mathematical framework for working with spin models.
In particular, this framework formalizes the definition of gauge transformations, the characterization of the loop structure and of equivalence classes, and the definition of symmetries in data and models.

While the complete model can be used to infer all probability distributions, it is generally over-parameterized for modeling systems with limited data. The challenge of finding a good model for data lies in selecting the most concise description that captures the relevant statistics without overfitting. Ideally, one would compare all possible models to find the one that best describes a dataset
\boldsymbol{\hat{s}}=\{\boldsymbol{s}^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N}
, i.e., the one achieving an optimal balance between goodness-of-fit and simplicity. Assuming no prior preference between models, we aim to find the model with the largest
evidence
(or
marginal likelihood
)
31
:

P(\boldsymbol{\hat{s}}\,|\,\mathcal{M})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}\!d\boldsymbol{g}\,\prod_{i=1}^{N}\,p\big(\boldsymbol{s}^{(i)}|\,\boldsymbol{g},\mathcal{M}\big)\;P_{0}(\boldsymbol{g}\,|\,\mathcal{M})\,,

(7)

where
P_{0}(\boldsymbol{g}\,|\,\mathcal{M})
is a prior distribution over the model parameters. Assuming Jeffreys’ prior, in the limit of a large number
N
of datapoints, maximizing the evidence is also equivalent to finding the model minimizing the description length of the data
44
;
43
;
21
;
5
;
38
:

\displaystyle L(\boldsymbol{\hat{s}}\,|\,\mathcal{M})=\max_{\boldsymbol{g}}\log P(\boldsymbol{\hat{s}}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g},\,\mathcal{M})-\frac{K}{2}\,\log\left(\frac{N}{2\pi}\right)-c_{\mathcal{M}}+O\left(\frac{1}{N}\right)\,.

(8)

The first term measures goodness-of-fit of the model, while the subsequent terms penalize model complexity, based on the number
K
of parameters and the geometric complexity
c_{\mathcal{M}}

38
;
9
.
Calculating these quantities for all possible models is computationally intractable. To remedy this, we generalize the definition of minimally complex models (MCMs), introduced for binary data in Ref.
15
, to the discrete case.
MCMs are a sub-family of the
q
-state spin models that correspond to factorizations of the model probability distribution in some spin basis. A remarkable advantage of MCMs is that the model evidence (
7
) has a closed-form expression,
making Bayesian model selection among MCMs feasible. The MCM with the largest evidence then identifies an optimal factorization of the empirical probability distribution that matches the data structure.

The paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we define high-order vector Potts models, as maximum entropy models constrained to reproduce high-order moments of a discrete spin system.
In Section
3
, we obtain a loop expansion of the partition function, which connects the statistical properties of the model to its algebraic structure (the loops of the model), extending results from the binary case to general
q
.
In Section
4
, we introduce a linear algebra representation of spin models, which gives a formal framework for working with equivalence classes of
q
-state spin models.
Finally, in Section
5
, we focus on the subset of Minimally Complex Models and, in Section
6
, we illustrate their use for model selection in real-world discrete data.

2
Complete family of maximum entropy models for discrete data

In this section, we define a complete family of parametric models for discrete data. Inspired by the high-order generalization of the Ising model in Eq. (
1
)
34
;
9
, we define maximum entropy models able to reproduce any (high-order) moments of a discrete planar spin system, introducing a high-order generalization of the planar Potts model
(
41
)
for statistical inference of discrete data.
The resulting family of probability distributions extends the maximum entropy models in Eq. (
1
) to any discrete values of
q\geq 2
.
In Sec.
2.3
, we draw attention to the fact that these models correspond to a spatial discrete Fourier decomposition of the empirical log-probability (or energy function), which has interesting implications regarding the use of these models. Finally in Sec.
2.4
, we give some insight into the way these models encode patterns of discrete data.

2.1
Definitions –
q
-state spin models with interactions of arbitrary order

We are interested in studying systems of
n
random variables that can take
q
different values (where
q
is a positive integer).
One can map these
q
values to integers between
0
and
(q-1)
without losing in generality.
We consider discrete variables in this format and define planar spin variables by mapping these
q
values to evenly spaced positions on the unit circle.
The choice of this initial mapping must be taken into account when interpreting the interactions of the model, which will be discussed in Sec.
2.4
.

Definition 1
.

Discrete variables:
q
-state spins and colors.

Consider
n
discrete random variables
(\alpha_{1},\,\cdots,\,\alpha_{n})
taking integer values in
[0,\,q-1]
:

\displaystyle{\rm for}\;{\rm all}\;j\in\{1,\cdots,n\},\qquad\alpha_{j}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})\,.

(9)

This system can be mapped to the system of
n
complex variables
(s_{1},\,\cdots,s_{n})
taking values in the set of the
q
-th roots of unity, using:

\displaystyle{\rm for}\;{\rm all}\;j\in\{1,\cdots,n\},\qquad\qquad s_{j}=\exp\left({\displaystyle\frac{2i\pi}{q}\alpha_{j}}\right)=z_{q}^{\;\alpha_{j}}\,,

(10)

where
z_{q}=\exp(\frac{2\pi}{q}\,i)
is the first
q
-th root of unity.
We refer to the variables
s_{j}
as
q
-state spin variables
and to the variables
\alpha_{j}
as their
colors
.

Figure 2:
Representation for
q
-state spin variables in the complex plane
for different values of
q
. For a given value of
q
, the color variable
\alpha_{i}
can take any integer value modulo
q
,
\alpha_{i}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
.
The corresponding
q
-state spin variable then takes the complex value
s_{i}=z_{q}^{\,\alpha_{i}}
, where
z_{q}
is the first
q
-th root of unity. The spin variable can thus be represented in the complex plane by a random unit vector which can point in any of the
q
different directions corresponding to the
q
-th roots of unity. Observe that
z_{q}^{q-\alpha_{i}}
and
z_{q}^{\,\alpha_{i}}
are complex conjugate spin values,
z_{q}^{q-\alpha_{i}}=(z_{q}^{\,\alpha_{i}})^{*}
, which correspond to mirrored directions of
s_{i}
with respect to the
x
-axis.

Equation (
10
) defines a bijection between the two sets of random variables. In the complex plane, the spin variable
s_{j}
can be seen as a random unit vector whose direction is given by the color variable
\alpha_{j}
(see Fig.
2
), similarly to the planar spins introduced in the very first version of the Potts model
(
41
)
.
We denote the state of the system by the vector
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\,\cdots,\alpha_{n})\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
when expressed in terms of the variables
\alpha_{j}
and by the complex vector
\boldsymbol{s}=(s_{1},\,\cdots,s_{n})
when expressed in terms of the variables
s_{j}
. In the following, we will use interchangeably the notation with dependency in
\boldsymbol{s}
or in
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
.
For
q=2
, the variables
s_{j}
can take the values
+1
(for
\alpha_{j}=0
) or
-1
(for
\alpha_{j}=1
), just like in the Ising model and its high-order generalization (
1
).
One reason for introducing the spin variables
s_{j}
in the form of (
10
) is that they satisfy the property
s_{j}^{\,q}=1
. In the binary case, this property is useful for defining gauge transformations and to obtain a loop expansion of the partition function
9
, both of which we aim to generalize here.
Below, this property is also useful for defining a complete set of high-order moments from which a complete family of maximum entropy model can constructed.

2.1.1
High-order moments, spin operators, and complete models

We aim to define a complete family of parametric models, able to capture any pattern of discrete data. This can be done by defining maximum entropy models constrained to reproduce any choice of moments of the observed system (including high-order moments).
In the following, we construct a family of maximum entropy models able to reproduce arbitrary-order moments of the spin variables
s_{j}
, as opposed to moments of the colors
\alpha_{j}
.

Definition 2
.

Moments of an
n
-spin system.

For the system of
n

q
-state spin variables
s_{j}
, there are at most
(q^{n}-1)
possible distinct moments, each corresponding to a product of the spins raised to some power between
0
and
(q-1)
:

\displaystyle\bigg\langle\,\prod_{j=1}^{n}\,s_{j}^{\;\mu_{j}}\,\bigg\rangle\,,\qquad\textrm{for all integer values of }\;\mu_{j}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})\,,

(11)

except
(\mu_{1},\,\cdots,\,\mu_{n})=\boldsymbol{0}\,
for which the product is simply equal to
1
.
The angle brackets
\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle
denotes the ensemble average.

Because the spin variables defined in Eq. (
10
) verify
2
2

2

For all
\mu_{j}\geq q\,
,
\;s_{j}^{\mu_{j}}=\exp\left({\frac{2i\pi}{q}\alpha_{j}\mu_{j}}\right)=\exp\left({\frac{2i\pi}{q}\alpha_{j}(\mu_{j}\bmod{q})}\right)=s_{j}^{\mu_{j}\bmod{q}}\,
.

that
s_{j}^{\mu_{j}}=s_{j}^{\mu_{j}\bmod{q}}
,
all the moments corresponding to values of
\mu_{j}\geq q
are equal to lower order moments of the form of Eq. (
11
), and therefore there are at most
(q^{n}-1)
possible distinct moments.
These moments define a complete set of patterns of discrete data, i.e., they can fully describe any possible statistical structure of discrete data.
We introduce the
n
-dimensional vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(\mu_{1},\,\cdots,\,\mu_{n})\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
,
such that each value of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
uniquely identifies one of the moments in Eq. (
11
) (except for
\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{0}
).
The maximum entropy model
25
;
50
constrained to reproduce all these moments then takes the form:

\displaystyle p(\boldsymbol{s}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g})=\frac{1}{Z(\boldsymbol{g})}\,\exp\Bigg({\;\sum_{\vecmu\in[\Field^n]^*}
g_{\vecmu}\,\phi^{\vecmu}(\spin)}\Bigg)\,,

(12)

where the sum is over all
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
except
\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{0}
(indicated by the star),
Z(\boldsymbol{g})
is a normalization factor corresponding to the partition function of the model,
the
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
’s are
(q^{n}-1)
complex parameters corresponding to Lagrange multipliers (
\boldsymbol{g}
is the vector of all the parameters
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
), and the
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})
’s are the spin operators associated to each of the moments in Eq. (
11
) defined as follow.

Definition 3
.

(Complex) Spin operators of arbitrary order.

For all states
\boldsymbol{s}
of the
n
-spin system and all vectors
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(\mu_{1},\cdots,\mu_{n})\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
, we define the spin operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})
as the monomial:

\displaystyle\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})=\prod_{j=1}^{n}s_{j}^{\;\mu_{j}}\,.

(13)

For binary systems (
q=2
), the exponents
\mu_{j}
’s can only take the values
0
or
1
, and we recover the Ising spin operators defined in Eq. (
2
)
34
;
9
.
Using Eq. (
10
), the spin operators can also be written in term of the color variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n})\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
:

\displaystyle\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\exp\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}{\displaystyle\frac{2i\pi}{q}\alpha_{j}\mu_{j}}\right)=\exp\left({\displaystyle\frac{2i\pi}{q}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right)=z_{q}^{\;\boldsymbol{\mu}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\,,

(14)

where
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}
is the scalar product of
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}
in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
.
Note that for
q=2
,
\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=(-1)^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\{\pm 1\}
.

The ensemble averages of the spin operators in Eq. (
13
) define all the moments of the spin system (
11
) (except for the operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{0}}(\boldsymbol{s})=1
).
Thus, at best fit of the maximum entropy model (
12
), the values taken by these moments in the model are precisely equal to their values in the data, because these are the patterns the model was constrained to reproduce
25
;
50
.

One caveat of the model in Eq. (
12
) is that the term in the exponential can take complex values (as both
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
and
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})
can be complex), and therefore the state probabilities
p(\boldsymbol{s}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g})
can be complex.
Observe however that for each complex operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})
, there is another operator
\phi^{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})=[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})]^{*}
that is equal to its complex conjugate
3
3

3

\phi^{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})=[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})]^{*}
because
s_{j}^{-\mu_{j}}=\exp\left(-{\frac{2i\pi}{q}\alpha_{j}\mu_{j}}\right)=[\exp({\frac{2i\pi}{q}\alpha_j\mu_j})]^{*}=\big[s_{j}^{\,\mu_{j}}\big]^{*}

for all
\mu_{j}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})
.
in the model (
12
).
These operators correspond to the vectors
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
such that
-\boldsymbol{\mu}\neq\boldsymbol{\mu}
, where
-\boldsymbol{\mu}
denotes
4
4

4

The vector
-\boldsymbol{\mu}
is defined in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
by applying a “minus” sign to all the entries of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
and taking them modulo
q
.
the vector
-\boldsymbol{\mu}=(q-\mu_{1},\cdots,

q-\mu_{n})\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
(for
-\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\mu}
, the operators already take real values
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})=\pm 1
).
In other words, the two operators
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
and
\phi^{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}
impose similar constraints to the model.
For the state probabilities
p(\boldsymbol{s}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g})
to be real, we can then impose that:

\displaystyle g_{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}=g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\,*}\,,\qquad\textrm{for all}\;\,\boldsymbol{\mu}=(\mu_{1},\,\cdots,\,\mu_{n})\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}\,,

(15)

where
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}
is the complex conjugate of
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
.
Consequently, each pair of conjugate operators
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
and
\phi^{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}
is only parameterized by two real parameters,
a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
and
b_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
(instead of four), chosen below such that
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=(a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}-ib_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})/2=g_{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}
(see App.
A.1
).
They constrain the model on reproducing respectively the real and the imaginary parts of
\langle\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})\rangle
.
For values of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
such that
-\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\mu}
, the operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})\in\{\pm 1\}
is real and Eq. (
15
) implies that
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}
is also real.
Equation (
12
) can thus be re-written under the form of a maximum entropy model constrained to reproduce the real and the imaginary part of all the moments (
11
) of a spin system:

\displaystyle p(\boldsymbol{s}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g})=\frac{1}{Z(\boldsymbol{g})}\,\exp[\;\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{\mu}\in[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]^{*}\\
\boldsymbol{\mu}\leq-\boldsymbol{\mu}\end{subarray}}\left(a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})+b_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\psi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)\Bigg]\,,

(16)

where the operators
\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})={\rm Re}[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})]
and
\psi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})={\rm Im}[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})]
are respectively the real and imaginary parts of
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})
(see Definition
4
– note that
\psi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})=0
for all
\boldsymbol{\mu}
such that
-\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\mu}
).
For all
\boldsymbol{\mu}
such that
-\boldsymbol{\mu}\neq\boldsymbol{\mu}
, contributions from conjugate operators have been grouped together as
a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}+g_{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}
and
b_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=i(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}-g_{-\boldsymbol{\mu}})
.
For
\boldsymbol{\mu}
such that
-\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\mu}
, we use the convention
a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}
.
The constraint
\boldsymbol{\mu}\leq-\boldsymbol{\mu}
ensures that the sum includes each conjugate pair
(\boldsymbol{\mu},-\boldsymbol{\mu})
only once
5
5

5

The ordering between operators
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
is based on the ordering of the integers represented by
\boldsymbol{\mu}
in base
q
.
. Here, the vector
\boldsymbol{g}
denotes the vector of real parameters
a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
and
b_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
.
In Eq. (
16
), it is clear that the state probabilities
p(\boldsymbol{s}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g})
are now real.

Definition 4
.

(Real) Spin operators of arbitrary order.

For all state
\boldsymbol{s}
of the
n
-spin system
and all vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(\mu_{1},\cdots,\mu_{n})\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
, we define the real
spin operators
\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})
and
\psi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})
as respectively the real and the imaginary parts of
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})
:

\displaystyle\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})={\rm Re}[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})]=\frac{\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})+\phi^{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})}{2}\qquad{\rm and}\qquad\psi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})={\rm Im}[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})]=\frac{\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})-\phi^{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})}{2i}\,.

(17)

For all
\boldsymbol{\mu}
such that
-\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\mu}
,
\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})=\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})\in\{\pm 1\}
and
\psi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})=0
.
These operators can also be written in term of the discrete variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
as:

\displaystyle\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\cos\left({\displaystyle\frac{2\pi}{q}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right)\qquad{\rm and}\qquad\psi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\sin\left({\displaystyle\frac{2\pi}{q}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right)\,,

(18)

where
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n})\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}

and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}
is the scalar product of
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}
in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
.

Number of real parameters.

Requiring
p(\boldsymbol{s}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g})
to be real reduces the effective number of real parameters in the model of Eq. (
12
). There are two real parameters,
a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
and
b_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
, for each pair of conjugate operators and only one real parameter
a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
for each real operator (for which
-\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\mu}
).
Therefore, the model has only
(q^{n}-1)
real parameters in total (equal to the number of non-zero vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
).
This is consistent with the number of parameters one would need to model the probability distribution by its
(q^{n}-1)
independent state probabilities (where the “
-1
” comes from normalization).
Because the model (
12
) (resp. (
16
)) provides an alternative complete description of the discrete system,
there exits a bijection between the
(q^{n}-1)
state probabilities and the
(q^{n}-1)
real parameters of the model. This means that for any distribution of the state probabilities
6
6

6

assuming that all the probabilities are strictly positive

p(\boldsymbol{s})
, there is a unique value of the vector of model parameters
\boldsymbol{g}
that creates this distribution,
which will be given in Eq. (
46
) (resp. (
47
)).

2.1.2
Families of maximum entropy models for discrete data

Equation (
12
) with the constraints (
15
) gives the most general parametric model able to reproduce all possible patterns of discrete data, expressed under the form of the multivariate moments (
11
).
In the context of statistical modeling, besides being able to reproduce the data patterns, one also aims to
distinguish patterns that are significant from those that are just noise (for example, in order to extract structural information about the modeled system, or for the model to better generalize).
In other words, one aims to reduce the set of parameters of the model to only those that captures relevant patterns of the data.
Identifying these relevant parameters can be addressed by comparing (sub-)models of the complete model, in which different sets of parameters have been exactly fixed to zero (before fitting), and selecting the best among them based on model selection criteria, as discussed in the introduction.
In this context, the sub-models of (
12
), for which some of the complex parameters
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
’s have been set exactly to zero, form a complete family of maximum entropy models constrained on reproducing arbitrary subsets of moments of the spin variables from the data.
Each of these sub-models is identified by the choice of a subset
\mathcal{M}=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{K}\}\subseteq[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]^{*}
of operators for which the parameters
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
are not set to zero and which identify the fitted moments. We use this notation to define the family of
q
-state spin models.

Definition 5
.

(
q
-state spin models) Complete family of maximum entropy models constrained on reproducing arbitrary sets of spin moments.

Consider a system of
n

q
-state spin variables (
10
)
\boldsymbol{s}=(s_{1},\,\cdots,\,s_{n})
.
A
q
-state spin model is a parametric probability distribution of the form:

p(\boldsymbol{s}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g},\mathcal{M})=\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})}\,\exp\left({\;\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}}g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})}\right)
,

(19)

with
\qquad\forall\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}\,,\qquad-\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}

and

g_{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}=g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}
,

(20)

where the set
\mathcal{M}\subseteq[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]^{*}
identifies the empirical moments
\langle\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})\rangle
that the model is constrained to reproduce when fitting the parameters
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
’s.
The spin operators
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})
are given by Eq. (
13
) and can be of arbitrary order.
For the probabilities
p(\boldsymbol{s}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g},\,\mathcal{M})
to be real, complex operators must be included by conjugate pairs in
\mathcal{M}

and the corresponding parameters must satisfy Eq. (
20
).
The normalization factor
Z_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})
is the partition function of the model:

\displaystyle Z_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})=\displaystyle\sum_{\boldsymbol{s}}\;\exp\left(\,\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}}g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})\right)\,,

(21)

where
\boldsymbol{g}
is a vector containing all the parameters
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
’s and the first sum is over the
q^{n}
states of
\boldsymbol{s}
. The spin models in Eq. (
19
) can also be expressed in terms of the discrete variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, by simply replacing the dependencies in
\boldsymbol{s}
by dependencies in
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
.

For binary systems (
q=2
), all the operators take real (binary) values
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})\in\{\pm 1\}
, the parameters
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
are real,
and one recovers the family of high-order Ising models defined in Eq. (
1
)
34
;
9
.

Note that, if we relax the constraint that operators must be included in the model by conjugate pairs with conjugate parameters satisfying (
20
), then the energy function in the exponential can take complex values. This implies complex state probabilities and a complex partition function. Although this may not be physical for classical systems, it could still be worthy of study.
For instance, extending the partition function to the complex plane has been commonly used to study phase transitions in finite size classical many-body systems
55
, including on complex networks
29
;
30
.

In the spin model (
19
), upon fitting the complex parameter
g^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
, the model reproduces both the real and the imaginary parts of the corresponding moment
\langle\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\rangle
.
More generally, one could choose to set to zero any of the
(q^{n}-1)
real parameters
a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
and
b_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
in Eq. (
16
).
This is different from the previous setting, because it allows the modeler to choose to reproduce only the real or the imaginary part of a given moment (as opposed to both of them simultaneously).
This approach then corresponds to defining the following family of maximum entropy models, constrained to reproduce the real and/or the imaginary part of a selected set of moments of the spin variables:

Definition 6
.

Complete family of maximum entropy models for discrete data.

Consider a system of
n

q
-state discrete variables (
9
)
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\,\cdots,\,\alpha_{n})
. The following exponential family of probability distributions defines a complete family of parametric models for discrete data:

p(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g},\mathcal{M})=\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})}\,\exp[\displaystyle\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}\cup\mathcal{M}_{Re}}a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\cos\left({\displaystyle\frac{2\pi}{q}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right)+\displaystyle\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{Im}}b_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\sin\left({\displaystyle\frac{2\pi}{q}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right)\Bigg]

(22)

where
a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
and
b_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
are real parameters.
\mathcal{M}_{0}
identifies an arbitrary set of real moments (for
\boldsymbol{\mu}=-\boldsymbol{\mu}
),
\langle\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\rangle=\langle\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\rangle
, that the model is constrained to reproduce, and
\mathcal{M}_{Re}
and
\mathcal{M}_{Im}
identify two arbitrary sets of complex moments (for
\boldsymbol{\mu}\neq-\boldsymbol{\mu}
) for which the model is constrained to reproduce respectively the real part
\langle\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\rangle
and the imaginary part
\langle\psi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\rangle
.
These sets are chosen such that:

\begin{cases}\;\mathcal{M}_{0}&\subseteq\{\,\boldsymbol{\mu}\in[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]^{*}\mid\boldsymbol{\mu}=-\boldsymbol{\mu}\,\}\,,\\[1.0pt]
\;\mathcal{M}_{Re}&\subseteq\{\,\boldsymbol{\mu}\in[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]^{*}\mid\boldsymbol{\mu}<-\boldsymbol{\mu}\,\}\,,\\[1.0pt]
\;\mathcal{M}_{Im}&\subseteq\{\,\boldsymbol{\mu}\in[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]^{*}\mid\boldsymbol{\mu}<-\boldsymbol{\mu}\,\}\,,\end{cases}

(23)

where the ordering between vectors
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
is based on the ordering of the integers represented by
\boldsymbol{\mu}
in base
q
.
The condition that
\boldsymbol{\mu}<-\boldsymbol{\mu}
is used so that
\mathcal{M}_{Re}
and
\mathcal{M}_{Im}
only contains one vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}
for each conjugate pair (
\forall\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{Re}\,,\;-\boldsymbol{\mu}\notin\mathcal{M}_{Re}
).

Special cases:

–

For odd values of
q
,
\mathcal{M}_{0}=\emptyset
.

–

The sets
\mathcal{M}_{Re}
and
\mathcal{M}_{Im}
can overlap. When these sets are equal, the model parametric distribution in Eq. (
22
) is identical to the one in Eq. (
19
) with
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{0}\cup\mathcal{M}_{Re}\cup(-\mathcal{M}_{Re})
, and the parameters
a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
and
b_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
are related to the
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
’s by
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=(a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}-ib_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})/2=g_{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}
for all
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{Re}
and
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
for all
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
.

–

For the binary case (
q=2
),
\mathcal{M}_{Re}=\mathcal{M}_{Im}=\emptyset
and, for all
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
,
\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=(-1)^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}}
. We recover the family of binary spin models defined in Eq. (
1
).

The families of models in Eq. (
19
) and Eq. (
22
) both generalize the family of binary spin models in Eq. (
1
) to values of
q\geq 2
.

2.1.3
Interactions of arbitrary order between the variables

In the maximum entropy models (
19
) and (
22
), the term in the exponential, which we denote
-H(\boldsymbol{s})
, can be interpreted as (minus) the energy of the spin system, resulting from interactions between the spins.
More precisely, the
q
-state spin models in Eq. (
19
) have the same form as the binary models in Eq. (
1
), but generalized to
q\geq 2
.
By analogy to the binary case, one can interpret the discrete vectors
\boldsymbol{\mu}
as encoding interactions (of arbitrary order) between the spins
s_{j}
for which
\mu_{j}\neq 0
,
with an interaction energy
H^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})=-g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})=-g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\prod_{j=1}^{n}s_{j}^{\mu_{j}}
that depends only on the state of these variables.
However, unlike the binary case, the contribution of each spin to the interaction energy is now weighted differently depending on the value of the
\mu_{j}
’s, and therefore there can be different types of interactions involving the same set of spins.

For example, for a binary system (
1
) with
n=4
spins,
the vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(1,1,0,1)
identifies the spin operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})=s_{1}s_{2}s_{4}
and is interpreted as encoding a 3-body interaction between the spins
s_{1}
,
s_{2}
, and
s_{4}
.
In the ternary case
q=3
, the vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(2,1,0,1)
identifies the spin operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})=s_{1}^{2}s_{2}s_{4}
and can similarly be interpreted as encoding a 3-body interaction between the same spins, but the spin
s_{1}
is now weighted twice in the interaction.
The vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(1,1,0,1)
also encodes a 3-body interaction between the same spins, but
it has a different interaction energy.
Indeed, different values of
\mu_{j}
weight the colors
\alpha_{j}
differently in the operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\exp\big((2i\pi/q)\sum_{j=1}^n \mu_j\,\alpha_j\big)
, thus resulting in different contributions to the energy landscape.
In Sec.
2.4
, we discuss the interpretation of different types of interaction involving the same spins.

Similarly, in Eq. (
22
), each vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}
can be interpreted as encoding an interaction, whose energy
H^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=-a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})-b_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\psi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=-a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\cos((2\pi/q)\,\vecmu\cdot\state)-b_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\sin((2\pi/q)\,\vecmu\cdot\state)
only depends on the values taken by the spins
s_{j}
for which
\mu_{j}\neq 0
.
More generally:

Definition 7
.

Arbitrary-order interactions.
For a system of
n
random variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\,\cdots,\,\alpha_{n})
,
one can identify an interaction of arbitrary order by:

–

an
n
-dimensional vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
, whose non-zero entries identify which variables interact,

–

and an interaction energy
H^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
that defines how the variables interact and whose functional form depends only on the variables
\alpha_{j}
for which
\mu_{j}\neq 0
.
For example, for the models discussed in this paper,
H^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
depends on the scalar product
\boldsymbol{\mu}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\sum_{j}\mu_{j}\,\alpha_{j}
.

In the cases considered here, the interaction energies,
H^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
or
H^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})+b_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\psi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
, are non-linear functions of a linear combination
\boldsymbol{\mu}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\sum_{j}\mu_{j}\,\alpha_{j}
of the colors of the spins involved in the interaction encoded by
\boldsymbol{\mu}
.

Definition 8
.

Order of an interaction.

The order of an interaction corresponds to the number of variables involved in the interaction. For an interaction identified by a vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}
, it is given by the numbers of non-zero entries in
\boldsymbol{\mu}
, which is
|\boldsymbol{\mu}|=\sum_{j=1}^{n}(1-\delta_{0,\mu_{j}})
.
Note that this is not the same as the order of the operator identified by
\boldsymbol{\mu}
(or of the corresponding moment), which is
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mu_{i}
.

2.2
Comparison with other models for discrete systems

2.2.1
Examples of pairwise discrete models

Example 1
.

The planar Potts model
(
41
)
(also known as vector Potts model or clock model).
In this model, spins take
q
discrete values uniformly distributed around the unit circle, as in Eq. (
10
), and are placed on a lattice or, more generally, on the nodes of a network.
Spins connected by a link in the network interact and the Hamiltonian of the system is given by:

\displaystyle H(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=-\displaystyle\sum_{\langle i,j\rangle}J_{ij}\,\cos\left(\displaystyle\frac{2\pi}{q}(\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})\right)\,,

(24)

where
\langle i,j\rangle
denotes the pairs of nodes
i,j
that are connected by a link in the network.
In the original model, there is only one parameter,
J_{ij}=J
for all pairs
\langle i,j\rangle
. Here, we adopted a more general notation, where the coupling parameters
J_{ij}
can take different values.

The models we introduced in (
19
) and (
22
) can be seen as high-order generalizations of the planar Potts model.
Indeed, it is straightforward to check that the Hamiltonian in Eq. (
24
) corresponds to a model of the form of Eq. (
22
), in which
b_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=0
for all
\boldsymbol{\mu}
and all the interactions are pairwise of the type:
for each pair of nodes
(i,j)
connected by a link, there is one interaction vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}
such that
7
7

7

Which of the two nodes is ‘
i
‘ or ‘
j
‘ doesn’t matter. One could choose to take the conjugate operators instead in
\mathcal{M}_{0}\cup\mathcal{M}_{Re}
, i.e. the operator
\boldsymbol{\mu}
with
\mu_{i}=(q-1)
and
\mu_{j}=1
. This would give the exact same model.

\displaystyle\mu_{i}=1\,,\qquad\qquad\mu_{j}=(q-1)\,,\qquad{\rm and}\qquad\mu_{k}=0\;\qquad\forall k\neq i,j\,.

(25)

The corresponding operator
\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
in (
22
) is then given by:

\displaystyle\cos\left(\frac{2\pi}{q}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}\right)=\cos\left(\frac{2\pi}{q}\left(\alpha_{i}+(q-1)\alpha_{j}\right)\right)=\cos\left(\frac{2\pi}{q}(\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})\right)\,.

(26)

Note that the contribution of each pairwise interaction in Eq. (
24
) can also be written as a scalar product between the vectors representing the two spins in the complex plane:

\displaystyle H(\boldsymbol{s})=-\displaystyle\sum_{\langle i,j\rangle}J_{ij}\;\vec{s}_{i}\cdot\vec{s}_{j}\,,

(27)

where
\vec{s}_{j}=(\cos(\frac{2\pi\alpha_j}{q}),\sin(\frac{2\pi\alpha_j}{q}))
.
Similarly, a maximum entropy model constrained to reproduce the imaginary parts of the same moments would take the form:

\displaystyle H(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=-\sum_{\langle i,j\rangle}J_{ij}\,\sin\left(\displaystyle\frac{2\pi}{q}(\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j})\right)=-\Bigg(\displaystyle\sum_{\langle i,j\rangle}J_{ij}\;\vec{s}_{i}\times\vec{s}_{j}\Bigg)\cdot\vec{e}_{z}\,.

(28)

where
\times
denotes the vector product between the spin vectors
\vec{s}_{j}=(\cos(\frac{2\pi\alpha_j}{q}),\sin(\frac{2\pi\alpha_j}{q}),0)
representing the planar spins in a 3-dimensional space, and
\vec{e}_{z}=(0,0,1)
. This produces a model with antisymmetric interactions in the values taken by the pair
\alpha_{i}
and
\alpha_{j}
.

Although the planar Potts model wasn’t introduced in the context of statistical inference, one may wonder if it could be used to model the pairwise structure of discrete data.
In Eq. (
24
), each pairwise interaction is parameterized by 1 parameter. If we were to also fit the corresponding imaginary part, we would have 2 parameters.
However, to fully parameterize all the pairwise moments based on a given pair of spins, one needs a total of
(q-1)^{2}
real parameters
8
8

8

The number of pairwise operators on a given pair of spins is equal to the number of possible values of the pair
(\mu_{i},\mu_{j})\in\{1,\cdots,(q-1)\}^{2}
(
\mu_{i}
and
\mu_{j}
must be both non-zero for
\boldsymbol{\mu}
to encode an interaction between
\alpha_{i}
and
\alpha_{j}
).
Besides, there are two independent real parameters for each pair of conjugate operators, and thus one can count the number of independent parameters as being equal to the number of operators (see Sec.
2.1.1
).
.
For example, for
q=3
and
n=2
spins, one needs 4 real parameters: 2 to parameterize the conjugate operators
(1,2)
and
(2,1)
, and 2 for the conjugate operators
(1,1)
and
(2,2)
(see Sec.
2.4
for a discussion on the meaning of these different interactions).
These latter are not accounted for in Eq. (
24
), but are needed to fully parameterize statistical patterns of pairs of spins.
In the context of statistical inference, a common parametric model that can account for all first and second order patterns of discrete data is the following.

Example 2
.

Generalized pairwise Potts model for statistical inference.
A generalization of the Potts model commonly used in statistical inference
39
takes the form:

\displaystyle H(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\sum_{<i,j>}J_{ij}(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})+\sum_{i}h_{i}(\alpha_{i})\,,

(29)

where the coupling parameters
J_{ij}
and field parameters
h_{i}
can take different values for each state of the nodes
\alpha_{i}
and
\alpha_{j}
,
i.e.,
J_{ij}(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})
is the energy of the interaction between nodes
i
and
j
when they take the value
(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})
. Note that
J_{ij}(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
is not necessarily equal to
J_{ij}(\alpha^{\prime},\alpha)
.

The form of this model is different from the one in Eq. (
19
) and (
22
) reduced to all first and second order interactions.
Although each of these models is able to fit all the pairwise patterns of discrete data, the model (
29
) has more parameters than the number of pairwise constraints in the system.
In total, there are
q
real parameters for
h_{i}(\alpha_{i})
(one for each value of
\alpha_{i}
) and
q^{2}
parameters for each pairwise interactions (one for each value of
(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})
), while there are only
(q-1)
moments based on a given spin and
(q-1)^{2}
moments on a given pair of spins.
This overparameterization results in the parameters of Eq. (
29
) being defined up to a gauge
17
;
6
;
46
, which is not the case for Eq. (
19
) and (
22
).
The parameters of (
29
) are related to the parameters of (
19
) and (
22
) by:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&h_{j}(\alpha_{j})=\displaystyle\sum_{\mu_{j}=1}^{q-1}g_{\mu_{j}}\exp\left(\frac{2i\pi}{q}\mu_{j}\alpha_{j}\right)\,,\qquad\forall\alpha_{j}\\
&J_{ij}(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})=\displaystyle\sum_{\mu_{i}=1}^{q-1}\sum_{\mu_{j}=1}^{q-1}g_{\mu_{i}\mu_{j}}\exp\left(\frac{2i\pi}{q}(\mu_{i}\alpha_{i}+\mu_{j}\alpha_{j})\right)\,,\qquad\forall(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})\end{cases}

(30)

where
g_{\mu_{j}}
(resp.
g_{\mu_{i}\mu_{j}}
) denotes the parameter
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
for which the
j
-th (resp.
i
-th and
j
-th) element of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is
\mu_{j}
(resp.
\mu_{i}
and
\mu_{j}
) and all the other elements are
0
.
We recall that
g_{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}=g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}=(a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}+ib_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})/2
.
On the left-hand-side, there are
q+q^{2}
parameters,
while on the right-hand-side, there are
(q-1)+(q-1)^{2}
independent real parameters
a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
and
b_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
.

For example, for a system with
q=3
and
n=2
variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
, the one-body terms are parametrized as:

\displaystyle h_{j}(\alpha_{j})

\displaystyle=g_{(10)}z_{3}^{\alpha_{j}}+g_{(20)}z_{3}^{2\alpha_{j}}\,,\qquad{\rm with}\;g_{20}=g_{10}^{*}

\displaystyle=a_{(10)}\cos\left(\frac{2\pi}{3}\alpha_{j}\right)+b_{(10)}\sin\left(\frac{2\pi}{3}\alpha_{j}\right)\,,

where there are three parameters
h_{j}
on the left-hand side and only two real parameters on the right-hand side. The pairwise interaction is parametrized as:

\displaystyle J_{12}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})

\displaystyle=g_{(11)}z_{3}^{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}+g_{(22)}z_{3}^{2\alpha_{1}+2\alpha_{2}}+g_{(12)}z_{3}^{\alpha_{1}+2\alpha_{2}}+g_{(21)}z_{3}^{2\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}\,,\;{\rm with}\;g_{22}=g_{11}^{*}\,,\;g_{21}=g_{12}^{*}

\displaystyle=a_{(11)}\cos\Big(\frac{2\pi}{3}(\alpha_1+\alpha_2)\Big)+b_{(11)}\sin\Big(\frac{2\pi}{3}(\alpha_1+\alpha_2)\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad+a_{(12)}\cos\Big(\frac{2\pi}{3}(\alpha_1-\alpha_2)\Big)+b_{(12)}\sin\Big(\frac{2\pi}{3}(\alpha_1-\alpha_2)\Big)\,,

where there are nine parameters
J_{ij}
on the left-hand side and only four real parameters on the right-hand side

Example 3
.

Continuous limit and XY model.

In the limit
q\to\infty
, the planar Potts model (
24
) becomes the XY model (after adding one-body contributions):

\displaystyle H(\boldsymbol{s})=-\displaystyle\sum_{\langle i,j\rangle}J_{ij}\,\cos\left(\theta_{i}-\theta_{j}\right)-\displaystyle\sum_{j}h_{j}\,\cos\left(\theta_{j}\right)\,,

(31)

where the variable
\theta_{j}\in[0,\,2\pi]
corresponds to the angle that the planar spin
\vec{s}_{j}
forms with the
x
-axis.
Before taking the limit
q\to\infty
,
\theta_{j}
is related to the variable
\alpha_{j}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
by
\theta_{j}=\frac{2\pi}{q}\alpha_{j}
.

Similarly, defining the angle
\theta_{j}=\frac{2\pi}{q}\alpha_{j}
for each value of
\alpha_{j}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
and taking the limit
q\to\infty
, the model in Eq. (
22
) can be seen as a generalization of the XY model to higher-order interactions, where now the vectors
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
.
Note that after taking
q\to\infty
,
\theta_{j}
takes real values in
[0,2\pi]
, but
\alpha_{j}
remains an integer,
\alpha_{j}\in\mathbb{N}
.

2.2.2
Examples of high-order discrete models

Example 4
.

High-order generalization of the standard Potts model.

Ref.
20
introduced a high-order generalization of the standard Potts
54

model of the form:

\displaystyle H(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,|\,E)=-\displaystyle\sum_{e\in E}J_{e}\,\delta_{e}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\,,\qquad{\rm where}\qquad\delta_{e}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\begin{cases}\;1&{\rm if}\;\;\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{j}\;\;{\rm for\;all}\;\;i,j\in e\\
\;0&{\rm otherwise}\end{cases}

(32)

where is
E
set of hyperedges
e
between the variables.
\delta_{e}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=1
only if all the spins connected by
e
have the same value.
When all hyperedges are pairwise, one recovers the standard Potts model.

Although this model wasn’t introduced in the context of statistical inference, one may wonder if it could be used for this purpose. Similarly to our own case, each operator
\delta_{e}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
defines a (high-order) interaction between the spins connected by the hyperedge
e
.
However, the interaction energy
-J_{e}\delta_{e}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
can only take two values (instead of
q
in our case)
and is unable to distinguish states for which the variables
\alpha_{i}
connected by
e

take non-equal values.
In particular,
it doesn’t allow to weigh differently the contributions of different spins to the same interaction.
This is an issue for statistical inference, because one aims to model how similar or different the values taken by the random variables are. Besides, each hyperedge
e
is only associated to one interaction (compared to
(q-1)^{|e|}
in our case).
This means that there are at most
(2^{n}-1)
different
\delta_{e}
-functions, which is not sufficient to form a basis of
\mathbb{R}[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]
for
q>2
,
and therefore a full model of the form of Eq. (
32
) wouldn’t be able to capture all patterns of discrete data for
q>2
.

For
q=2
, this model could a priori model all possible patterns of binary data, if the set of
\delta_{e}
-functions forms a basis of
\mathbb{R}[(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{n}]
(this remains to be checked) and after adding terms of order one (self-interactions). If this were the case, this basis would differ from the Ising spin basis studied here, decomposing multi-variate information in a different manner. Note that models of the form (
32
) with only pairwise interactions
e=(ij)
, can be mapped to pairwise Ising models using
\delta_{ij}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=(1+\phi^{(ij)}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}))/2
, where
(ij)
denotes the vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}
in which
\mu_{i}=\mu_{j}=1
and all the other elements are zero.

Example 5
.

Other high-order models for discrete systems.
Ref.
51
recently introduced another model for the statistical inference of three-state systems inspired by the Blume-Capel model
10
;
12
from condensed matter.
This model corresponds to a maximum entropy model constrained on reproducing multivariate moments of the discrete variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, instead of moments of the spin variables
\boldsymbol{s}
. So far, this model only includes interactions up to pairwise, but can easily be generalized by constraining the model on higher-order moments as well.
Another approach in the context of statistical modeling is the one by Ref.
23
, which defines models constrained to reproduce high-order cumulants from multivariate data.

2.3
Properties of
q
-state spin models and discrete Fourier analysis

In this section, we show that the set of
q^{n}
complex spin operators (
13
) forms an orthonormal basis of the vector space of complex functions over
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
, and thus that the model (
12
) corresponds to a particular choice of basis decomposition of the multivariate log-probability, more specifically to a (spatial) Fourier decomposition.
Similarly, Eq. (
16
) corresponds to a decomposition of the log-probability over the (spatial) trigonometric Fourier basis.

The properties discussed below extend results known in the binary case
9
to the more general discrete case
q\geq 2
. They are direct consequences of the operators being Fourier basis functions.
The advantage of mapping Eq. (
12
) and (
16
) to Fourier series is that it enables the use of Fourier analysis tools for statistical inference
(e.g., efficient algorithms to compute the Fourier coefficients can be used to compute the model parameters
27
;
11
;
36
;
40
).

2.3.1
Spin models as Fourier decompositions of the log-probability

Property 1
.

Group
\Omega
of complex spin operators.
The set
\Omega
of all the
q^{n}
spin monomials (
13
) forms a finite multiplicative group with identity element
\phi^{\boldsymbol{0}}(\boldsymbol{s})=1
. It is the cyclic group of order
q
generated by the spin variables
s_{1},s_{2},\cdots,s_{n}
:

\displaystyle\Omega\doteq\{\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})\mid\boldsymbol{\mu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}\}\,=\;\langle s_{1},s_{2},\cdots,s_{n}\rangle_{gen}\,.

(33)

where, for all
j
, the variable
s_{j}
takes values in the
q
-th roots of unity (
10
).

Proof.

From Definition
4
, it is straightforward that, for all
(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu})\in[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]^{2}
,

\displaystyle\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{s})=\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}+\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{s})\in\Omega\,,

(34)

is also a monomial that belongs to
\Omega
, where the sum
\boldsymbol{\mu}+\boldsymbol{\nu}
is performed on
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
(i.e. with modulo
q
). The inverse of an operator is
[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})]^{-1}=\phi^{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})=[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})]^{*}
. In the binary case,
each operator is its own inverse.
Each element of
\Omega
is at most of order
q
, as:

\displaystyle[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})]^{q}=\exp\left({\displaystyle 2i\pi\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right)=1\,,\qquad\qquad\forall\;\boldsymbol{\mu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}\,.

(35)

∎

Property 2
.

Sum of spin operator values.
The monomials
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})\in\Omega
verify that:

\displaystyle\sum_{\boldsymbol{s}}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=q^{n}\,\delta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{0}}\,,\qquad\qquad\forall\;\boldsymbol{\mu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}\,,

(36)

where the two sums are respectively over all possible
q^{n}
states of
\boldsymbol{s}
and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
,
and where the delta function
\delta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{0}}
is equal to
1
if
\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{0}
and to
0
otherwise.

Replacing the expression of
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
in Eq. (
36
), and identifying the real and the imaginary parts on the two sides of the equality, one obtains a similar property for
\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
and
\psi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
:

\displaystyle\forall\boldsymbol{\mu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}\,,\;\;\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}}\cos\left(\frac{2\pi}{q}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}\right)=q^{n}\,\delta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{0}}\qquad{\rm and}\;\;\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}}\sin\left(\frac{2\pi}{q}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}\right)=0\,.

(37)

Equation (
36
) is a direct consequence of the fact that the variables
s_{j}
are roots of unity, which satisfy
9
9

9

For all
\mu_{j}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})
,

\sum_{s_{j}}s_{j}^{\mu_{j}}=\sum_{\alpha_{j}=0}^{q-1}e^{\frac{2i\pi}{q}\alpha_{j}\,\mu_{j}}=\frac{1-\Big(e^{\frac{2i\pi}{q}\mu_{j}}\Big)^{q}}{1-e^{\frac{2i\pi}{q}\mu_{j}}}=0\,.

\sum_{s_{j}}s_{j}^{\mu}=0
for all strictly positive integer
\mu
.
It reflects that,
similarly to
s_{j}
,
the values taken by
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})
as one varies
\boldsymbol{s}
are evenly distributed over
the
q
-th roots of unity
10
10

10

Or evenly distributed over the
q^{\prime}
-th roots of unity, if
q
is non-prime and
q^{\prime}<q
is the smallest integer satisfying
q^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{0}
(see Sec.
2.4.3
).
.

Property 3
.

Orthogonality of complex spin operators.
We denote by
\mathbb{C}[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]
the vector space of functions from
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
to
\mathbb{C}
endowed with the following inner product:

for two complex functions
f
and
h
in
\mathbb{C}[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]
,

\displaystyle\langle\,f\,|\,h\,\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q^{n}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}}[f(\boldsymbol{\alpha})]^{*}\,h(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\,,

(38)

where
[f(\boldsymbol{\alpha})]^{*}
is the complex conjugate of
f(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
.
Applying this scalar product to any pair of operators in
\Omega=\{\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\mid\boldsymbol{\mu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}\}
, one obtains that they are orthonormal (see App.
A.2
):

\displaystyle\;\hskip-79.6678pt{\rm for}\;{\rm all}\;(\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\phi^{\boldsymbol{\nu}})\in\Omega^{2}\,,\qquad\qquad\langle\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,|\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\,\rangle

\displaystyle=\delta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu}}\,.

(39)

Thus, the set
\Omega
of all spin operators form an orthonormal set of vectors of
\mathbb{C}[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]
.

Besides, there are exactly
q^{n}
such independent vectors, which is also the dimension of the vector space
\mathbb{C}[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]
.
The set
\Omega
of monomials thus forms a basis of
\mathbb{C}[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]
:

Theorem 1
.

Complex spin model (
12
) as Fourier decomposition of the log-probability.

The set
\Omega=\{\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\mid\boldsymbol{\mu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}\}
of all spin operators (including
\phi^{\boldsymbol{0}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=1
) forms an orthonormal basis of the vector space
\mathbb{C}[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]
of complex functions over
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
.
As a consequence, any complex function of the variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
can be decomposed over this basis. In particular, the complete spin model in Eq. (
12
) corresponds to an expansion of the log-probability over this basis:   for all
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
,

\displaystyle\log P(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\,=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}}\,g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\;=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}}\,g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\exp\left({\displaystyle\frac{2i\pi}{q}\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right)\,,

(40)

where the
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
’s are
q^{n}
complex coefficients. The coefficient
g_{\boldsymbol{0}}
is fixed
by the normalization of the probability distribution
P(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
(recalling that
\phi^{\boldsymbol{0}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=1
):

\displaystyle g_{\boldsymbol{0}}=-\log\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}}e^{\,\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\neq\boldsymbol{0}}g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})}=-\log Z(\boldsymbol{g})\,,

(41)

where we identified the partition function
Z(\boldsymbol{g})
.
One can easily recognize that Eq. (
40
) is a (spatial) discrete Fourier decomposition of the log-probability
11
11

11

This is also valid for the (high-order) Ising model
q=2
, for which
s_{i}=\exp(\frac{2i\pi\alpha_i}{q})=(-1)^{\alpha_{i}}
.
, and that the set
\Omega
of monomials is the discrete Fourier basis of complex functions on
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
.

The high-order Ising model (
1
) is a particular case of (
40
) for
q=2
, and can thus also be understood as a Fourier decomposition of the log-probability.

Similarly, one can show that the complete model in Eq. (
16
) corresponds to an expansion of the log-probability over the trigonometric Fourier basis of
\mathbb{R}[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]
.

Theorem 2
.

Real spin model (
16
) as trigonometric Fourier decomposition of
\log P(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
.
The set
12
12

12

In the definition of
\Omega_{R}
, the ordering between vectors
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
is defined by the ordering of the integers represented by
\boldsymbol{\mu}
in base
q
. The ordering is used to ensure that
\Omega_{R}
doesn’t contain identical
(
\varphi^{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
for
-\boldsymbol{\mu}\neq\boldsymbol{\mu}
) or parallel elements
(
\psi^{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=-\psi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
for
-\boldsymbol{\mu}\neq\boldsymbol{\mu}
).

\Omega_{R}
of real spin operators (
18
)

\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\cos(\frac{2 \pi}{q}\state\cdot\vecmu)
and
\psi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\sin(\frac{2 \pi}{q}\state\cdot\vecmu)
,

\displaystyle\Omega_{R}

\displaystyle=\{\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\mid\boldsymbol{\mu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}\;{\rm and}\;\boldsymbol{\mu}\leq-\boldsymbol{\mu}\}\cup\{\psi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\mid\boldsymbol{\mu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}\;{\rm and}\;\boldsymbol{\mu}<-\boldsymbol{\mu}\}\,,

(42)

forms an orthogonal basis of the vector space
\mathbb{R}[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]
of real functions over
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
(see proof in App.
A.3
),
which can be recognized as the trigonometric discrete Fourier basis.
The complete model (
16
) corresponds to an expansion of the log-probability over this basis, where the coefficient of the operator
\varphi^{\boldsymbol{0}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=1
is fixed by normalization to
a_{\boldsymbol{0}}=-\log Z(\boldsymbol{g})
.

More generally, the spin models (
19
) and (
22
) correspond to an expansion of the log-probability over a subset of Fourier basis elements, i.e. in which some harmonics have been (artificially) set exactly to zero.

Finally, exchanging the roles of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
in Eq. (
39
) leads to another property of the complex spin operators,
called completeness of
\Omega
in the binary case
9
;
34
:

Property 4
.

Completeness property of
\Omega
.
For all states
(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime})\in[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]^{2}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{q^{n}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\,\phi^{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime})=\delta_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}}\,.

(43)

This relation can also be written under the form of the scalar product:

\displaystyle\langle\phi_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}}\mid\phi_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\rangle=\frac{1}{q^{n}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}}\,\phi_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{\mu})\,[\phi_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\mu})]^{*}=\delta_{\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}}\,,

(44)

where, for each state
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
, we introduce the function
\phi_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{\mu})\doteq\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\exp\left({\frac{2i\pi}{q}\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right)
for all
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
.

2.3.2
Inversion formulas for the model parameters

The complete models in Eq. (
12
) and (
16
) correspond respectively to the complex and real Fourier series of
\log P(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
, and thus the Fourier coefficients
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
,
a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
, and
b_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
can be obtained by Fourier transform of
\log P(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
.
Equations (
46
) and (
47
) below generalize to
q\geq 2
the inversion formulas known for binary spin models
34
;
9
(for
q=2
,
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
and
b_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=0
).

Property 5
.

The coefficients
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}

in Eq. (
12
) can be obtained by Fourier Transform of the log-probability function
\log P(\boldsymbol{\alpha})

(see App.
A.4
):   for all
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]^{*}
,

\displaystyle g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=\mathcal{F}\,[\log P](\boldsymbol{\mu})\,

\displaystyle=\,\frac{1}{q^{n}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}}\,\log P(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\,\exp\left({\displaystyle-\frac{2i\pi}{q}\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right)\,,

(45)

which can also be written under the form of the inner product (
38
):

\displaystyle g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}

\displaystyle=\,\frac{1}{q^{n}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}}\,\log P(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\,\left[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right]^{*}=\left\langle\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,|\,\log P\right\rangle\,.

(46)

Property 6
.

The coefficients
a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
and
b_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
in Eq. (
16
) can be obtained by real Fourier Transform of the log-probability function
\log P(\boldsymbol{\alpha})

(see App.
A.4
):   for all
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]^{*}
,

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\;\;\;{\rm Re}[g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}]=\displaystyle\frac{1}{q^{n}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}}\log P(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\cos\left(\frac{2\pi}{q}\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}\right)=\left\langle\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,|\,\log P\right\rangle=\begin{cases}\,a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}&{\rm for\;}\boldsymbol{\mu}=-\boldsymbol{\mu}\\[2.0pt]
\,a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}/2&{\rm else}\end{cases}\\[15.0pt]
-{\rm Im}[g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}]=\displaystyle\frac{1}{q^{n}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}}\log P(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\sin\left(\frac{2\pi}{q}\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}\right)=\left\langle\psi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,|\,\log P\right\rangle=\begin{cases}\,0&{\rm for\;}\boldsymbol{\mu}=-\boldsymbol{\mu}\\[2.0pt]
\,b_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}/2&{\rm else}\end{cases}\end{cases}

(47)

Reciprocally, choosing a set
\mathcal{M}
of interactions and their coefficients
\boldsymbol{g}=(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\mathbb{C}\mid\forall\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M})
, and defining the discrete function
G(\boldsymbol{\mu})=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathcal{M}}g_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu}}
for all
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
, the probability distribution of the spin model (
12
) can be obtained by inverse Fourier transform:

\displaystyle\log P(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\mid\mathcal{M},\,\boldsymbol{g})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{-1}[G](\boldsymbol{\alpha})\,=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}}\,G(\boldsymbol{\mu})\,\exp\left({\displaystyle\frac{2i\pi}{q}\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right)=q^{n}\,\langle G^{*}\mid\phi_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\rangle\,.

(48)

where we introduced the functions
\phi_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{\mu})=\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
for all
\boldsymbol{\mu}
and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
(see App.
A.4
).

For systems with up to about
25
spins, this approach can be used to perform direct sampling from any chosen high-order spin model, or to compute exactly the coefficient
g^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
from the empirical probability distribution. For instance, in the binary case, Ref.
1

uses the Walsh–Hadamard transform to perform exact sampling from high-order spin models with
n=20
spins in order to create benchmark data. Ref.
27
;
11
;
36
;
40
also use the Walsh–Hadamard transform to compute efficiently the parameters of specific pairwise and high-order Ising models.

Using
q
-state spin models for data corresponds to decomposing the model log-probability over a specific choice of basis functions (the Fourier basis functions).
One could choose to use other forms of basis functions, each of them corresponding to a different way of decomposing the information of the multivariate system into basis patterns
2
.

2.4
Interpretation of
q
-state spin interactions

Figure 3:
Example for
q=3
.

One-spin system
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1})
modeled by a single interaction
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(1)
, corresponding to the operator
\phi^{1}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\exp\big({\frac{2i\pi}{3}\alpha_1}\big)
with parameter
g_{1}
(and its c.c.).

a) Probability distribution of the model
for different directions and strengths of the parameter
\vec{g}_{1}^{*}=r_{1}\vec{u}_{1}^{*}
. Each column correspond to a fix direction of
\vec{g}_{1}^{*}
, indicated by the vector
\vec{u}_{1}^{*}
in the complex plane representation (see first row).
The energy of a state
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
is given by the scalar product (
51
) between
\vec{g}_{1}^{*}
(direction
\vec{u}_{1}^{*}
indicated by red arrow) and
\vec{\phi}^{1}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
(black arrows) in the complex plane.
The dots indicate the projection of the vector
\vec{u}_{1}^{*}
on each possible direction of
\vec{\phi}^{1}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
.
The state
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
for which
\vec{\phi}^{1}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
is best aligned with
\vec{g}_{1}^{*}
has the highest probability, and states become less likely as
\vec{\phi}^{1}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
points further away from
\vec{g}_{1}^{*}
. In the left column,
\vec{g}_{1}^{*}
points halfway between the values of
\phi^{1}(\alpha_{1})
for
\alpha_{1}=1
and
\alpha_{1}=-1
,
and thus these two states have equal probability (recall that
\alpha=-1=2
in
\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}
).
In the right column,
\vec{g}_{1}^{*}
is exactly aligned with
\alpha_{1}=1
, which then has the highest probability, the two other states having equal probability.
The probability distributions are shown for two values of the parameter strength,
r_{1}=0.25
and
r_{1}=1
.

b) Entropy of the model
for varying values of the parameters
a_{1}=2\,{\rm Re}[g^{*}_{1}]=2\,r_{1}\cos\big(\theta_{u_1^*}\big)
and
b_{1}=2\,{\rm Im}[g^{*}_{1}]=2\,r_{1}\sin\big(\theta_{u^*_1}\big)
, where
\theta_{u^{*}_{1}}
is the angle formed by
\vec{u}^{*}_{1}
along the unit circle.
At fixed value of the strength
r_{1}
, the entropy is minimal when the direction of
\vec{u}_{1}^{*}
coincides with one of the three possible directions of
\vec{\phi}^{1}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
