(roots of unity) and maximal when it points exactly between two consecutive directions of
\vec{\phi}^{\,1}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
.

In this section, we give some insight into the way
q
-state spin models encode patterns of data. We discuss how spin operators shape the energy landscape of models, and what distinguish different interactions based on the same set of variables (e.g., for
q=5
, there are two types of one-body interaction – see Fig.
4
).

In Eq. (
19
), the energy of the spin system (Hamiltonian) for a given model
\mathcal{M}\subseteq(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
with
K=|\mathcal{M}|
operators can be represented as a scalar product in
\mathbb{C}^{K}
:

\displaystyle H(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,|\,\mathcal{M},\,\boldsymbol{g})=-\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}}g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=-\,\boldsymbol{g}^{*}\cdot\boldsymbol{\phi}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\,,

(49)

where
\boldsymbol{g}^{*}
is a
K
-dimensional complex vector containing the values of the
K
parameters
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}
(conjugate of
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
), and
\boldsymbol{\phi}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
is a
K
-dimensional complex vector containing the values of the
K
operators
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
evaluated on the state
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
(both vectors are indexed by
\boldsymbol{\mu}
).
The energy of the system is minimal if these two vectors are aligned and maximal if they are anti-aligned.
However, this is difficult to visualize or interpret in the
K
-dimensional space.
Besides, for the Hamiltonian
H(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,|\,\mathcal{M},\,\boldsymbol{g})
to be real, we must take into account that
g_{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}=g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}
for all
\boldsymbol{\mu}\neq-\boldsymbol{\mu}
.
In the following, we focus on the contribution of pairs of conjugate operators
H^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=-g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})-g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}\,\phi^{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
(for
\boldsymbol{\mu}\neq-\boldsymbol{\mu}
) to the Hamiltonian and give a geometrical representation and interpretation of the effect of a given interaction on the energy of the states, and therefore on the probability distribution.

2.4.1
Geometrical representation of the interaction energy

Definition 9
.

Representation of
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
and
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
as vectors in the complex plane:

Similarly to the planar spin variables, the states of an operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=z_{q}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}}
and the complex parameters
g^{*}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
can be represented by vectors in the complex plane with coordinates:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&\vec{\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\left(\cos\left(\frac{2\pi}{q}\boldsymbol{\mu}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right),\,\sin\left(\frac{2\pi}{q}\boldsymbol{\mu}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}\right)\right)=\left(\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}),\,\psi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right)\\[8.53581pt]
&\vec{g^{*}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}=\left({\rm Re}[g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}],\,{\rm Im}[g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}]\right)=\begin{cases}\left(\frac{a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}{2},\,\frac{b_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}{2}\right)&{\rm for}\;\;-\boldsymbol{\mu}\neq\boldsymbol{\mu}\\[5.69054pt]
\left(a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\,0\right)&{\rm for}\;\;-\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\mu}\end{cases}\end{cases}

(50)

where
a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
and
b_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
are the real parameters from Eq. (
16
).
Let us also denote
\vec{g^{*}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}=r_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\vec{u^{*}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}
, where
r_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=\lVert g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\rVert
is the amplitude of the parameter and
\vec{u}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}
is its direction.

Property 7
.

Interaction energy.
Using this vector representation, the energy
H^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
of an interaction
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is given by:

\displaystyle H^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=-[a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})+b_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\psi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})]=\begin{cases}-2\;\vec{g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}}\cdot\vec{\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})&{\rm for}\;\;-\boldsymbol{\mu}\neq\boldsymbol{\mu}\\
-\;\vec{g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}}\cdot\vec{\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})&{\rm for}\;\;-\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\mu}\end{cases}

(51)

which contains the contributions of both
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
and its complex conjugate (c.c.) if
-\boldsymbol{\mu}\neq\boldsymbol{\mu}
, or just the single real operator if
-\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\mu}
.
The total Hamiltonian is then

\displaystyle H(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}:\boldsymbol{\mu}\leq-\boldsymbol{\mu}}H^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\,,

(52)

where the contribution of
H^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
can be visualized by the projection of
\vec{g}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}
onto the vector
\vec{\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
.
In the following, we analyze
H^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
for values of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
such that
\boldsymbol{\mu}\leq-\boldsymbol{\mu}
(the conjugate term being identical
H^{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}=H^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
).

Let’s discuss on the contribution
H^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
of a given interaction to the energy of the system.
For a given interaction
\boldsymbol{\mu}
, the direction of the operator
\vec{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
in the complex plane rotates as one changes the state of the variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
(see black arrows in Fig.
3
.a and
4
.a).
The contribution of an interaction
\boldsymbol{\mu}
to the energy of the state
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
then depends on how well the direction of the operator
\vec{\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
aligns with the direction of its parameter
\vec{g}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}
, as expressed by Eq. (
51
): the better the alignment, the lower the interaction energy, and therefore the higher the (relative) probability
P(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
of the state (see Fig.
3
.a and
4
.a, and interactive widget in Supplementary Material).
Moreover, increasing the strength
r_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
of the parameter amplifies the differences between the state probabilities, making the pattern associated with the interaction more significant and in particular leading to a decrease of the entropy of the operator (see Fig.
3
and
4
.b).
Overall, in order to minimize its energy, the system will favor states for which the operators
\vec{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
of the model are best aligned with the direction
\vec{u}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}
of their respective (conjugate) parameter,
while weighting the alignments of the operators
based on the relative strengths
r_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
of their parameters.
Below we discuss examples of different types of interactions between the same variables.
Note that this effect is specific to non-binary discrete models, as in the binary case there is only one type of spin operator over each subset of spins.

Figure 4:

Example for
q=5
and two types of 1-body operator.

One-spin system
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1})
modeled by two examples of a single-spin interaction: an interaction
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(1)
corresponding to the operator
\phi^{1}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\exp\big({\frac{2i\pi}{5}\alpha_1}\big)=z_{5}^{\,\,\alpha_{1}}
(and its c.c.) with probability distribution denoted
p_{1}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
; and an interaction
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(2)
corresponding to the operator
\phi^{2}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\exp\big({\frac{2i\pi}{5}\,2\alpha_1}\big)=z_{5}^{\,\,2\alpha_{1}}
(and its c.c.) with probability distribution denoted
p_{2}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
.

a)
Probability distributions for the models
p_{1}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
(in blue) and
p_{2}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
(in orange) for different directions of the parameter
\vec{g}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}=r_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\vec{u}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}
at fixed strength
r_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=0.4
; the respective directions of
\vec{u}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}
are indicated by a red arrow in the circles above the distributions.
We indicated around the unit circle, at the five positions that can be taken by
\vec{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\alpha_{1})
(black arrows located at the fifth roots of unity), the values of the state of
\alpha_{1}
that yields this direction, in blue for
\phi^{1}
and in orange for
\phi^{2}
.
On the left,
\vec{u}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}
is aligned with
\phi^{1}(0)
and
\phi^{2}(0)
, which implies that
\alpha_{1}=0
is the state of highest probability in both
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
; the probability of the other states depends on how far they are from
\vec{u}^{*}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
. On the right
\vec{u}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}
is aligned with
\phi^{1}(1)
and
\phi^{2}(-2)
.
From left to right, the angle of
\vec{u}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}
is progressively increased by
\pi/10
.

b)

Entropy of the model for varying values of the parameters
a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=2\,{\rm Re}[g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}]
and
b_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=2\,{\rm Im}[g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}]
.
The two models
p_{1}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
and
p_{2}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
have exactly the same entropy plot.
This can be appreciated in the probability distributions of panel a), where, for each value of the parameter, the probability of the states are simply shuffled between
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
.

2.4.2
Different types of interactions between the same variables and examples

Each interaction vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
defines a linear combination
\boldsymbol{\mu}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\sum_{i}\mu_{i}\alpha_{i}
of the colors
\alpha_{i}
of the spins involved in the interaction (for which
\mu_{i}\neq 0
).
The direction of the spin monomial
\vec{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
in the complex plane results from this linear combination, as
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=z_{q}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
, where
z_{q}
is the first
q
-th root of unity.
For example in Fig.
4
.a, the direction of the operator
\phi^{1}(\alpha_{1})=z_{5}^{\,\,\alpha_{1}}
rotates by
2\pi/5
each time
\alpha_{1}
is incremented by
1
, while the direction of
\phi^{2}(\alpha_{1})=z_{5}^{\,\,2\alpha_{1}}
rotates by
4\pi/5
.
As a result, the operators
\phi^{1}
and
\phi^{2}
order the states differently around the unit circle.
Because
H^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
increases when the direction of
\vec{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
is further away from
\vec{g}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}
(
51
), this ordering thus constrains the energy landscape
H^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
created by the interaction (e.g., states that are directly on each side of the direction of
\vec{g}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}
have the lowest energy).

In particular, we can observe the impact of this ordering on the shape of the probability distribution associated with a single interaction.
For example in Fig.
4
.a, the shapes of
p_{1}(\alpha_{1})
and
p_{2}(\alpha_{1})
reflect the respective ordering of the states of
\alpha_{1}
by
\phi^{1}
and
\phi^{2}
around the unit circle.
For each model,
choosing the states with highest probability (by aligning
\vec{g}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
with the direction of the operator
\vec{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\alpha_{1})
for these states) automatically imposes an order on the probabilities of the other states, given by the ordering of the states around the unit circle.
It is not possible for instance
to recreate the orange probability distribution in the bottom left of Fig.
4
.a with the model
p_{1}(\alpha_{1})
, no matter the value of the parameters. Indeed in
p_{1}(\alpha_{1})
, for
\alpha_{1}=0
to have the largest probability,
\vec{g}^{*}_{1}
must be closely aligned with
\vec{\phi}^{1}(0)
, which then imposes
that
\alpha_{1}=\pm 2
has a smaller probability than
\alpha_{1}=\pm 1
,
due to the state ordering created by
\phi^{1}
.

Property 8
.

Effect of different operators on the energy landscape of a model.

Different operators yield different orderings of the states around the unit circle, imposing different constraints on the energy landscape
H^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
created by an interaction
\boldsymbol{\mu}
.
These different orderings are what distinguishes different types of interactions acting on the same sets of variables.
Examples of such interactions are given in Fig.
4
.a for one-body interactions and in Fig.
5
.b for pairwise interactions.

Another way to think about this is from the point of view of the Fourier decomposition. The differences between the distributions
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
comes from the change in periodicity of the distributions (frequency is doubled from
p_{1}
to
p_{2}
), which reflects the fact that they correspond to different Fourier modes.

We discuss below examples in which certain types of first- or second-order interactions may be more appropriate to model the system.

Example 6
.

First-order interactions when modeling nominal or ordinal categorical data.

For modeling many systems, it is common to include one-body interactions in the model. However, for
q\geq 5
, there are more than one type of first-order interactions, and one may wonder which to include.
We saw that each of these interactions encodes an ordering on the probability of the states of the variable
\alpha_{i}
affected by the interaction
(see e.g.
\phi^{1}
and
\phi^{2}
for
q=5
in Fig
4
.a).
But in general, variables in the studied systems may or may not have a natural ordering.

For ordinal data,
such as answers to questionnaires of the type
\{
“Strongly agree”, “Agree”, “Neutral”, “Disagree”, “Strongly disagree”
\}
, there is a natural order between the values taken by the variables (see example of personality test in Sec.
6.4
).
In such cases, it is natural to reflect this order, as well as possible opposite valence of the variables, in the choice of the integer value
\alpha_{i}
assigned to each category.
It is then possible that certain single-variable patterns in the data are best modeled by a subset of one-body interactions instead of all (see for instance the patterns in Fig.
4
.a).
In the example above, a natural choice reflecting the order and opposite valence between the possible answers
is to assign values of
\alpha_{i}
ranging from
-2
for “Strongly disagree” to
2
for “Strongly agree”, passing by
\alpha_{i}=0
for “Neutral”.
In this case, choosing an informed assignment of the discrete states over other assignments can help with the interpretation of the uncovered interactions.

For nominal data
(e.g., amino-acids in protein sequencing), ordering the categories by mapping them to integer values
\alpha_{i}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
has no specific meaning and the mapping choice is often arbitrary.
In this case, the constraints on single-variable patterns imposed by using a subset of single-spin operators (e.g.,
\phi^{1}
or
\phi^{2}
in Fig.
4
.a) are a priori arbitrary, and one can expect to include all one-body operators in the model, from which any one-body pattern can be created.
In Fig.
5
.a for example, the two operators
\phi^{1}
and
\phi^{2}
for
q=5
are combined to give the largest probability to a specific value of
\alpha_{i}
and equal probability to the other states, a pattern that can’t be obtained with a single operator.

Example 7
.

Encoding symmetric or antisymmetric pairwise patterns.

For
q=3
for instance, pairwise interactions can be encoded by two different types of spin operators (and their c.c.):

\phi^{(11)}(\boldsymbol{s})=s_{1}s_{2}
(with c.c.
\phi^{(22)}(\boldsymbol{s})=s_{1}^{2}s_{2}^{2}
) and
\phi^{(12)}(\boldsymbol{s})=s_{1}^{\,}s_{2}^{2}
(with c.c.
\phi^{(21)}(\boldsymbol{s})=s_{1}^{\,2}s_{2}^{\,}
).
Fig.
5
.b shows how these two operators place the states of a two-spin system around the unit circle: states are divided in three groups with three states each. Observe that the interaction encoded by the operator
\phi^{12}
and a parameter with direction
u^{*}_{12}=0
(along the
x
-axis) gives the largest probability to symmetric states
13
13

13

We recall that the values
\alpha_{i}=2
and
\alpha_{i}=-1
are equal in
\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}
.

(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\{(0,0),(1,1),(-1,-1)\}
, while the interaction encoded by the operator
\phi^{11}
and a parameter direction
u^{*}_{11}=0
gives the largest probability to anti-symmetric states of the type
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\{(0,0),(-1,+1),(+1,-1)\}
(imagine for instance a system in which
\alpha_{i}=0
is a neutral state, while
\alpha_{i}=\pm 1
encode opposite states).

To interpret these observations from a statistical modeling perspective, consider a dataset for two discrete variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\,\alpha_{2})
with
q=3

for which a large fraction of the datapoints are in a symmetric state
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\{(0,0),(1,1),(2,2)\}
.
For all these symmetric states, the interaction
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(1,2)
verifies that
\boldsymbol{\mu}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}=0\,({\rm mod}\,3)
.
This means that the operator
\phi^{12}
takes the value
\phi^{12}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=z_{5}^{\,0}=1
for a large fraction of the datapoints, which lowers the entropy of
\phi^{12}
and increases its significance in comparison to the other pairwise operator
\phi^{11}
.
Alternatively, if the symmetric pattern is particularly unlikely in the data, this would also reduce the entropy of
\phi^{12}
, thus also increasing the significance of this patterns. Patterns can be significant because they are either more predominant or more rare than what can be expected from chance.
Similarly,
\phi^{11}
would be more relevant than
\phi^{12}
in a dataset dominated by the anti-symmetric state mentioned above.
Note that the converse is not necessarily true: a low entropy of
\phi^{12}
doesn’t necessarily imply that the symmetric pattern is dominant in the data, because any of the two other groups of states (orange and blue states in Fig.
5
.b) could also be dominant. However, these patterns can be harder to interpret a priori.

More generally for larger values of
q
, the pairwise interaction
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(1,\,q-1)
groups together, in the direction of
u^{*}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=0
, the states
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\,\alpha_{2})
that satisfy
\boldsymbol{\mu}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}=0
, which are the symmetric states
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}
in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})
.
Similarly, the pairwise interaction
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(1,\,1)
groups, in the direction of
u^{*}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=0
, the states
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\,\alpha_{2})
that satisfy
\boldsymbol{\mu}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}=0
, which are the anti-symmetric states of the form
\alpha_{1}=-\alpha_{2}
in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})
.
These types of interactions are thus a good choice to model significant symmetric or anti-symmetric pairwise patterns. In particular,
the pairwise interactions used in the planar Potts model
(
41
)
(see Example
1
) are of the symmetric form
with
u^{*}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
aligned with the
x
-axis (as
b_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=0
); this model thus favors or disfavors symmetric pairwise patterns depending on the sign of the parameter
J_{ij}
.

As an example, the most prominent voting patterns in the US Supreme Court dataset analyzed in Sec.
6.3
are symmetric votes between pairs of justices, which explains why the best basis uncovered in that case (after artificially increasing
q
) has mostly interactions of the type
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(1,\,q-1)
(and not
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(1,\,1)
as one could naively expect).

Figure 5:

a) Example of a model for
q=5
with two types of one-body interactions.

One-spin system
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1})
modeled by the operators
\phi^{1}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=z_{5}^{\alpha_{1}}
and
\phi^{2}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=z_{5}^{2\alpha_{1}}
(same as in Fig.
4
).
At the top is the probability distribution
p_{1}(\alpha_{1})
of the model with operator
\phi^{1}
(and its c.c.) parameterized by
g_{1}^{*}=r_{1}u_{1}^{*}
with
u_{1}^{*}=z_{5}^{\,2}
(see red arrow in the unit circle).
In the middle is the distribution
p_{2}(\alpha_{1})
with operator
\phi^{2}
(and c.c.) parameterized by
g_{2}^{*}=r_{2}u_{2}^{*}
with
u_{2}^{*}=z_{5}^{\,4}
.
In both cases, the parameter
is aligned with the direction of

\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\alpha_{1}=2)
and has a strength
r_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=0.5
.
At the bottom is the distribution
p(\alpha_{1})
of the model containing both operators (and their c.c.) with the same parameters
g_{1}^{*}
and
g_{2}^{*}
as above, giving
the largest probability to the state
\alpha_{1}=2
and equal probability to the other states.

b) Examples of two types of pairwise interactions for
q=3
.
The table reports the values of
\boldsymbol{\mu}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}
for two pairwise operators:
\phi^{11}(\boldsymbol{s})=s_{1}s_{2}
for
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(1,1)
and
\phi^{12}(\boldsymbol{s})=s_{1}s_{2}^{2}
for
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(1,2)
.
In the two first columns, the values of
\alpha_{i}
are reported using two notations:
\alpha_{i}\in\{-1,0,1\}
and
\alpha_{i}\in\{0,1,2\}
(using that
\alpha=2=-1\,{\rm mod}\,3
).
Because
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=z_{q}^{\,\boldsymbol{\mu}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
, where
z_{q}
is the first
q
-th root of unity,
the value of
\boldsymbol{\mu}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}
gives the position of the operators
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
around the unit circle, as shown below the table. States are uniformly distributed over the three
3
-rd roots of unity.

2.4.3
Entropy of spin operators, symmetry and invariance

A final observation is that each interaction
\boldsymbol{\mu}
divides the space of states into equal-size groups of states that have the same interaction energy
H^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
.
Indeed, by definition (
14
), an operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
only takes values in the
q
roots of unity, corresponding to
q
locations around the unit circle. The states
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
for which the dot product
\boldsymbol{\mu}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}
takes the same value correspond to the same location (see example in Fig.
5
.b) and thus have the same interaction energy
H^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
. Besides, there are the same number of states at each these locations
14
14

14

which is also why an operator verifies Property
2
on the sum of spin operators.
, and therefore a single spin operator divides the state space into equal-sized groups of states of equal interaction energy (see Fig.
5
.b).

A consequence is that a model with a single interaction
\boldsymbol{\mu}
divides the state space into equal-sized groups of states of equal probability, thus encoding certain symmetries in the model probability distribution.
If
q
is prime, or if
\boldsymbol{\mu}
verifies for all integer
q^{\prime}\in[0,q-1]
that
\,q^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\mu}\neq\boldsymbol{0}
in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
, then there are
q
such groups (otherwise there are fewer groups
15
15

15

In this case, the states are equally distributed over a subset of roots, corresponding to the
q^{\prime}
-th roots of unity,
where
q^{\prime}
is the smallest integer verifying that
\,q^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{0}
in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
(
q^{\prime}
is a divisor of
q
).

).
Moreover, each time an independent interaction
16
16

16

A set of operators
\{\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\}
is said independent if the corresponding set of interaction vectors
\{\boldsymbol{\mu}\}
is linearly independent in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
(see Sec.
3.3
on independent models). There at most
n
independent operators.

is added to the model, each group is divided further into
q
equal-size groups of states of equal probabilities. Here the independence between interactions is defined by the linear independence between the corresponding vectors
\boldsymbol{\mu}
in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
(see Sec.
3.3
on independent models), and at most
n
independent operators can be added to a model.
For example, for
q=2
, a model with a single interaction
\boldsymbol{\mu}
maps each state
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
to the location
x=1
(for
\boldsymbol{\mu}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}=0
)
or
x=-1
(for
\boldsymbol{\mu}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}=1
)
on the unit circle depending on the parity of the variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mid\boldsymbol{\mu}}
involved in the interaction, thus dividing the states into two groups of equal probability. Adding another independent interaction
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}
will further divide each group in two depending on the parity of the variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mid\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}}
involved in
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}
.

The equal distribution of the states around the unit circle by an operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
implies a permutation in the energy
H^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
of the states as one rotates the parameter
\vec{g}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}
by
2\pi/q
at fixed strength
r_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
(as the amplitudes of the projections of
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}
on the
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
’s remain unchanged, just shuffled). In particular, for a model with a single interaction
\boldsymbol{\mu}
, this means that the amplitudes of the probability distribution over the states are simply shuffled when the parameter
\vec{g}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}
is rotated by
2\pi/q
at fixed strength.
For example, rotating the vector
\vec{g}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}
, from being aligned with one of the
q
-th roots of unity (i.e., aligned with the operator value of a group of states) to being aligned with another root (i.e., with the operator value of another group of states) results in shuffling the amplitude of the probability distribution over the states,
as shown in the first and last columns of Fig.
3
.a and
4
.a.
This effect is reflected in the invariance of the entropy of a model with a single interaction
\boldsymbol{\mu}
when the parameter
\vec{g}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}
is rotated by
2\pi/q
, thus giving a periodic structure to the entropy of the model as
\vec{g}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}
is continuously rotated at fixed strength
(see Fig.
3
.b and
4
.b).
At fixed value of the strength
r_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
, the entropy is maximal when
\vec{g}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}
points exactly between two consecutive directions of
\vec{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
, and minimal when its direction coincides with the direction of one of the vectors
\vec{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
.

More generally, the entropy landscape of an operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
under a spin model with the single interaction
\boldsymbol{\mu}
as one varies the parameter
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
is the same for all operators at a given value of
q
(see Fig.
A1
for entropy plot at different values of
q
). This is valid under the condition that
q
is prime, or that
\boldsymbol{\mu}
verifies for all integer
q^{\prime}\in[0,q-1]
that
\,q^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\mu}\neq\boldsymbol{0}
in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
. Otherwise, the entropy plot will be that of the smallest value
q^{\prime}
such that
\,q^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{0}
.
Indeed, for a
q
-state spin model with a single interaction
\boldsymbol{\mu}
satisfying the condition above, the energy of the states can only take
q
different values,

H^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=-2\,\vec{g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}}\cdot\vec{z}_{q}^{\,j}
for
j\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})
, where
\vec{z}_{q}^{\,j}
is the vector in the complex plane corresponding to the root
z_{q}^{j}
.
The entropy of the model is then (see Appendix
A.5
):

\displaystyle S[P_{\mathcal{M}}]=(n-1)\log q+S[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}]\,,

(53)

which corresponds to the entropy of
(n-1)
purely random
q
-state variables, plus the entropy of the operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
under the model. This later is given by:

\displaystyle S[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}]=-\sum_{j=0}^{q-1}p_{j}\log p_{j}\,,\qquad{\rm where}\;\;p_{j}=\frac{e^{\,2\,\vec{g^{*}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\cdot\vec{z}_{q}^{\,j}}}{Z_{q}}\,\,{\rm and}\;\;Z_{q}=\sum_{j=0}^{q-1}e^{\,2\,\vec{g^{*}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\cdot\vec{z}_{q}^{\,j}}\,.

(54)

At fixed
q
, the entropy of an operator in a model with a single interaction
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is the same function of the parameters
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}
for all
\boldsymbol{\mu}
(if
\boldsymbol{\mu}
verifies that
\,c\boldsymbol{\mu}\neq\boldsymbol{0}
for all integer
c\in[0,q-1]
).
We will see in Sec.
4
that this is because all the
q
-state spin models with a single operator
\boldsymbol{\mu}
(verifying that
\forall c\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})
,
\,c\boldsymbol{\mu}\neq\boldsymbol{0}
) are equivalent.

3
Loop and cluster expansion of the partition function

In the binary case, Ref.
9
showed that multiple spin models can have the same partition function (up to permutation of their parameters) and therefore describe the same statistical structure.
This was obtained using a cluster expansion of the partition function, expressing it as a sum over the loops of the model: models with the same loop structure have the same partition function.
Introducing spin basis transformations, Ref.
9
showed that these models are different representations of the same abstract model seen in different bases.
These basis transformations, called gauge transformations in
9
, divide the space of models into equivalence classes of models describing the same statistical structure: models of the same class have the same partition function, and thus the same Fisher information matrix and model complexity.
They are characterized by the same number of operators, the same rank, and the same loop structure.
Here, we extend these results to the more general case of
q
-state spin models with
q\geq 2
, starting with the generalization of the loop expansion of the partition function in this section, and followed by the notion of gauge transformations and equivalent models in Sec.
4
.

3.1
Loop expansion of the (complex) partition function

For
q=2
, the partition function (
3
) of the high-order Ising model can be written as a sum over its loops
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{M})

9
:

\displaystyle{\rm for}\;\;q=2,\;\;\;

\displaystyle Z_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})=2^{n}\bigg(\prod_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}}\cosh(g_{\vecmu})\bigg)\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{M})}\prod_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\ell}\tanh(g_{\vecmu})\,,

(55)

\displaystyle{\rm where}\;\;\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{M})=\Big\{\ell\subseteq\mathcal{M}\;\Big|\;\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\ell}\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{0}\Big\}\,.

(56)

In Eq. (
56
), the sum over
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is taken in
(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{n}
(i.e., with a modulo 2) and
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{M})
contains the empty set
\ell=\emptyset
, for which the product over the elements of
\ell
in Eq. (
55
) is equal to
1
. Note that Ref.
9
defines a loop as a subset of operators
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
of the model whose product is equal to 1 for all states
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
of the system; this is equivalent to the sum of the corresponding binary representation
\boldsymbol{\mu}
being equal to
\boldsymbol{0}
, as written in Eq. (
56
).
Equation (
55
) implies that all the models with the same loop structure also have the same partition function (up to permutations of the parameters), and thus they have the same statistical properties (in particular, they have the same Fisher Information Matrix).

Similarly, for
q\geq 2
, we found that the partition function of the high-order discrete spin models in Eq. (
19
) can also be written as a sum over the “loops” of the model.

Property 9
.

Loop expansion of the partition function of
q
-state spin models (
19
).

Consider a
q
-state spin model of the form (
19
) defined by
\mathcal{M}\subseteq(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
. We denote by
K=|\mathcal{M}|
the number of operators in the model and arbitrarily index the operators from
1
to
K
:
\mathcal{M}=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\,\cdots,\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{K}\}
.
Using the generalized Euler formula (
A25
) for
e^{\,g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})}

37
, the partition function (
21
) of
\mathcal{M}
can be written under the form (see proof in App.
B.4
):

\displaystyle Z_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})=q^{n}\,\sum_{\boldsymbol{\ell}\in\mathcal{L}_{q}(\mathcal{M})}\prod_{k=1}^{K}c_{\ell_{k}}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}})\,,\qquad{where}\;\;c_{\ell}(x)=\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{x^{kq+\ell}}{(kq+\ell)!}

(57)

are the generalized Euler coefficients
37
(see App.
B.1
), and

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{q}(\mathcal{M})=\bigg\{\boldsymbol{\ell}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{K}\;\Big|\;\sum_{k=1}^{K}\ell_{k}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}=\boldsymbol{0}\bigg\}

(58)

is the set of loops of the model
\mathcal{M}
, in which
\ell_{k}
denotes the
k
-th coefficient of
\boldsymbol{\ell}
. The set
\mathcal{L}_{q}(\mathcal{M})
always contains the null vector
\boldsymbol{\ell}=\boldsymbol{0}
, for which the product over
k
in Eq. (
57
) is equal to
\prod_{k=1}^{K}c_{0}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}})
.

In Equation (
58
), we used a more general notation for
\mathcal{L}_{q}(\mathcal{M})
compared to Eq. (
56
): a loop is denoted as a vector
\boldsymbol{\ell}
in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{K}
and
\ell_{k}
denotes the
k
-th coefficient of
\boldsymbol{\ell}
.
For
q=2
, the coefficients
\ell_{k}
only take the values
0
or
1
and the loop
\boldsymbol{\ell}
is equivalently identified by
the subset of elements of
\mathcal{M}
for which
\ell_{k}=1
, as defined in Eq. (
56
).
Moreover, for
q=2
, the Euler coefficients in Eq. (
57
) are
c_{0}(x)=\cosh(x)
and
c_{1}(x)=\sinh(x)
, which reduces Eq. (
57
) to the expansion in Eq. (
55
). Equation (
57
) thus generalizes the loop expansion of the partition function to all
q
-state spin models for
q\geq 2
.

The loops of a spin model, defined in Eq. (
58
), are a set of linear dependencies between the operators of the model that result from the choice of the model architecture (i.e., which operators are chosen in the model).
These dependencies capture the statistical properties of the parametric model, as shown by the loop expansion of the partition function in Eq. (
57
).
The properties of the set of loops
\mathcal{L}_{q}(\mathcal{M})
of a spin model are further discussed in Sec.
4.5
. Figure
6
gives an example of the set of loops of a
q
-state spin model for
q=3
. Note that the figure doesn’t show trivial loops resulting from the structure of complex conjugate operators (see next section).

3.2
Loop expansion of the real partition function over non-trivial loops (accounting for c.c. operators)

The expansion of the partition function in Eq. (
57
) is obtained for any
q
-state spin model, real or complex valued.
In general, operators are not necessarily included by conjugate pairs in the model and the Hamiltonian
H(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
and the partition function in Eq. (
57
) can take complex values.
In order to interpret a
q
-state spin model as a probabilistic model, the Hamiltonian of the model must be real. This introduces “trivial” loops that can be removed from the expansion.

For the Hamiltonian to be real, operators should be included by conjugate pairs
(\boldsymbol{\mu},-\boldsymbol{\mu})
in the model and their parameters should satisfy the constraint
g_{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}=g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}
(see Definition
5
).
For example, for
q=3
, a model containing the operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})=s_{1}
must also have its complex conjugate (c.c.) operator
17
17

17

We recall that the conjugate of an operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})
is the operator
\phi^{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})
, where
-\boldsymbol{\mu}\in((\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z}))^{n}
is the vector with elements of the form
(q-\mu_{i})
(see Sec.
2
).
For example, for a one-spin system
\boldsymbol{s}=(s_{1})
with
q=3
, the operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}(\boldsymbol{s})=s_{1}
is identified by the (one-dimensional) vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}=(1)
, and the operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{s})=s_{1}^{2}
by the vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}=(2)
. The latter can be re-written as

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}=(q-1)=-(1)=-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}
over
\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}
, which means that the operators
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}(\boldsymbol{s})
and
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{s})
are conjugate operators (see Sec.
2
).

\phi^{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})=s_{1}^{\,2}
.
By definition, conjugate operators,
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}
, verify that
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}=\boldsymbol{0}
in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
, and as a consequence they always form a loop in Eq. (
58
).
However, this is a “trivial” loop, in the sense that conjugate operators are included together by construction for the Hamiltonian of the model to be real.
This loop thus encodes a “trivial” statistical dependency of the model.

A model can have many such trivial loops contributing to its partition function (
57
). A model with a single pair of conjugate operators has already multiple such contributions. For example, for
q=3
, a model with a single pair of conjugate operators
\mathcal{M}=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}\}
with
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}=-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}
(e.g.,
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}=\{s_{1},s_{1}^{2}\}
) has two trivial loops resulting from the conjugate relation:
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}=0
and
2\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}+2\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}=0
. Although these equations encode the same dependence relation, they correspond to two different loops
\boldsymbol{\ell}
in Eq. (
58
) and therefore to two different terms in the expansion of the partition function.
The set of loops of
\mathcal{M}
is then
\mathcal{L}_{q}(\mathcal{M})=\{(0,0),(1,1),(2,2)\}
, and the partition function (
57
) then takes the form:

\displaystyle Z_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})

\displaystyle=3^{n}\,\Big[c_{0}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}})\,c_{0}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}^{*})+c_{1}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}})\,c_{1}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}^{*})+c_{2}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}})\,c_{2}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}^{*})\Big]\,,

(59)

where the explicit expressions of the functions
c_{0}
,
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
for
q=3
are given in Eq. (
B.1
), and where we used that
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}}=g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}^{*}
because
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}=-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}
.

The loops (
58
) of a spin model capture the statistical constraints of the probabilistic model imposed by the choice of the model architecture (i.e., of the set of interactions).
Because the constraints imposed by complex-conjugate operators are there by construction, we can compute explicitly their contribution to the partition function and remove them from the set of loops. The latter will thus better capture non-trivial constraints of the model. In the following, we reformulate the partition function with this approach, separating trivial c.c. dependencies of the model from the non-trivial ones.

Definition 10
.

To group complex conjugate terms together, we organize the operators of
\mathcal{M}
into three disjoint sets,
\mathcal{M}_{0}
,
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{1}
, such that
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{0}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0}

\displaystyle=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}\mid\boldsymbol{\mu}=-\boldsymbol{\mu}\}\,,

(60)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{1}\cup\mathcal{M}^{*}_{1}

\displaystyle=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}\mid\boldsymbol{\mu}\neq-\boldsymbol{\mu}\}\,,\qquad{\rm with}\;\;\forall\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{1},\;-\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}^{*}_{1}\;.

(61)

As all three sets are disjoint, if
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{1}
, then
-\boldsymbol{\mu}\notin\mathcal{M}_{1}
.
For all odd values of
q
,
\mathcal{M}_{0}=\emptyset
.
The sets
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{1}
are not uniquely defined, but any choice that respects Eq. (
61
) is fine. For example, one way to construct
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}
is, for each element
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{1}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}
, place
\boldsymbol{\mu}
in
\mathcal{M}_{1}
if
\boldsymbol{\mu}<-\boldsymbol{\mu}
18
18

18

As for Definition
6
, the ordering is based on the order of the integer representations of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
and
-\boldsymbol{\mu}
in base
q
, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{\mu}<-\boldsymbol{\mu}
, if its first entry (most significant qits)
\mu_{i}
such that
\mu_{i}\neq-\mu_{i}
is in the interval
[0,\frac{q}{2}[
.
,
and place it in
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}
otherwise.
Another way is to successively remove conjugate pairs from the set
\{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}\mid\boldsymbol{\mu}\neq-\boldsymbol{\mu}\}
and to place one element of the pair in
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and the other in
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}
.

This organization gives the following expression for the partition function:

\displaystyle Z_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\left(\prod_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}}e^{\;g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})}\right)\left(\prod_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{1}}e^{\;g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})+g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}\phi^{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})}\right)\,,

(62)

in which we grouped together c.c. contributions
(\boldsymbol{\mu},-\boldsymbol{\mu})
for the operators in
\mathcal{M}_{1}
(operators
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
are their own c.c. by definition (
60
)).
Using the generalized Euler formula (
A25
), the contribution coming from complex conjugate terms can be re-written as a single sum over powers of
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}

(see App.
B.3
):

\displaystyle\forall\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{1},\qquad e^{\;g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})+g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}\,\phi^{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})}=\sum_{r=0}^{q-1}B_{r}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\,\left[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right]^{r}\,,

(63)

\displaystyle{\rm where}\;\;\;\forall r\in\{0,\,\cdots,\,q-1\}\,,\;\;\;B_{r}(x)=\sum_{m=0}^{q-1}c_{m}(x)\,c_{[(m-r)\bmod{q}]}(x^{*})\,,

(64)

where the coefficients
c_{m}(x)
are given in Eq. (
57
).
Besides, by definition (
60
), all the elements
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
verify that
2\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{0}
in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
and, as a consequence, that
\left[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right]^{2}=1
for all states
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
.
This means that for
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
, one can use the original Euler formula for
\exp(g_{\vecmu}\phi^{\vecmu}(\state))
(see App.
B.2
):

\displaystyle\forall\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{0},\qquad e^{g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})}=A_{0}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})+A_{1}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\;\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\,,

(65)

\displaystyle{\rm where}\;\;\;A_{0}(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{\frac{q}{2}-1}c_{2k}(x)=\cosh(x)\;\;\;{\rm and}\;\;\;A_{1}(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{\frac{q}{2}-1}c_{2k+1}(x)=\sinh(x)\,.

(66)

As a sanity check, if
q=2
, then
\mathcal{M}_{1}=\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}=\emptyset
, and Eq. (
62
) combined with Eq. (
65
) returns the original definition of the partition function of the high-order Ising model (
3
).
Finally, substituting these formulas in Eq. (
62
) and summing over the states
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, we obtain the following loop expansion of the partition function (see proof in App.
B.5
), where the set of loops does not contain any trivial contributions from c.c. operators.

Property 10
.

(for real-valued
q
-state spin models) Loop expansion of the partition function without trivial loops from c.c. operators.
Consider a spin model
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{0}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}
.
Let us denote
K_{0}=|\mathcal{M}_{0}|
and
K_{1}=|\mathcal{M}_{1}|
the number of operators respectively in
\mathcal{M}_{0}
and in
\mathcal{M}_{1}
, and arbitrarily index the operators
\mathcal{M}_{0}=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0,1},\cdots,\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0,K_{0}}\}
and
\mathcal{M}_{1}=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,1},\cdots,\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,K_{1}}\}
.
The partition function can be written under the form (see App.
B.5
):

\displaystyle Z_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})=q^{n}\,\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\ell}_{0},\boldsymbol{\ell}_{1})\in\mathcal{L}^{cc}_{q}(\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{1})}\prod_{i=1}^{K_{0}}A_{\ell_{0,i}}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0,i}})\prod_{j=1}^{K_{1}}B_{\ell_{1,j}}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,j}})\,,

(67)

where the coefficients
A_{0}(x)
,
A_{1}(x)
, and
B_{r}(x)
are given by Eq. (
66
) and Eq. (
64
), and where we defined the reduced set of loops of a model as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{cc}_{q}(\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{1})=\bigg\{(\boldsymbol{\ell}_{0},\boldsymbol{\ell}_{1})\in(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{K_{0}}\times(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{K_{1}}\;\Big|\;\sum_{i=1}^{K_{0}}\ell_{0,i}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0,i}+\sum_{j=1}^{K_{1}}\ell_{1,j}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,j}=\boldsymbol{0}\bigg\}\;.

(68)

A loop is now defined by two vectors,
\boldsymbol{\ell}_{0}\in(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{K_{0}}
weighting the elements of
\mathcal{M}_{0}
and
\boldsymbol{\ell}_{1}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{K_{1}}
weighting the elements of
\mathcal{M}_{1}
. The coefficients
\ell_{0,i}
and
\ell_{1,i}
denote respectively the
i
-th elements of
\boldsymbol{\ell}_{0}
and
\boldsymbol{\ell}_{1}
.
Note that the entries of
\boldsymbol{\ell}_{0}
are taken in
\{0,1\}
, but they are thought of as elements of
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})
.
The sum over elements of
\mathcal{M}_{0}
and
\mathcal{M}_{1}
is thus well defined as a sum in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
:
the sum over the elements
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0,i}
is implicitly taken modulo
2
because
2\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0,i}=\boldsymbol{0}
, while the sum over
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,i}
is modulo
q
. The resulting two vectors should sum to zero in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
.

We added the superscript
cc
to
\mathcal{L}_{q}^{cc}
to indicate that this set of loops is obtained after removing the contributions from c.c. operators.
One can easily check that there are indeed no such contributions left in
\mathcal{L}_{q}^{cc}
, as none of the operators
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
are c.c. to each other.
The reduced loop structure
\mathcal{L}_{q}^{cc}
thus better captures non-trivial dependencies imposed by the model architecture.
Figure
6
gives an example of the set of loops
\mathcal{L}_{q}^{cc}
of a
q
-state spin model for
q=3
after removing trivial loops from c.c. operators.
Note that besides removing c.c. contributions, the approach also removes contributions from combinations of these trivial loops with other loops.

3.3
Independent models and cluster expansion of the partition function

In the binary case, Ref.
9
;
15
define
independent models
, as models whose set of operators
\mathcal{M}_{ind}
is an independent set, or equivalently whose set of loops only contains the empty loop,
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{M}_{ind})=\{\emptyset\}
. The cluster expansion of the partition function usually consists of expanding the partition function around the approximation of a “non-interacting” system,
which, in the context of spin models, corresponds to the partition function of the model computed as if it was an independent model. For binary systems (
q=2
), this is the case of the expressions of the partition function in Eq. (
55
) and (
57
), for which having no loop between operators yields the known expression of the partition function of an independent model
\mathcal{M}_{ind}

9
:

\displaystyle Z_{\mathcal{M}_{ind}}(\boldsymbol{g})=2^{n}\,\prod_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{ind}}\cosh(g_{\vecmu})\,,\qquad{\rm for}\;\;q=2\,.

(69)

The cluster expansion can then be made explicit by rewriting Eq. (
55
) under the form:

\displaystyle{\rm for}\;\;q=2,\qquad

\displaystyle Z_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})=Z^{ind}_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})\left[1+\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{M})\backslash\emptyset}\;\prod_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\ell}\tanh(g_{\vecmu})\right]\,,

(70)

where
Z^{ind}_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})
denotes the partition function of
\mathcal{M}
computed as if
\mathcal{M}
were an independent model, given by Eq. (
69
), and where the sum over the loops now excludes the empty loop.
Note that the fact that a model is independent doesn’t mean that the spins don’t interact. An independent model can have interactions of any order between the spins. Instead,
independence
means that the interactions don’t constrain each other (no loops), so in some sense there is no “interaction” between the operators.

Similarly, one can factorize the term corresponding to the empty loop in the expansion of the partition function for
q>2
, both in Eq. (
57
) and Eq. (
67
). However, this term doesn’t necessarily correspond to the partition function one would get for an independent model. In the following, we give the definition of independent models for complex- and real-valued
q
-state spin models,
which we then use to write the partition function in the form of a cluster expansion.

3.3.1
General expression

The definition of independent models
9
;
15
can be easily extended to
q\geq 2
. To do so, we first introduce the notion of independence between spin operators, from which follows a natural definition of independent models.

Definition 11
.

Independent set of operators.
Consider a system of
n

q
-state spin variables.
A set of operators
\{\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}},\,\dots,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{K}}\}
is said independent if and only if the set of vectors
\mathcal{S}=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\,\dots,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{K}\}
is linearly independent in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
:

\displaystyle\forall(r_{k})\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{K},\;\;\;\;\sum_{k=1}^{K}r_{k}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}=\boldsymbol{0}\;({\rm mod}\;q)\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad r_{k}=0\;\;\;\;\forall\,k\,.

(71)

This is equivalent to the set of loops of
\mathcal{S}
containing only the null vector:
\mathcal{L}_{q}(\mathcal{S})=\{\boldsymbol{0}\}
.

Definition 12
.

Independent model.
A
q
-state spin model is said independent if the set
\mathcal{M}
of operators of the model is an independent set, or equivalently, if the set of loops of the model only contains the null vector (empty loop)
\mathcal{L}_{q}(\mathcal{M})=\{\boldsymbol{0}\}
.

Property 11
.

Partition function of an independent model:

Consider an independent model
\mathcal{M}_{ind}
for any
q\geq 2
. Taking the term corresponding to the empty loop in Eq. (
57
), the partition function of
\mathcal{M}_{ind}
takes the form:

\displaystyle{\rm for}\;q\geq 2\,,\qquad Z_{\mathcal{M}_{ind}}(\boldsymbol{g})=q^{n}\,\prod_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{ind}}c_{0}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\,,\qquad{where}\;\;c_{0}(x)=\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{x^{kq}}{(kq)!}\,.

(72)

This equation generalizes Eq. (
69
) to larger values of
q
, as
c_{0}(x)=\cosh(x)
for
q=2
.

The partition function (
57
) can then be rewritten under the following form.

Property 12
.

Cluster expansion of the partition of
q
-state spin models:

Consider a
q
-state spin model
\mathcal{M}
with
K
operators arbitrarily indexed as
\mathcal{M}=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\cdots,\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{K}\}
. For all
q\geq 2
, the partition function of
\mathcal{M}
can be written under the cluster expansion form:

\displaystyle Z_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})=Z^{ind}_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})\,\left[\,1+\sum_{\boldsymbol{\ell}\in\mathcal{L}_{q}(\mathcal{M})\backslash\boldsymbol{0}}\;\prod_{k=1}^{K}\frac{c_{\ell_{k}}}{c_{0}}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}})\,\right]\,,

(73)

where the coefficients
c_{\ell}(x)
are given in Eq. (
57
), and where
Z^{ind}_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})
is the partition function of
\mathcal{M}
computed as if
\mathcal{M}
was an independent model, which is given by Eq. (
72
).

For
q=2
, this equation reduces to the known Eq. (
70
), using that
c_{0}(x)=\cosh(x)
and
c_{1}(x)=\sinh(x)
.

3.3.2
Real-valued spin models

For real-valued spin models, we recall that operators must be included by conjugate pairs in the model for the Hamiltonian to be real. It is clear that two conjugate operators
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}
are not independent from each other, as they satisfy
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}=-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}
. However, we previously obtained a more explicit form of the loop expansion of the partition function (
67
) by computing analytically the contributions of complex conjugate operators (thus removing them from the loop structure).
To obtain a similar form for the cluster expansion, we extend the definition of independent models to real-valued
q
-state spin models, by requiring independence only for one element for each conjugate pair.

Definition 13
.

Real-valued independent model.
Using the decomposition of Definition
10
, a real-valued spin model
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{0}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}
is said independent if
\mathcal{M}_{0}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}
is an independent set of operators.

Although the decomposition of
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{0}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}\,
is not unique, this definition doesn’t depend on the precise assignment of the conjugate operators to
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}
, because the elements of
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}
are related by just a sign change.
The absence of dependencies among the operators in
\mathcal{M}_{0}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}
of an independent model means that the model doesn’t contain any (non-empty) loop, except for the trivial loops due to c.c. operators in
\mathcal{M}_{1}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}
. Reciprocally, a real-valued model that doesn’t contain any loop, except due to c.c. operators, verifies the definition of independence.

Corollary 1
.

A real-valued independent model
is a model whose set of loops in Eq. (
58
) only contains: 1) the empty loop, and 2) trivial loops due to c.c. operators.
Equivalently, it is a model whose reduced set of loops in Eq. (
68
) solely contains the empty loop,
\mathcal{L}^{cc}_{q}=\{(\boldsymbol{0},\,\boldsymbol{0})\}
.

The partition function expansion in Eq. (
67
) thus already separates the contributions obtained as if the real-valued model were independent, for which
(\boldsymbol{\ell}_{0},\boldsymbol{\ell}_{1})=(\boldsymbol{0},\,\boldsymbol{0})
,
from the contributions resulting from non-trivial loop constraints between the operators, for which
(\boldsymbol{\ell}_{0},\boldsymbol{\ell}_{1})\neq(\boldsymbol{0},\,\boldsymbol{0})
.

Note that we kept
\mathcal{M}_{0}
in definition
13
, because it allows the definition to be also valid for spin models with
q=2
(which are already real-valued without having to introduce the structure of c.c. operators). Indeed in the binary case,
\mathcal{M}_{1}=\emptyset
and the definition and corollary above are consistent with the previous definition of independent models
9
;
15
.
For
q>2
, any operator
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
verifies that
2\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{0}
(by definition of
\mathcal{M}_{0}
) and therefore doesn’t satisfy the condition of independence in Eq. (
71
).
This means that, for
q>2
, independent models have
\mathcal{M}_{0}=\emptyset
. The term corresponding to the empty loop (
\boldsymbol{\ell}_{1}=\boldsymbol{0}
) in the partition function (
67
) then gives the partition function of a real-valued independent model for
q>2
.

Property 13
.

Partition function of a real-valued independent model for
q>2
:

Consider a real-valued independent model
\mathcal{M}_{Rind}
for
q>2
.
Using the decomposition of Definition
10
,
\mathcal{M}_{Rind}=\mathcal{M}_{1}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}
and the partition function of
\mathcal{M}_{Rind}
takes the form
(term
\boldsymbol{\ell}_{1}=\boldsymbol{0}
in Eq. (
67
)):

\displaystyle{\rm for}\;q>2\,,\qquad Z_{\mathcal{M}_{Rind}}(\boldsymbol{g})

\displaystyle=q^{n}\,\prod_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{1}}\,\left[\,\sum_{m=0}^{q-1}\left\lVert c_{m}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}})\right\rVert^{2}\,\right]\,,\;\;\;\;\;

(74)

where the
c_{m}(x)
are complex coefficients given in Eq. (
57
) and
\lVert c_{m}(x)\rVert=\sqrt{c_{m}(x)\,c_{m}(x^{*})}
denotes the norm of
c_{m}(x)
(because
c_{m}(x^{*})=[c_{m}(x)]^{*}
).

To obtain Eq. (
74
) from Eq. (
67
), we used that
B_{0}(x)=\sum_{m=0}^{q-1}c_{m}(x)c_{m}(x^{*})
from Eq. (
64
).
For example, for
q=3
,

\displaystyle Z_{\mathcal{M}_{Rind}}(\boldsymbol{g})=3^{n}\,\prod_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{1}}\Big[c_{0}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}})\,c_{0}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}^{*})+c_{1}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}})\,c_{1}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}^{*})+c_{2}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}})\,c_{2}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}^{*})\Big]\,,

which gives back Eq. (
59
), previously obtained for a model with a single conjugate pair.

For
q>2
, for models for which
\mathcal{M}_{0}=\emptyset
(this is always the case if
q
is prime), the partition function (
67
) can then be written under the following cluster expansion form.

Property 14
.

Cluster expansion of the partition of a real-valued model
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{1}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}
(for
q>2
):

Consider a real-valued spin model
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{1}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}
, for which
\mathcal{M}_{0}=\emptyset
in the decomposition of Definition
10
.
We denote by
K_{1}=|\mathcal{M}_{1}|
the number of operators in
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and arbitrarily index the operators,
\mathcal{M}_{1}=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,1},\cdots,\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,K_{1}}\}
. The partition function of
\mathcal{M}
can then be written under the cluster expansion form:

\displaystyle Z_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})=Z^{Rind}_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})\,\left[\,1+\sum_{\boldsymbol{\ell}_{1}\in\mathcal{L}^{cc}_{q}(\mathcal{M}_{1})\backslash\boldsymbol{0}}\;\prod_{j=1}^{K_{1}}\frac{B_{\ell_{1,j}}}{B_{0}}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,j}})\,\right]\,,

(75)

where the coefficients
B_{r}(x)
are given by Eq. (
64
), and where
Z^{Rind}_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})
is the partition function of
\mathcal{M}
computed as if it were a real-valued independent model, which is given by Eq. (
74
).
For clarity, we simplified the notation
(\boldsymbol{\ell}_{0},\boldsymbol{\ell}_{1})\in\mathcal{L}^{cc}_{q}(\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{1})\backslash(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{0})
into
\boldsymbol{\ell}_{1}\in\mathcal{L}^{cc}_{q}(\mathcal{M}_{1})\backslash\boldsymbol{0}
, because
\mathcal{M}_{0}=\emptyset
.

This cluster expansion is
always valid when
q>2
is prime
.

More generally, for real-valued models with
\mathcal{M}_{0}\neq\emptyset
, we can factorize the term corresponding to the empty loop
(\boldsymbol{\ell}_{0},\boldsymbol{\ell}_{1})=(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{0})
in Eq. (
67
), which gives the following general expression of the cluster expansion:

\displaystyle Z_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})=\frac{Z^{ind}_{\mathcal{M}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{g}_{0})}{2^{n}}\,Z^{Rind}_{\mathcal{M}_{1}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{g}_{1})\,\left[\,1+\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\ell}_{0},\boldsymbol{\ell}_{1})\in\mathcal{L}^{cc}_{q}(\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{1})\backslash(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{0})}\;\prod_{i=1}^{K_{0}}\frac{A_{\ell_{0,i}}}{A_{0}}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0,i}})\prod_{j=1}^{K_{1}}\frac{B_{\ell_{1,j}}}{B_{0}}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,j}})\,\right]\,,

(76)

where we can now recognize the factorized term as the partition function of
\mathcal{M}
computed as if: 1) the elements in
\mathcal{M}_{1}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}
were to form a real-valued independent model
Z^{Rind}_{\mathcal{M}_{1}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}}(\boldsymbol{g}_{1})=q^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{K_{1}}B_{0}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,j}})
as in Eq. (
74
), and 2) the elements in
\mathcal{M}_{0}
were to form an binary independent model
Z^{ind}_{\mathcal{M}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{g}_{0})=2^{n}\prod_{i=1}^{K_{0}}A_{0}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0,i}})
as in Eq. (
69
) (with
A_{0}(x)=\cosh(x)
).

3.3.3
Additional considerations when
q
is not prime

When
q
is not prime, some operators
\boldsymbol{\mu}
other than those in
\mathcal{M}_{0}
can create “
self-loops
” of the form
\eta\,\boldsymbol{\mu}=0
, where
\eta
is an integer strictly smaller than
q
. Just like for the operators in
\mathcal{M}_{0}
, these operators cannot contribute to an independent model, because they don’t satisfy Eq. (
71
). We call them
self-dependent
operators.
Because of these operators, the notion of independent models may seem ambiguous.
For example, consider a model for
q=6
with a single pair of c.c. operators
\mathcal{M}=\{\boldsymbol{\mu},-\boldsymbol{\mu}\}
that verifies the self-dependence relation
3\boldsymbol{\mu}=0
(e.g., the one-body operator
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(2)
for a system with a single spin variable). This model has two loops in Eq. (
68
), the empty loop
\boldsymbol{\ell}_{1}=(0)
and the self-loop
\boldsymbol{\ell}_{1}=(3)
, which yields the partition function (
67
):

\displaystyle Z_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})=6^{n}\,[B_{0}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})+B_{3}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})]\,.

(77)

This model is not independent (because of the self-loop). However, from a modeling perspective, a model with a single pair of c.c. operator could be seen as “independent”, in the sense that: 1) it models (at least partially) one dimension of the system, and 2) it doesn’t encode constraints between different operators. For
q
non-prime, we extend the notion of independent models to also include models with self-dependent operators that do not create non-trivial constraints (loops) with the other operators of the model.
We call these models
weakly independent
19
19

19

The notion of weakly independent model is best defined using the definition of length of a module.
As
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
is not a vector space, but a module
47
we introduce the length of a module, a generalization of dimension from vector spaces to modules.
The length of a module
M
, denoted
l(M)
, is the number of non-zero submodules in the longest possible chain of distinct submodules:

0\subset M_{1}\subset M_{2}\subset\dots\subset M_{l(M)}=M.

For a finite set of vectors
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{\nu}_{K}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
, we denote by
\langle\boldsymbol{\nu}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{\nu}_{K}\rangle
the submodule of
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
generated by these vectors, i.e., the set of all
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})
-linear combinations
\sum_{k}r_{k}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{k}
with
r_{k}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})
. Using this notion, a real-valued spin model

\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{0}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}
is weakly independent if and only if

l(\langle\mathcal{M}_{0}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}\rangle_{gen})=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\,\in\,\mathcal{M}_{0}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}}l\bigl(\langle\boldsymbol{\mu}\rangle\bigr).

(78)

.

Corollary 2
.

A real-valued weakly independent model (for
q
non-prime)
is a model whose set of loops in Eq. (
58
) solely contains the following loops or their combinations: 1) the empty loop; 2) trivial loops due to c.c. operators; 3) trivial loops due to self-dependent operators
(i.e., of the type
\eta\,\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{0}
with
\eta<q
).

For models with self-dependent operators, one can get a more explicit form of the partition function by computing the contribution of the self-loops to the partition function (similarly to what was done in Eq. (
67
) for the operators of
\mathcal{M}_{0}
).
The terms corresponding to the non-trivial loops would then capture the correction to the partition function due to the non-trivial dependencies between the different operators (i.e., not due to self-loops or c.c. operators).

The contributions of self-loops of the form
2\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{0}
, which come from the operators of
\mathcal{M}_{0}
(when
q
is even),
have been already computed explicitly in Eq. (
67
). This was done by grouping odd and even powers of
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
in the Euler formula (
65
) for
e^{g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})}
.
Similarly, consider a self-dependent operator
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{1}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}
and the smallest integer
\eta
such that
\eta\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{0}
forms a self-loop (
\eta
is a factor of
q
).
The contribution of this self-loop can be explicitly computed by grouping together the terms that correspond to

\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
raised to the same power modulo
\eta
in the generalized Euler formula of Eq. (
63
) (using the fact that
\eta\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{0}
implies
[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})]^{\eta}=1
):

\displaystyle e^{\;g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})+g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}\,\phi^{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})}=\sum_{r=0}^{q-1}B_{r}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\,\left[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right]^{r}=\sum_{r^{\prime}=0}^{\eta-1}C^{\eta}_{r^{\prime}}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\,\left[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right]^{r^{\prime}}\,.

(79)

Here the coefficient
C^{\eta}_{r^{\prime}}(x)
collects the coefficients
B_{r}(x)
that correspond
to the same power
r^{\prime}
(modulo
\eta
) of
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
:

\displaystyle\forall r^{\prime}\in\{0,\,\cdots,\,\eta-1\}\,,\;\;\;C^{\eta}_{r^{\prime}}(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{(q/\eta)-1}B_{k\eta+r^{\prime}}(x)\,,

(80)

and
B_{r}(x)
is given by Eq. (
64
). We recall that
\eta
is a factor of
q
, and thus
q/\eta
is an integer.
This expression is valid also when
\eta=q
, in which case
C_{r}^{q}(x)=B_{r}(x)
.
Finally, substituting Eq. (
65
) and (
79
) in Eq. (
62
) and computing the sum over the states, we obtain the following loop expansion for the partition function:

Property 15
.

Loop expansion of the partition function of real-valued
q
-state spin models (
19
),
without trivial loops from c.c. operators and without self-loops.

Consider a spin model
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{0}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}
and
arbitrarily index the operators
\mathcal{M}_{0}=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0,1},\cdots,\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0,K_{0}}\}
and
\mathcal{M}_{1}=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,1},\cdots,\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,K_{1}}\}
.
The partition function can be written under the form:

\displaystyle Z_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})=q^{n}\,\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\ell}_{0},\boldsymbol{\ell}_{1})\in\mathcal{L}^{cc,s}_{q}(\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{1})}\prod_{i=1}^{K_{0}}A_{\ell_{0,i}}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0,i}})\prod_{j=1}^{K_{1}}C^{\eta_{j}}_{\ell_{1,j}}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,j}})\,,

(81)

where
\eta_{j}
denotes the smallest integer such that
\eta_{j}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,j}=\boldsymbol{0}
(
\eta_{j}
is equal to
q
if
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,j}
is not self-dependent, and is a factor of
q
otherwise).
Here, the contributions from self-loops are explicitly computed and the loop structure
\mathcal{L}^{cc,s}_{q}
doesn’t contain self-loops anymore:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{cc,s}_{q}(\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{1})=\bigg\{(\boldsymbol{\ell}_{0},\boldsymbol{\ell}_{1})\in N_{2}^{\,K_{0}}\bigotimes_{j=1}^{K_{1}}N_{\eta_{j}}\;\Big|\;\sum_{i=1}^{K_{0}}\ell_{0,i}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0,i}+\sum_{j=1}^{K_{1}}\ell_{1,j}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,j}=\boldsymbol{0}\bigg\}\;,

(82)

where
N_{\eta}=\{0,\dots,\eta-1\}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z}
.
The coefficients
A_{0}(x)
,
A_{1}(x)
, and
C_{r}^{\eta}(x)
are given by Eq. (
66
) and Eq. (
80
). We added the superscript
s
to
\mathcal{L}_{q}^{cc,s}
to indicate that this set of loops is obtained after removing self-loops.

Finally, the term corresponding to the empty loop in Eq. (
81
) now gives the expression of the partition function for a weakly independent model:

Property 16
.

Partition function of a weakly independent model
\mathcal{M}_{wind}=\mathcal{M}_{0}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}
.

\displaystyle Z_{\mathcal{M}_{wind}}(\boldsymbol{g})

\displaystyle=q^{n}\,\prod_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}}\cosh(g_{\vecmu_{0}})\,\prod_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{1}}C_{0}^{\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}})\,,

(83)

where
\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
denotes the smallest integer such that
\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{0}
for all operator
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{1}
, and
C_{0}^{\eta}
is given by Eq. (
80
).

For example, for a model
\mathcal{M}=\{\boldsymbol{\mu},-\boldsymbol{\mu}\}
verifying
3\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{0}
with
q=6
, we recover Eq. (
77
).
As a sanity check, for an independent model with
q=2
, one has that
\mathcal{M}_{1}=\emptyset
and we recover Eq. (
69
). For a real-valued independent model with
q>2
, one has that
\mathcal{M}_{0}=\emptyset
and
\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=q
for all
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{1}
, and we recover Eq. (
74
).
Finally, substituting Eq. (
83
) in Eq. (
81
), we obtain:

Property 17
.

Cluster expansion of the partition around the weakly independent model.

Consider a real-valued spin model
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{0}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}
. The cluster expansion of the partition function of
\mathcal{M}
around the weakly independent model is given by:

\displaystyle Z_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})=Z^{wind}_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})\,\left[\,1+\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\ell}_{0},\boldsymbol{\ell}_{1})\in\mathcal{L}^{cc,s}_{q}(\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{1})\backslash(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{0})}\;\prod_{i=1}^{K_{0}}\frac{A_{\ell_{0,i}}}{A_{0}}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0,i}})\prod_{j=1}^{K_{1}}\frac{C_{\ell_{1,j}}^{\eta_{j}}}{C_{0}^{\eta_{j}}}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,j}})\,\right]\,,

(84)

where
Z^{wind}_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})
denotes the partition function of
\mathcal{M}
computed as if it were weakly independent, which is given by Eq. (
83
). The remaining set of loops
\mathcal{L}^{cc,s}_{q}(\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{1})
doesn’t contain any trivial loops (from c.c. operators or self-loops).

3.4
Rank and dimension of a spin model

The definition
11
of an independent set of operators allows us to extend the definition of the rank of a spin model,
originally introduced in the binary case
9
;
15
, to
q\geq 2
:

Definition 14
.

Rank of a spin model.
The rank of a spin model
\mathcal{M}
, denoted by
\rank(\M)
, is the cardinality of the largest linearly independent set of operators in
\mathcal{M}
.
By definition, in an
n
-spin system,
\,\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{M})\leq n
.

For example, by definition
12
, the rank of an independent model is equal to the number of operators in the model.
For a real-valued independent spin model with
q>2
,
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{1}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}
, the rank of the model is equal to the number of operators in
\mathcal{M}_{1}
.

When
q
is not prime, the rank of the model is not sufficient to capture the number of dimensions in which the model is embedded.
Consider for example a model for
q=6
with a single pair of c.c. operators
\mathcal{M}=\{\boldsymbol{\mu},-\boldsymbol{\mu}\}
verifying the self-dependence relation
3\boldsymbol{\mu}=0
.
The rank of this model is zero, but the model is embedded in at least one dimension. This is because the operator
\boldsymbol{\mu}
lives in one dimension, but doesn’t span the whole
(\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z})
.
To clarify this distinction when
q
is not prime, we introduce the definition of the (embedding) dimension of a
q
-state spin model.
This definition is inspired by the definition of basis of a spin model, which was introduced in the binary case
15
as a minimal set of independent operators that can generate all the operators of the model.

Definition 15
.

Dimension of a spin model.
The dimension of a spin model
\mathcal{M}
, denoted
\dim(\mathcal{M})
, is the cardinality of the minimal independent set of operators that can generate all the operators of the model
\mathcal{M}
. By definition, in an
n
-spin system,
\,\dim(\mathcal{M})\leq n
.

For example, for
q=6
, for the one-spin model
\mathcal{M}=\{\boldsymbol{\mu},-\boldsymbol{\mu}\}
with the one-body operator
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(2)
and its c.c.
-\boldsymbol{\mu}=(4)
, the set with the single operator
\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}=(1)\}
is a minimal independent set that can generate all the operators of
\mathcal{M}
in
\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
(the operator
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}
is not self-dependent, as oppose to the operators
(2)
and
(4)
). The dimension of
\mathcal{M}
is therefore
\dim(\mathcal{M})=1
.

For models without self-dependent operators, any maximal independent set of operators in the model can generate all the operators of the model, which means that the dimension of the model is equal to its rank.
This is always the case for models with
q=2
, and in general for any
q
prime, for which there is therefore no need to introduce a distinction between rank and dimension
15
.
This is also the case for independent models (complex- or real-valued) at any value of
q
(prime or not).
For models with self-dependent operators (which exist only when
q
is not prime), the dimension of the model can be larger than its rank. In the example above,
\mathcal{M}=\{(2),(4)\}
for
q=6
, the rank of the model is
\rank(\M)=0
, but its dimension is
\dim(\mathcal{M})=1
.

3.5
Discussion: Loops as algebraic constraints defining the statistical properties of the spin model

The loops of a
q
-state spin model, defined in Eq. (
58
), are linear dependencies between the operators of the model that result from the choice of the model architecture (i.e., which operators are in the model).
Equation (
57
) connects these structural dependencies, captured by the loop structure on the r.h.s., to the statistical properties of the model, captured by the partition function on the l.h.s.
More specifically, the Hessian of the log-partition function corresponds to the Fisher information matrix, a covariance matrix that characterizes how susceptible the model probability distribution is to perturbations around any given value of the parameters.
The structural dependencies encoded by the loops
\mathcal{L}_{q}(\mathcal{M})
thus ultimately shape the Fisher information matrix, making the model more or less susceptible to changes in the parameters, or, from a modeling perspective, more or less malleable to fit a wide variety of data patterns.
This flexibility of a statistical model can be summarized in a single characteristic called the geometric complexity of the model
5
;
38
, corresponding to the term
c_{\mathcal{M}}
in Eq. (
8
) (when the integral is defined).
In the binary case, Ref.
9
explores the impact of the loop structure of a spin model (
56
) on the geometric complexity.

In summary, equation (
57
) draws a useful connection between the statistical properties of a spin model and algebraic properties of its operators, i.e., the linear dependencies between the operators encoded in the loop structure (
58
). Equation (
57
) describes how these structural dependencies constrain the statistical properties of the model.
In the following section, we introduce an algebraic representation of spin models, which will allow for a better characterization of this connection.

4
Gauge transformations (GTs) and equivalence classes of
q
-state spin models

In the binary case, Ref.
9
observed that all the models that have the same loop structure (
56
) have the same partition function (
55
) up to permutation of their parameters.
They introduce bijective transformations of the spin variables (and thus of the state space) that preserve the structure of spin operators, which they call gauge transformations.
These transformations map a spin model onto another spin model (with different operators), while keeping the loop structure invariant, therefore preserving the partition function. The transformed spin models can be seen as different representations of the same abstract model.
Let us generalize these ideas to
q
-state spin models.

4.1
Gauge transformations as changes of spin representation

Following Ref.
9
,
we use the notion of independence between spin operators (see Definition
11
) to generalize the definition of gauge transformations to systems of
q
-state spin variables with
q>2
.
Ref.
15
also introduced a linear algebra formalism for spin models and gauge transformations, which we generalize to larger values of
q
. Let us start by recalling the definition of independence between spin operators:

Definition 16
.

Independent set of operators.
Consider a system of
n

q
-state spin variables. A set of operators
\{\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}},\,\dots,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{K}}\}
is independent if and only if the set of vectors
\mathcal{S}=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\,\dots,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{K}\}
is linearly independent in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
:

\displaystyle\forall(r_{k})\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{K},\;\;\;\;\sum_{k=1}^{K}r_{k}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}=0\;({\rm mod}\;q)\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad r_{k}=0\;\;\;\;\forall\,k\,.

(85)

This is equivalent to the set of loops of
\mathcal{S}
containing only the null vector:
\mathcal{L}_{q}(\mathcal{S})=\{\boldsymbol{0}\}
.

We recall that spin operators are defined in Eq. (
13
) as products of the spin variables identified by the vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}
. Equation (
85
) thus means that no operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
in the set can be obtained as a product of other operators in the set raised to any power. Moreover, in a system of
n
spin variables, an independent set of operators can have at most
n
operators, because there are at most
n
independent vectors in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
.

Note that
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
is not a vector space when
q
is not prime. However, the definition above in terms of linear independence between elements of
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
still holds in this case, as well as the other definitions and the linear algebra representation discussed in this section (see the notion of
modules
in mathematics
47
).

Definition 17
.

Gauge transformations (GTs).

Consider a system of
n

q
-state spin variables
\boldsymbol{s}=(s_{1},\,\dots,\,s_{n})
.
A gauge transformation
\mathcal{T}
is a change of spin basis identified by an ordered set of
n
independent operators
(\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}},\,\dots,\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}})
, where the new basis variables
\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}=(s_{1}^{\prime},\,\dots,\,s_{n}^{\prime})
are defined as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}:\boldsymbol{s}\rightarrow\begin{cases}s_{1}^{\prime}=\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}(\boldsymbol{s})\\
\;\;\cdots\\
s_{n}^{\prime}=\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}}(\boldsymbol{s})\end{cases}\,.

(86)

The transformation can equivalently be written in terms of the discrete variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\,\dots,\,\alpha_{n})\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
, where each
\alpha_{j}
is the color of the spin
s_{j}
.
Under the gauge transformation
\mathcal{T}
above, the new variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}=(\alpha_{1}^{\prime},\,\dots,\,\alpha_{n}^{\prime})\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
are defined as:

\displaystyle{\rm for}\;{\rm all}\;j\in\{1,\cdots,n\},\qquad\alpha_{j}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}\,,

(87)

where each variable
\alpha_{j}^{\prime}
is the color of the spin
s_{j}^{\prime}
and where the scalar product is taken in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
.
Figure
6
.a gives an example of a gauge transformation for a spin model with
q=3
.

Note that self-dependent operators (operators
\boldsymbol{\mu}
for which there exists a scalar
c\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})
such that
c\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{0}
) cannot contribute to a set of independent operators, and therefore, they cannot be used to define a GT.

Using the definition of spin operators in Eq. (
14
), it is straightforward to check that the new variables
s_{j}^{\prime}
defined by the transformation (
86
) are indeed still
q
-state spin variables (
10
):

\displaystyle s_{j}^{\prime}=\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}}(\boldsymbol{s})=z_{q}^{\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}}=z_{q}^{\,\alpha_{j}^{\prime}}\,,\qquad{\rm where}\;\;\alpha_{j}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}

(88)

are the corresponding colors. This also proves the expression (
87
