) of the GT in terms of the colors.
Because the set of
n
vectors
(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\,\dots,\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n})
are linearly independent, they form a new basis of
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
, and Eq. (
87
) simply re-writes the states of
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
into this new basis.
Hence, GTs written for the variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
correspond to linear basis transformations in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
, which can be identified as the automorphisms of
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
.

Property 18
.

Matrix representation of gauge transformations.

For convenience, the gauge transformation in Eq. (
87
) can be written as the vector-matrix product over
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})

15
:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,\boldsymbol{T}\,,

(89)

where
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\,\dots,\,\alpha_{n})
and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}=(\alpha_{1}^{\prime},\,\dots,\,\alpha_{n}^{\prime})
are two row vectors containing respectively the states of the original variables
\alpha_{j}
and of the new variables
\alpha_{j}^{\prime}
, and
\boldsymbol{T}=(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\,\dots,\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n})
is an
n\times n
-matrix whose columns are the linearly independent vectors
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}
defining the gauge transformation.

When performing the matrix product over
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})
in Eq. (
89
), the elements of the resulting vector are taken modulo
q
.
The
n
columns of
\boldsymbol{T}
being linearly independent in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
means that
\boldsymbol{T}
is invertible in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
.
Multiplying Eq. (
89
) on both side by the inverse matrix
\boldsymbol{T}^{-1}
allows one to recover the original variables from the transformed ones, thus defining the inverse gauge transformation (also confirming that GTs are indeed bijective).

Property 19
.

Inverse gauge transformation.
Gauge transformations (GTs) are bijective. Following a GT, the original variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
can be recovered from the transformed variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}
using:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}\,\boldsymbol{T}^{-1}\,,

(90)

where
\boldsymbol{T}^{-1}
is the inverse of the matrix representation
\boldsymbol{T}
of the GT.

In the following, we will often denote by
\boldsymbol{b}=(\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}},\,\dots,\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}})
the set of
n
independent operators used to define the new basis, by
\mathcal{T}_{b}
the corresponding gauge transformation, and by
\boldsymbol{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}
its matrix representation.
The change of basis will then be denoted by
\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{b}(\boldsymbol{s})
or, equivalently, by
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,\boldsymbol{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}
.
Similarly, we will denote by
\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}^{-1}
the inverse GT, which is represented by the inverse matrix
\boldsymbol{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}^{\,-1}
.
The columns of
\boldsymbol{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}^{\,-1}
identify
n
independent vectors
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}^{\prime}
, which correspond to the operators defining the basis of the inverse GT,
\,\boldsymbol{b}^{-1}=(\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\prime}},\,\dots,\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}^{\prime}})
.

For a system of
n

q
-state spin variables, each choice of gauge transformation
\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{b}(\boldsymbol{s})
can be performed on
the vector of discrete variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
by multiplying it with the corresponding invertible
n\times n
-matrix
\boldsymbol{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}
in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
, as described in Property
18
. Reciprocally, the columns of each invertible
n\times n
-matrix
\boldsymbol{T}
in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
uniquely identify
n
linearly independent vectors
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
,
\boldsymbol{T}=(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\,\dots,\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n})
, and therefore uniquely identify a gauge transformation.
It is then easy to see that GTs, when written in terms of the variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
, are the automorphisms of
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
(i.e., corresponding to all possible invertible
n\times n
-matrices over
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})
).

Property 20
.

Consider a system of
n

q
-state spins characterized by the discrete color variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
. The gauge transformations defined for spin systems in Definition
17

are the automorphisms of the state space of the variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, i.e., they are the automorphisms of the cyclic group
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{q}(n)=Aut[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]\,,

(91)

where
\mathcal{G}_{q}(n)
denotes the set of all gauge transformations.

Property 21
.

Total number of GTs.
It follows that, for
q
prime, the number of possible gauge transformations is:

\displaystyle\textrm{for}\;q\;\textrm{prime},\;\qquad|\mathcal{G}_{q}(n)|=\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}(q^{n}-q^{i})\,.

(92)

For
q
not prime, let’s consider the prime factorization
q=\prod_{k=1}^{K}p_{k}^{\,m_{k}}
, where the
p_{k}
’s are prime numbers and the
m_{k}
’s are their multiplicity. The number of automorphisms of
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
is given by:

\displaystyle\textrm{for}\;q\;\textrm{non prime},\;\qquad|\mathcal{G}_{q}(n)|=\prod_{k=1}^{K}p_{k}^{\,(m_{k}-1)n^{2}}\,\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}(p_{k}^{\,n}-p_{k}^{\,i})\,.

(93)

When
q
is prime, the number of GTs (
92
) is straightforward to compute: after choosing the
i
first independent operators
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i}
of the basis in Eq. (
86
), there are exactly
(q^{n}-q^{i})
possibilities to choose the
(i+1)
-th operator
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i+1}
such that it is independent from the
i
operators already chosen.
This is because the set of
i
independent vectors
\boldsymbol{\mu}
already chosen can generate
q^{i}
different vectors (and thus, different operators) through linear combinations in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
, and
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i+1}
cannot be chosen among them.

If
q
is not prime, then for some operators
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
there exists a strictly positive integer
c<q
such that
c\,\boldsymbol{\mu}=0\,({\rm mod}\,q)
(for example, for
q=4
and
n=2
, the vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(2,2)
verifies that
2\boldsymbol{\mu}=0
).
These operators don’t satisfy the condition of linear independence, and thus cannot be used to construct a set of independent operators.
The total number of GTs in this case is therefore smaller than the number in Eq. (
92
). Computing the precise number (
93
) uses notions from group theory and is provided in App.
C.1
.
For example, for
n=1
spin with
q=4
, there are three possible operators, corresponding to the vectors
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\{(1),(2),(3)\}
, as
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(2)
cannot be used to define a new basis because
2\,\boldsymbol{\mu}=0\,({\rm mod}\,4)
. We thus find that the number of GTs in this simple case is
|\mathcal{G}_{4}(1)|=2
, which is also obtained from Eq. (
93
).

Gauge transformations of a spin system correspond to changes of representation of the spin variables. As a result, a GT induces a transformation of any dataset or spin model expressed in these variables. In the following, we discuss how these transformations affect data and spin models.

4.2
Gauge transformations of data

Consider a dataset composed of multiple observations (datapoints) of the states of a spin system.
For instance, this can be a discrete dataset, composed of observations of the discrete color variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
.
A GT changes the basis in which the spin system is represented and thus induces a transformation of the dataset, which is then observed in a new basis of discrete variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
.
A dataset is therefore transformed under a GT by re-writing each datapoint in this new basis.

This operation can be performed efficiently using the vector-matrix product in Eq. (
89
), where
\boldsymbol{T}
is the matrix representing the GT, and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}
are the discrete vectors representing respectively the original and the new value of a given datapoint.
Defining the data matrices
\boldsymbol{D}
and
\boldsymbol{D}^{\prime}
, whose
i
-th row contains the
i
-th datapoint written respectively in the original and in the new basis, the GT of a dataset can be obtained by the matrix product over
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})
:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{D}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{D}\,\boldsymbol{T}\,.

(94)

Because a gauge transformation corresponds to a change of basis, there is no loss of information between the original and the transformed data; the new dataset is just the old one represented in a different basis.

Under a gauge transformation, each state
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
of the discrete system is mapped to a unique new state
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
. A gauge transformation thus draws a bijection in state space, between the
q^{n}
states of the original variables and the
q^{n}
states of the new variables. As a result, the empirical distribution (i.e., frequency of occurrence of the states) remains unchanged, but the states corresponding to each frequency are now shuffled.

4.3
Gauge transformations of
q
-state spin models

GTs are changes of the basis in which the spin variables are represented. As a result, spin operators, which are functions of the spin variables, are also modified under GTs.
Remarkably, by construction of GTs, the transformation of a spin operator remains a spin operator, and as a result, a spin model is transformed into another spin model, as described in this section.

Definition 18
.

Transformed operator.
A spin operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})
is transformed under a GT
\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}
by rewriting
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})
in terms of the new variables
\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}
, using the inverse GT
\boldsymbol{s}=\boldsymbol{b}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}](\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})\doteq\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\big(\boldsymbol{b}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})\big)\,.

(95)

The resulting transformed operator is still a spin operator and is given by:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}](\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})=\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})\,,\qquad{\rm where}\qquad\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}

(96)

is a matrix-vector multiplication over
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})
,
\boldsymbol{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}^{-1}
is the matrix representation of the inverse gauge transformation
\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{b}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{s})
(see Property
19
), and
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
are two column vectors representing the operator, respectively in the original and in the new basis.

Proof.

We obtain this result by using the matrix representation of the inverse GT
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}^{-1}
in the expression of the operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
written in terms of the colors:

\displaystyle\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=z_{q}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}}=z_{q}^{(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}^{-1})\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}}=z_{q}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}}\doteq\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime})\,,\qquad{\rm where}\;\;\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}\,.

(97)

∎

Transforming a spin model with a gauge transformation
\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}
consists in re-writing the Hamiltonian (energy landscape) of the model in the new basis
\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{b}(\boldsymbol{s})
, mapping the energy of a state
\boldsymbol{s}
to the new state
\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}
.
As the transformation of a spin operator under a GT is also a spin operator, the new Hamiltonian remains that of a spin model, and takes the following form.

Definition 19
.

Transformed model.

Under a gauge transformation
\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}
, the Hamiltonian of a spin model, with operators
\mathcal{M}
and parameters
\boldsymbol{g}
, is rewritten in the new basis
\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{b}(\boldsymbol{s})
. This gives the Hamiltonian of a new spin model
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
with parameters
\boldsymbol{g}^{\prime}
(see App.
C.2
):

\displaystyle H(\boldsymbol{s}\mid\mathcal{M},\,\boldsymbol{g})\overset{\boldsymbol{s}=\boldsymbol{b}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})}{=}H(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\,\boldsymbol{g}^{\prime})\,,

(98)

where the set of operators
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}[\mathcal{M}]
is obtained by transforming each operator of
\mathcal{M}
20
20

20

We use the notation
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
to denote the set of spin operators
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})
of the model and the notation
\mathcal{M}
for the set of their corresponding vector representations
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
.

:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}=\{\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}](\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})\mid\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})\in\tilde{\mathcal{M}}\}\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\{\boldsymbol{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}\mid\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}\}\,,

(99)

and where the vector of parameters
\boldsymbol{g}^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}[\boldsymbol{g}]
is a permutation of the original parameters
\boldsymbol{g}
:

\displaystyle g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}}=g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,,\qquad{with}\qquad\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}=T_{\boldsymbol{b}}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}\,.

(100)

The permutation is such that the transformed operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}}=\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}]
in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is parameterized by the same parameter as the operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
in
\mathcal{M}
.

Because GTs are bijections of the state space, the gauge transformation of a spin model maps the energy of a state
\boldsymbol{s}
to another (unique) state
\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}
, thus drawing a one-to-one mapping in state space that rearranges the energy landscape. In the context of real-valued
q
-state spin models, this yields a permutation of the state probabilities in the model probability distribution, just like the one described for GTs of data.

Figure
6
.a gives an example of a spin model for
q=3
and of a gauge transformation of that model. Observe that the order of the operators is not preserved under GT: the top model has only operators up to order
2
(i.e., operators connecting at most two variables), whereas the transformed model at the bottom has an operator of order
3
(and its c.c.), connecting all three variables. In general, the order of an operator is not preserved under GT. In Sec.
4.6
, we will discuss properties of spin models that remain invariant under GT.

For convenience, the gauge transformation of a spin model can be written as a matrix product. To do so we first introduce a matrix representation of spin models.

Definition 20
.

Matrix representation of spin models.
The matrix representation
\boldsymbol{M}
of a spin model
\mathcal{M}=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\,\dots,\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{K}\}
is the
n\times K
matrix over
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})
, whose
i
-th column is the vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i}
:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{M}=(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\,\dots,\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{K})\,.

(101)

Note that the rank of a spin model (see Definition
14
) is equal to the rank of its matrix representation over
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})
.

Property 22
.

Matrix representation of gauge transformed models.

For convenience, the gauge transformation of a spin model

\mathcal{M}=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\,\dots,\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{K}\}
can be written as a matrix product over
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})
, by extending Eq. (
96
) to multiple operators:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{M}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{T}^{-1}_{\boldsymbol{b}}\,\boldsymbol{M}\,,

(102)

where
\boldsymbol{T}^{-1}_{\boldsymbol{b}}
is the matrix representation of the inverse gauge transformation
\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{b}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{s})
(see Definition
19
), and where
\boldsymbol{M}=(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\,\dots,\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{K})
and
\boldsymbol{M}^{\prime}=(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\prime},\,\dots,\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{K}^{\prime})
are two
n\times K
matrices in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})
, whose columns are the vector representations of the operators, respectively in the original model
\mathcal{M}
and in the new models
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.
Note that the number
K=|\mathcal{M}|
of operators in the original model is preserved under gauge transformation.

The definitions and notations introduced so far for GTs are valid for all
q
-state spin models (with real or complex Hamiltonian). For
q
-state spin models to be probabilistic models, their Hamiltonian must be real, which requires operators to be included by conjugate pairs in the model (see Definition
5
). In the following, we discuss further simplifications in this case.

Figure 6:

a) Example of a gauge transformation between spin models for
q=3
.

Graphical representation of the 3-spin model

\tilde{\mathcal{M}}=\{s_{1},s_{1}^{2},s_{2},s_{2}^{2},s_{1}s_{2},s_{1}^{2}s_{2}^{2},s_{1}s_{3}^{2},s_{1}^{2}s_{3},s_{2}s_{3},s_{2}^{2}s_{3}^{2}\}
(top) and of the model
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}=\{\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3},\sigma_{2}^{2}\sigma_{3}^{2},\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}^{2}\sigma_{3}^{2},\sigma_{1}^{2}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{1}^{2},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{2}^{2},\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}^{2},\sigma_{1}^{2}\sigma_{2}\}
(bottom) obtained from the gauge transformation
\mathcal{T}
of
\mathcal{M}
. Because these models consist of 5 pairs of conjugate operators, we only represented one operator for each pair, choosing arbitrarily

\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{1}=\{s_{1},s_{2},s_{1}s_{2},s_{1}s_{3}^{2},s_{2}s_{3}\}
for
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{1}^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}[\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{1}]=\{\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3},\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}^{2}\sigma_{3}^{2},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}^{2}\}
for
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
.
Variables are indicated by circles labeled from
1
to
3
.
One-body spin operators are represented by colored circles on a single variable, pairwise interactions by lines between two variables, and 3-body interactions by triangles connecting three variables.
The colors indicate the power to which a variable is raised within an interaction: blue for 1 and red for 2.
For example, the operator
s_{1}s_{3}^{2}
is indicated by a bicolor line, blue on the side of
s_{1}
and red on the side of
s_{3}
.
Observe that the top model is pairwise (i.e., it has only operators up to second order), while the bottom model obtained after GT is not.
The matrix forms of the two models and of the GT are given in Eq. (
4.4
) and (
4.4
).

b) Example of the loop structure of a model for
q=3
.
Graphical representation of the loops
\mathcal{L}^{cc}_{q}
of the spin model
\mathcal{M}
in panel a) Top,
excluding trivial loops due to c.c. operators (which are of the type
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}=0
, and their combinations with other loops).
Operators are visualized using the same convention as in panel a). In this example, the model can be expressed as
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{1}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}
, where
\mathcal{M}_{1}
is represented in panel a). In this case, the loop
\boldsymbol{\ell}\in\mathcal{L}^{cc}_{q}(\mathcal{M})
is a vector
\boldsymbol{\ell}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{K_{1}}
such that
\sum_{j=1}^{K_{1}}\ell_{j}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}=\boldsymbol{0}
, where the operators
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}\in\mathcal{M}_{1}
(see Eq. (
68
)).
For each loop
\boldsymbol{\ell}
, we only visualized the operators
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}
for which the coefficient
\ell_{k}
is non-zero, and we indicated with a “
\times 2
” the operators for which
\ell_{k}=2
.
Every spin model also contains the empty loop
\boldsymbol{\ell}=\boldsymbol{0}
, which is not represented here.
All the loops of the model in
\mathcal{L}^{cc}_{q}(\mathcal{M})
can be obtained from two generators (see Sec.
4.5
).
Here, we chose the two loops on the top left as generators, represented by the vectors denoted
\boldsymbol{\ell}_{a}
and
\boldsymbol{\ell}_{b}
; the other loops are obtained by linear combinations of these two vectors.
The two-dimensional coordinate vector below each loop diagram indicates which combination of
\boldsymbol{\ell}_{a}
and
\boldsymbol{\ell}_{b}
produced the loop.
For instance, the last loop of the first row corresponds to
\boldsymbol{\ell}_{a}+2\boldsymbol{\ell}_{b}
.
We organized the diagrams such that
each loop in the second row corresponds to the complex conjugate of the loop directly above it (notice that their coordinate vectors are conjugate for
q=3
).

4.5
Algebraic representation of
q
-state spin models

As hinted at by the matrix representations introduced above, spin models can be studied from a linear algebra perspective. In this section, we formalize this framework by introducing a linear map representation of spin models.

Definition 22
.

Linear map representation of spin models.

Consider a system of
n
discrete variables
(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
and a
q
-state spin model
\mathcal{M}
for this system, specified by the choice of
K
operators,
\mathcal{M}=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{K}\}\subseteq[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]^{*}
.
The model
\mathcal{M}
can be represented as a linear map
f_{\mathcal{M}}
from
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{K}
to
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
which defines each operator of the model in terms of the state variables.
We denote
(\boldsymbol{u}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{u}_{K})
a basis of the domain
\mathcal{U}=(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{K}
, which can be thought of as the model’s space;
we associate each of these basis elements with one of the
K
operators of the model
\mathcal{M}
.
We denote
(\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{v}_{n})
a basis of the codomain
\mathcal{V}=(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
, which can be thought of as the space of the spin system, and associate each of these basis elements with one of the
n
discrete (color) variables of the system.
The linear map
f_{\mathcal{M}}
is defined by specifying the following image for each basis element of
\mathcal{U}
:

\displaystyle\textrm{for all }k\in\{1,\dots,K\}\,,\qquad\qquad f_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{k})=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mu_{k}^{(j)}\,\boldsymbol{v}_{j}=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}\,,\qquad\qquad

(103)

where the coefficient
\mu_{k}^{(j)}
corresponds to the
j
-th element of the vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
defining the
k
-th operator of
\mathcal{M}
. Here the vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}=(\mu_{k}^{(1)},\dots,\mu_{k}^{(n)})
is considered as an element of the codomain.

Following this approach, the matrix representation of the linear map
f_{\mathcal{M}}
defined in Eq. (
103
) corresponds precisely to the
n\times K
-matrix
\boldsymbol{M}
introduced in Definition
20
as the matrix representation of the spin model
\mathcal{M}
.
Note that
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})
is a vector space when
q
is prime, it is a module otherwise. The linear map representation is valid in both cases.
For example, the model
\mathcal{M}
shown in Fig.
6
.a top, with
K=10
operators over
n=3
spins, corresponds to the following linear map:
f_{\mathcal{M}}:(\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z})^{10}\to(\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z})^{3}
,

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\;f_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1})=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}=\boldsymbol{v}_{1}\,;\qquad&f_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{2})=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}=2\boldsymbol{v}_{1}\\
\;f_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{3})=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{3}=\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\,;\qquad&f_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{4})=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{4}=2\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\\
\;f_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{5})=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{5}=\boldsymbol{v}_{1}+\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\,;\qquad&f_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{6})=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{6}=2\boldsymbol{v}_{1}+2\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\\
\;f_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{7})=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{7}=\boldsymbol{v}_{1}+2\boldsymbol{v}_{3}\,;\;\qquad&f_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{8})=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{8}=2\boldsymbol{v}_{1}+\boldsymbol{v}_{3}\\
\;f_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{9})=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{9}=\boldsymbol{v}_{2}+\boldsymbol{v}_{3}\,;\qquad\qquad&f_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{10})=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{10}=2\boldsymbol{v}_{2}+2\boldsymbol{v}_{3}\end{cases}\,,

(104)

whose matrix representation is the same as the matrix representation
\boldsymbol{M}
of the spin model given in Eq. (
4.4
) (we labeled the operators in the same way in Eq. (
104
) and (
4.4
)).

Figure
7
gives an illustration of the linear map representation of
q
-state spin models.
The state of the system is characterized by the state of the
n
discrete random variables
\alpha_{j}
(colors of the spins) and is represented in the system space
\mathcal{V}
by the random vector
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}\boldsymbol{v}_{j}
, corresponding to the coordinate vector
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n})
.
The state of the model is characterized by the state of the
K
operators of the model. We denote by
m_{k}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})
the color of the
k
-th spin operator (the state of this operator is thus
z_{q}^{m_{k}}
).
With this notation, the state of the model is then described by the
K
discrete random variables
m_{k}
and is represented in the model space
\mathcal{U}
by the random vector
\boldsymbol{m}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}m_{k}\boldsymbol{u}_{k}=(m_{1},\cdots,m_{K})
.
Using the linear map
f_{\mathcal{M}}
, each operator
\boldsymbol{u}_{k}\in\mathcal{U}
of the model
\mathcal{M}
is given a representation
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}\in\mathcal{V}
in the system space.
The state
m_{k}
of the
k
-th operator when the system is in a state
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
is then given by the dot product
m_{k}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}
in
\mathcal{V}
(because
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=z_{q}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}}=z_{q}^{m_{k}}
).
We can thus obtain the state of the model
\boldsymbol{m}\in\mathcal{U}
when the system is in the state
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\mathcal{V}
by the vector-matrix product
\boldsymbol{m}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,\boldsymbol{M}
over
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})
, where
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
is considered as a row vector and
\boldsymbol{M}
is the matrix representation of
f_{\mathcal{M}}
.

In an
n
-spin system, all the statistical properties of the system are captured by the ensemble averages of each the
(q^{n}-1)
spin operators (i.e., all the high-order moments).
In this framework, each spin operator whose statistics is constrained by the model has two representations: one in the system space
\mathcal{V}
and one in the model space
\mathcal{U}
.

In the system space
, any spin operator can be written as a product of spin variables (monomials). This product corresponds to a linear combination of the color variables
\alpha_{i}
, which is represented by a vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{V}
. The random variable
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})
, obtained from the dot product of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
with the random state vector
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, then characterizes the state of the operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=z_{q}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}}
when the system is in the state
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
.

In the model space
, any spin operator that can be written as a product of operators of the model (monomials over the operators) is represented as a vector
\boldsymbol{u}=\sum_{k}u_{k}\boldsymbol{u}_{k}=(u_{1},\dots,u_{K})\in\mathcal{U}
.
For example, the vector
\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+2\boldsymbol{u}_{2}\in\mathcal{U}
represents the spin operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}(\boldsymbol{m})=\phi^{\boldsymbol{u_{1}}}(\boldsymbol{m})[\phi^{\boldsymbol{u_{2}}}(\boldsymbol{m})]^{2}
, where the operators
\phi^{\boldsymbol{u_{k}}}(\boldsymbol{m})
are the operators of the model. The random variable
\boldsymbol{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})
, obtained from the dot product of
\boldsymbol{u}
with the random state vector
\boldsymbol{m}
, then characterizes the state of the operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}(\boldsymbol{m})=z_{q}^{\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{m}}
when the model is in the state
\boldsymbol{m}
.

For a chosen model
\mathcal{M}
, the linear map
f_{\mathcal{M}}
associates each operator represented by
\boldsymbol{u}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}u_{k}\boldsymbol{u}_{k}\in\mathcal{U}
in model space to a representation
\boldsymbol{\mu}=f_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{u})=\sum_{k=1}^{K}u_{k}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}\in\mathcal{V}
in the system space.
The state of the spin operator can then be equivalently written in model space as
\boldsymbol{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}=\sum_{k}m_{k}u_{k}
and in system space as
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}=\sum_{k}u_{k}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}=\sum_{k}u_{k}m_{k}
(i.e.,
\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}}(\boldsymbol{m})=\phi^{f_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{u})}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
).

If the rank of the model is smaller than
n
, then not all spin operators in
\mathcal{V}
have a representation in the model space
\mathcal{U}
. The operators that can be decomposed in
\mathcal{U}
are those whose statistical properties are controlled by the statistics of the operators of the model (namely the relevant statistics). The other operators are given a uniform distribution in the model
22
22

22

For example, for the model
\mathcal{M}=\{\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}(\boldsymbol{s})=s_{1},\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{s})=s_{1}s_{2}s_{3}\}
with
q=2
, the spin operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})=s_{2}
cannot be obtained as a product of operators of the model, but the operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})=s_{2}s_{3}
can.

.
Different operators
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{U}
can be mapped by
f_{\mathcal{M}}
to the same operator
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{V}
, which means that the same spin operator (expressed in terms of the system’s variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
) can be decomposed in different ways over the operators of the model
23
23

23

For example, for the model
\mathcal{M}=\{\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}(\boldsymbol{s})=s_{1},\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{s})=s_{2},\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{3}}(\boldsymbol{s})=s_{3},\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{4}}(\boldsymbol{s})=s_{1}s_{2}s_{3}\}
with
q=2
, the operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})=s_{1}s_{2}
can be decomposed as
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})=\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}(\boldsymbol{s})\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{s})
or as
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})=\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{3}}(\boldsymbol{s})\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{4}}(\boldsymbol{s})
.
.
Finally, the operators
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{U}
that are mapped to
\boldsymbol{0}\in\mathcal{V}
correspond to the loops of the model (because they identify products of operators of the model that are equal to identity in the system space).

Gauge transformations are changes of basis of the state space of the system, which, in this framework, correspond to automorphisms of the codomain
\mathcal{V}=(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
(see Fig.
7
). These automorphisms can be represented by invertible
n\times n
-matrices over
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})
and the GT of a model is then obtained by the matrix product previously described in property
22
.

Definition 23
.

Gauge transformations.
GTs are automorphisms of the codomain of the linear map
f_{\mathcal{M}}
representing the spin model
\mathcal{M}
.

Figure 7:

Illustration: Representation of
q
-state spin models as linear maps.

We consider a system with
n

q
-state spin variables and a spin model
\mathcal{M}
with
K
operators.
The space
\mathcal{V}=(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
(on the right) is the system space, in which each of the
n
basis elements is associated with one of the spin variables.
The state of the system is then represented by the random vector
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})\in\mathcal{V}
, where
\alpha_{j}
is the color of the
j
-th spin, and a spin operator is represented as a vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{V}
, which gives the decomposition of the operator in terms of the variables of the system.
The space
\mathcal{U}=(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{K}
(on the left) is the model space, in which each of the
K
basis elements is associated with one of the operators of the model (which are the variables of the models).
The state of the model is then represented by the random vector
\boldsymbol{m}=(m_{1},\dots,m_{K})\in\mathcal{U}
, where
m_{k}
is the color of the
k
-th operator,
\phi^{\boldsymbol{u}_{k}}(\boldsymbol{m})=z_{q}^{m_{k}}
, and each spin operator that can be decomposed over the operators of the model is represented by a vector
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{U}
.
A model
\mathcal{M}
is represented by a linear map
f_{\mathcal{M}}
that associates each operator represented by
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{U}
in model space to a representation
\boldsymbol{\mu}=f_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{u})\in\mathcal{V}
in system space. The map
f_{\mathcal{M}}
is defined in Eq. (
103
) by specifying the image
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}=f_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{k})\in\mathcal{V}
of each basis vector
\boldsymbol{u}_{k}\in\mathcal{U}
.
Gauge transformations
\mathcal{T}
are automorphisms of
\mathcal{V}
. Loops of
\mathcal{M}
correspond to the vectors
\boldsymbol{\ell}\in\mathcal{U}
whose image is the null vector
f_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{\ell})=\boldsymbol{0}\in\mathcal{V}
.

Using this framework, we can identify several properties of linear maps that are useful for characterizing spin models, such as the rank of the map and the properties of its kernel.
The notion of rank of a spin model
\mathcal{M}
was already introduced in Definition
14
as the cardinality of the largest linearly independent set of operators in
\mathcal{M}
. This is the same as the rank of the matrix representation of
\mathcal{M}
, and therefore it coincides with the rank of the linear map associated with
\mathcal{M}
.
The kernel of a linear map is the set of all the vectors in the domain that are mapped to the zero vector in the codomain (as illustrated by the vector
\boldsymbol{\ell}
in Fig.
7
).
For example,
the vector
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\boldsymbol{u}_{3}+2\boldsymbol{u}_{5}
is in the kernel of the map
f_{\mathcal{M}}
defined in Eq. (
104
), as its image is the zero vector:

\displaystyle f_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\boldsymbol{u}_{3}+2\boldsymbol{u}_{5})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{3}+2\boldsymbol{\mu}_{5}=\boldsymbol{v}_{1}+\boldsymbol{v}_{2}+2(\boldsymbol{v}_{1}+\boldsymbol{v}_{2})=\boldsymbol{0}\,.

(105)

Here, we can recognize one of the loops of
\mathcal{M}
, which is the first loop represented in Fig.
6
.b (top left loop). We recall that a loop of a model with operators
\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}\}_{1\leq k\leq K}
is a vector
\boldsymbol{\ell}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{K}
that verifies that
\sum_{k=1}^{K}\ell_{k}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}=\boldsymbol{0}
, where
\ell_{k}
denotes the
k
-th coefficient of
\boldsymbol{\ell}
(see Eq. (
58
)). Labeling the operators in the same order as in Eq. (
104
), the loop above can be represented as the coordinate vector
\boldsymbol{\ell}=\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\boldsymbol{u}_{3}+2\boldsymbol{u}_{5}=(1,0,1,0,2,0,0,0,0,0)
in
\mathcal{U}
.
More generally, the loops of a model
\mathcal{M}
are precisely the elements of the kernel of
f_{\mathcal{M}}
; they identify dependence relations in
\mathcal{V}
between the operators
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}
of
\mathcal{M}
, i.e., between the images of the basis elements
\boldsymbol{u}_{k}
. Let’s provide a more formal proof of this last point.

Proof.

Consider a spin model
\mathcal{M}=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{K}\}\subseteq[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]^{*}
with
K
operators. We recall that the set of loops of
\mathcal{M}
is defined in Eq. (
58
) as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{q}(\mathcal{M})=\bigg\{\boldsymbol{\ell}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{K}\;\Big|\;\sum_{k=1}^{K}\ell_{k}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}=\boldsymbol{0}\bigg\}\,,

(106)

where
\ell_{k}
denotes the
k
-th coefficient of
\boldsymbol{\ell}
.
Using the matrix representation of the model
\boldsymbol{M}=(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{K})
, in which the vectors
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}
are the columns of the matrix (see Definition
20
),
and viewing
\boldsymbol{\ell}
as a column vector,
we can rewrite
\mathcal{L}_{q}(\mathcal{M})
in the following form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{q}(\mathcal{M})=\Big\{\boldsymbol{\ell}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{K}\;\Big|\;\boldsymbol{M}\,\boldsymbol{\ell}=\boldsymbol{0}\Big\}=\ker(\boldsymbol{M})\;.

(107)

Because
\boldsymbol{M}
is also the matrix representation of the linear map
f_{\mathcal{M}}
associated with the model
\mathcal{M}
, Eq. (
107
) above means that the set of loops of
\mathcal{M}
corresponds to the kernel of
f_{\mathcal{M}}
. ∎

Property 25
.

Spin models
\mathcal{M}
inherit properties of their linear map representation
f_{\mathcal{M}}
in Def.
22
:

–

the matrix representation of a model in Definition
20
is the matrix representation of the map;

–

the rank of a model in Definition
14
is the rank of the map,
\rank(\M)=\rank(f_{\M})
;

–

the set of loops of a model defined in Eq. (
58
) is the kernel of the map,
\mathcal{L}_{q}(\mathcal{M})=\ker(f_{\mathcal{M}})
.

Figure
6
.b shows the loops of the spin model
\mathcal{M}
taken as an example above. In this model, operators are included by conjugate pairs, just like for real-valued spin models (see Definition
5
). For clarity in Fig.
6
, we only displayed the elements of the reduced set of loops
\mathcal{L}^{cc}_{q}(\mathcal{M})
defined for real-valued spin models in Eq. (
68
); in short, we didn’t represent trivial loops resulting from complex conjugate operators (of the type
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i}+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}=\boldsymbol{0}
) nor their combinations with other loops.
For example, the first loop in the top left of the figure corresponds to
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{3}+2\boldsymbol{\mu}_{5}=\boldsymbol{v}_{1}+\boldsymbol{v}_{2}+2(\boldsymbol{v}_{1}+\boldsymbol{v}_{2})=\boldsymbol{0}
in
(\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z})^{n}
.
Considering all linear combinations of this loop with the trivial loop
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}=\boldsymbol{0}
from the c.c. operators
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}
, we also get the following loops:

2\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{3}+2\boldsymbol{\mu}_{5}=\boldsymbol{0}
,
2\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{3}+2\boldsymbol{\mu}_{5}=\boldsymbol{0}
, and
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}+2\boldsymbol{\mu}_{3}+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{5}=\boldsymbol{0}
, which are not represented in Fig.
6
.b.
Note that the choice of the loops in
\mathcal{L}^{cc}_{q}(\mathcal{M})
is not unique, but it is fully determined by the choice of the operators in the reduced model representation
\mathcal{M}_{r}
.

The reduced loop structure
\mathcal{L}_{q}^{cc}(\mathcal{M})
of a real-valued spin model can be also related to the kernel of the model’s reduced representation (see Definition
).
More precisely, for real-valued spin models that can be written under the form
24
24

24

This is always the case when
q
is prime and larger than
2
.

\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{1}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}
with the decomposition of Definition
10

(i.e., for which
\mathcal{M}_{0}=\emptyset
– such as the model taken as an example above), the reduced set of loops of
\mathcal{M}
, excluding trivial loops due to c.c. operators, is equal to the kernel of its reduced matrix representation
\boldsymbol{M}_{1}
:
\mathcal{L}_{q}^{cc}(\mathcal{M})=\mathcal{L}_{q}(\mathcal{M}_{1})=\ker(\boldsymbol{M}_{1})
, where

is a matrix whose columns are the operators in
\mathcal{M}_{1}
(see proof in App.
C.3
).

Finally, because
\mathcal{L}_{q}(\mathcal{M})=\ker(f_{\mathcal{M}})
, the loop structure of a spin model inherits the properties of kernels of linear maps.
In particular if
q
is prime, the kernel of
f_{\mathcal{M}}
is a vector subspace of
\mathcal{U}=(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{K}
of dimension
\dim(\ker(f_{\mathcal{M}}))=K-\rank(f_{\M})
, where
K=|\mathcal{M}|
; we denote this dimension
\lambda
.
Consequently, all the elements of
\ker(f_{\mathcal{M}})
can be generated by a set of
\lambda
linearly independent vectors in
\ker(f_{\mathcal{M}})
, and the cardinality of
\ker(f_{\mathcal{M}})
is then
q^{\lambda}
.
This generalizes to
q>2
and prime the result found in the binary case
9
that the set of loops of a binary spin model has the structure of an Abelian group generated by
\lambda
independent loops, where
\lambda=K-\rank(\M)
with
K=|\mathcal{M}|
.

Property 26
.

For
q
prime, the set of loops
\mathcal{L}_{q}(\mathcal{M})
of a spin model
\mathcal{M}
with

.

For real-valued spin models with

is the reduce representation

.
Similarly, the reduced loop structure

.
For example, the model

(including the empty loop and excluding trivial loops due to c.c. operators). They are all generated by two independent loops, because

.
Indexing the operators of

in the same order as in Eq. (

), the two top left loops in the first row of Fig.

.b correspond to the two vectors

\boldsymbol{\ell}_{a}=(1,1,2,0,0)
and
\boldsymbol{\ell}_{b}=(0,0,1,2,2)

in

reduced to the

shown below each loop diagram in the figure. For example, in the first row, the third loop corresponds to

and the fourth to

.

In summary, the algebraic representation of
q
-state spin models in terms of linear maps not only makes it easier to manipulate spin models (via vector-matrix operations), it also allows for a better characterization of their properties.
We saw in Sec.
3
that the loop expansion of the partition function
Z_{\mathcal{M}}
of spin models makes a connection between the statistical properties of a model (captured by
Z_{\mathcal{M}}
) and its algebraic properties corresponding to linear dependencies between its operators (captured by the loop structure). The framework introduced here formalizes the latter as the kernel of the linear map representing the model, thus providing a formal algebraic characterization of the statistical properties of
q
-state spin models.

4.6
Equivalence classes of spin models, and their statistical and algebraic properties

In the binary case (
q=2
), Ref.
9
introduces the notion of equivalence classes of spin models, as classes of models related by GTs.
They found that these models have the same partition function and thus define the same statistical structure, only observed in different bases. In other words, models of the same class can be seen as different representations of the same abstract statistical model. In particular, they share common statistical and algebraic properties: they have the same loop structure, partition function, and model information-theoretic complexity.
Ref.
9
calls these classes “complexity classes”. Let us discuss these results in the more general context of
q
-state spin models with
q\geq 2
.

Consider a real-valued
q
-state spin model
\mathcal{M}
. A gauge transformation
\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}
of
\mathcal{M}
is a bijection of the state space that rearranges the energy landscape of the model, assigning the energy of each state
\boldsymbol{s}
in
\mathcal{M}
to another (unique) state
\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{b}(\boldsymbol{s})
in the transformed model
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}[\mathcal{M}]
, as described in Definition
19
. As a result, the probability
25
25

25

We recall that for real-valued spin models, the probability to observe a state
\boldsymbol{s}
is proportional to exponential minus the energy
H(\boldsymbol{s})
of the state:
p(\boldsymbol{s})\propto\exp(-H(\spin))
.

of finding the system in the state
\boldsymbol{s}
in the model
\mathcal{M}
is equal to the probability of finding the system in the transformed state
\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{b}(\boldsymbol{s})
in the transformed model
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}[\mathcal{M}]
:

\displaystyle p(\boldsymbol{s}\,|\,\mathcal{M},\boldsymbol{g})=p(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}\,|\,\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{g}^{\prime})\,.

(108)

Here, the vector of parameters
\boldsymbol{g}^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}[\boldsymbol{g}]
is a permutation of the parameters
\boldsymbol{g}
of
\mathcal{M}
such that each transformed operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}}=\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}]
in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is parameterized by the same parameter as the operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
in
\mathcal{M}
(see definition
19
).
In equation (
108
), the reassignment in state space created by the GT shuffles the state probabilities in the model, but preserves the overall shape of the distribution.
In other words, Eq. (
108
) expresses that the probability distributions of model
\mathcal{M}
and of its gauge transformation
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
are the same up to permutation of the states.

More precisely, one can verify that the transformed spin model
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
has the same statistical properties as the original model
\mathcal{M}
by comparing their partition functions.
The partition function (
3
) of a spin model is obtained by summing, over all possible states
\boldsymbol{s}
of the spin system, a term that depends only on the energy of the state (exponential of minus the energy of the state).
This sum thus remains unchanged under the one-to-one state reordering of the energy landscape created by a GT (see proof in App.
C.2
, which is also valid for spin models with complex-valued Hamiltonian and partition function).
In other words, just like in the binary case
9
,
q
-state spin models related by GTs have the same partition function. Because the partition function encapsulates all the statistical properties of a spin model, this means that models related by GTs describe the same statistical structure, only represented in different bases.

From a statistical modeling perspective, the Fisher information matrix (when it is defined) is in general the mathematical object that captures all the statistical properties of a parametric statistical model (not just models that have a partition function, such as spin models). In particular, in the context of information geometry, it can be used as a metric for the model manifold to study the statistical properties of the model
42
;
3
. For spin models, the Fisher information matrix is given by the Hessian of the log-partition function:

\displaystyle[\,\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})\,]_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu}}=-\left\langle\partial_{g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\partial_{g_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}\log p(\boldsymbol{s}\,|\,\mathcal{M},\,\boldsymbol{g})\right\rangle_{p(\boldsymbol{s}|\boldsymbol{g},\mathcal{M})}=\partial_{g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\partial_{g_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}\log Z_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})\,,

(109)

and is therefore identical for models related by GTs (assuming the parameters are labeled consistently between the models, as explained for Eq. (
108
)).
This means that spin models related by GTs correspond to the same statistical manifold (up to permutation of the parameters).
Therefore, also from this geometrical perspective, models related by GTs are just different representations of the same abstract statistical model.

Property 27
.

Statistical properties of spin models invariant under GTs.

q
-state spin models related by gauge transformations have the same partition function (for a model
\mathcal{M}
and GT
\mathcal{T}
,
Z_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})=Z_{\mathcal{T}[\mathcal{M}]}(\boldsymbol{g})
).
For real-valued spin models, which can be used as probabilistic models in the context of statistical inference, this implies that they have the same Fisher information matrix. In other words, they describe the same statistical structure represented in different spin bases.

More generally, the invariance of the partition function under gauge transformations is valid for all
q
-state spin models (not just real-valued models).
This is straightforward to see
using the loop expansion of the partition function in Sec.
3
and the linear algebra framework introduced previously, in which a spin model
\mathcal{M}
is associated to a linear map
f_{\mathcal{M}}
.
Based on the loop expansion (
57
),
the partition of a spin model is fully specified by its number of operators (each parameterized by a different parameter) and its loop structure.
The number of operators of a model is invariant under GT (by definition
19
),
while the loop structure has been identified as the kernel of the linear map,
\mathcal{L}_{q}(\mathcal{M})=\ker(f_{\mathcal{M}})
.
Because a GT is an automorphism of the co-domain of
f_{\mathcal{M}}
(change of basis of the space of state variables – see Fig.
7
), it has no effect on the kernel of
f_{\mathcal{M}}
. This can be written down explicitly using the matrix representations
\boldsymbol{M}
of
f_{\mathcal{M}}
(and of the model
\mathcal{M}
) and
\boldsymbol{T}
of a given GT (which is an
n\times n
invertible matrix in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})
). The transformed model is represented by the matrix
\boldsymbol{M}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{T}^{-1}\boldsymbol{M}
and its loop structure is given by:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{q}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime})=\Big\{\boldsymbol{\ell}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{K}\;\Big|\;\boldsymbol{T}^{-1}\boldsymbol{M}\,\boldsymbol{\ell}=\boldsymbol{0}\Big\}=\Big\{\boldsymbol{\ell}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{K}\;\Big|\;\boldsymbol{M}\,\boldsymbol{\ell}=\boldsymbol{T}\,\boldsymbol{0}\Big\}=\mathcal{L}_{q}(\mathcal{M})\;,

where we multiplied by the matrix
\boldsymbol{T}
on both sides of the equation defining the set.
In other words, a spin model and its gauge transformation have the same loop structure, and therefore we recover that they have the same partition function. In doing so, we also identify an algebraic property of spin models that is invariant under GT, namely their loop structure.
Moreover, the rank and the dimension of a spin model, which are respectively the cardinality of the maximal set of independent operators within the model, and the cardinality of the minimal independent set of operators that can generate all the operators of the model,
are also invariant under GT.

Property 28
.

Algebraic properties of spin models invariant under GTs.

The number of operators, the rank, the dimension, and the loop structure
\mathcal{L}_{q}
of a
q
-state spin model are invariant under gauge transformations. For any
q
-state spin model
\mathcal{M}
and gauge transformation
\mathcal{T}
,
|\mathcal{T}[\mathcal{M}]|=|\mathcal{M}|
,
\rank(\T[\M])=\rank(\M)
, and
\mathcal{L}_{q}(\mathcal{T}[\mathcal{M}])=\mathcal{L}_{q}(\mathcal{M})
.

For example, take the top left loop of model
\mathcal{M}
in Fig.
6
.b;
this loop corresponds to the vector
\boldsymbol{\ell}=\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\boldsymbol{u}_{3}+2\boldsymbol{u}_{5}
, whose image by
f_{\mathcal{M}}
is:

\displaystyle f_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\boldsymbol{u}_{3}+2\boldsymbol{u}_{5})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{3}+2\boldsymbol{\mu}_{5}=\boldsymbol{v}_{1}+\boldsymbol{v}_{2}+2(\boldsymbol{v}_{1}+\boldsymbol{v}_{2})=\boldsymbol{0}\,,

(110)

where the sums on the right-hand side are taken in
(\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z})^{n}
.
For the transformed model
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}[\mathcal{M}]
, the image of the same vector
\boldsymbol{\ell}
by the linear map
f_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}
associated with
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is also the null vector:

\displaystyle f_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\boldsymbol{u}_{3}+2\boldsymbol{u}_{5})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{3}^{\prime}+2\boldsymbol{\mu}_{5}^{\prime}=(\boldsymbol{v}_{2}^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{v}_{3}^{\prime})+(\boldsymbol{v}_{1}^{\prime}+2\boldsymbol{v}_{2}^{\prime}+2\boldsymbol{v}_{3}^{\prime})+2\boldsymbol{v}_{1}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{0}\,,

(111)

where the vectors
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i}^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}[\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i}]
are given in Eq. (
4.4
).
The representation of the operators has changed from
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}
to
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}^{\prime}
, but not the dependence relations between them. In particular,
\boldsymbol{\ell}
, which was a loop of
\mathcal{M}
, remains a loop of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.

Like in the binary case
9
, GTs define an equivalence relation between
q
-state spin models (one can easily check for reflexivity, symmetry, transitivity
26
26

26

Reflexivity: a spin model is a GT of itself, where the GT is the identity. Symmetry: consider a model
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
obtained by the GT
\mathcal{T}
of another model
\mathcal{M}
, then
\mathcal{M}
is also the gauge transformation of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
using
\mathcal{T}^{-1}
. Transitivity: consider two GTs
\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
\mathcal{T}_{2}
, and the transformed model
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}_{1}[\mathcal{M}]
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}=\mathcal{T}_{2}[\mathcal{M}^{\prime}]
, then
\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}=\mathcal{T}[\mathcal{M}]
where
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{2}[\mathcal{T}_{1}]
is also a GT resulting from the composition of
\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
\mathcal{T}_{2}
.
).
This equivalence relation divides the (superexponential) space of the
2^{q^{n}-1}
spin models into equivalence classes of models that share common statistical and algebraic properties.
In section
5
, we will focus on the subset of classes corresponding to minimally complex models
15
.

Property 29
.

Equivalence classes of
q
-state spin models and their properties.
Gauge transformations define an equivalence relation between
q
-state spin models, dividing the space of models into equivalence classes. Models of the same class are different representations of the same abstract statistical model. Each class is thus characterized by the statistical and algebraic invariants given in Property
27
and
28
.

4.7
Discussion: equivariant modeling and learning symmetries of data under gauge transformations

The gauge transformation of a discrete dataset simply re-writes the data in a different basis, without any loss or added information or noise. Ideally, a perfect model selection procedure would be able to find models that are consistent for the original data and its transformed version, as illustrated in Figure
1
in the introduction.
In other words, this ideal model selection would be equivariant under GTs. Such approach is theoretically possible if we select among all
q
-state spin models, because this family of models is invariant under GTs (for any given
q
).
However, brute force selection of the best model among all is impossible in practice, due to the huge set of models (super-exponential in the number of spins). In Section
5
, we extend to discrete data the work of Ref.
15
on minimally complex models, which are a sub-family of spin models within which one can perform equivariant model selection in practice.

More generally, if one wants to tackle model selection among all
q
-state spin models, it is important to find principled ways to reduce the number of parameters and the set of possible models for a given dataset.
One approach is to focus on modeling symmetries of the data, instead of specific correlation patterns.
If a dataset is invariant (up to noise) under certain bijective transformations, then a good model should encode that invariance, i.e., it should be invariant under the same transformation.
This reduces the set of candidate models. In other words, we simplify the problem of identifying relevant correlation patterns of the data by searching first for relevant symmetries of the data.
Indeed, it is in general difficult to identify in a robust manner the set of the most relevant (high-order) correlation patterns of a dataset, because these patterns are typically not independent from each other and, as a result,
many combinations of possible relevant patterns
can explain the data similarly
27
27

27

This problem is in fact equivalent to identifying the best spin model for the data, by definition of maximum entropy models
25
.
.
In contrast, one can treat symmetries of the data independently from each other, and each newly uncovered symmetry further reduces the pool of possible candidate models.
In the following, we formalize this idea based on the notion of invariance under GTs.

As discussed in Sec.
4.2
, applying a GT to a dataset permutes the probability of occurrence of the states in the empirical distribution. However, certain GTs can leave the empirical distribution fully invariant, by only permuting states that have the same probability. Such invariance can be interpreted as a symmetry of the data under GT.
The definition of GTs thus allows us to formalize the notion of generalized symmetries in discrete data, as the invariance of the empirical probability distribution under GT. These symmetries go beyond ordinary symmetries, such as translational and reflection symmetries for spatially organized variables, or symmetries under permutations of spin variables.

Definition 24
.

Generalized symmetry of (stationary) discrete data.

We call symmetry of a discrete dataset the invariance of its empirical distribution under a gauge transformation.

For
q
-state spin models, gauge transformations are changes of representation of the models.
They transform a model’s architecture (i.e., which operators are in the model), while preserving its statistical and algebraic properties (e.g., partition function and loop structure).
However, certain GTs can leave a given model architecture fully invariant, by transforming each operator of the model into a spin operator that is already in the model.
In other words, a model
\mathcal{M}
and its transformation
\mathcal{T}[\mathcal{M}]
have the same set of operators and thus correspond to the same parametric model.
For example, take Bob’s model in the introduction Figure
1
. The permutation of the spins
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
is a GT that leaves the model’s architecture invariant. Equivalently, for Alice’s model, the GT
(\sigma_{1}^{\prime},\sigma_{2}^{\prime},\sigma_{3}^{\prime})=(\sigma_{1},\,\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3},\,\sigma_{3})
leaves the model invariant (as it permutes the field operator
\sigma_{2}
with the three-body operator
\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}
).
We call such invariance of the model architecture under GT a symmetry of the spin model.

Contrary to the definition
24
of symmetry of data, the symmetry of a model under GT doesn’t necessarily leave the model probability distribution invariant, because the parameters associated with the permuted operators are then also permuted. For the model probability distribution to remain invariant for any value of the parameters, the parameters of the permuted operators must be equal (degenerate parameters).

Definition 25
.

Generalized symmetry of
q
-state spin models.

We call symmetry of a spin model the invariance of its architecture (which operators are in the model) under a gauge transformation.
We call “strong” symmetry of a spin model the invariance of the model probability distribution under a GT.
Such invariance requires both an invariance of the model architecture and the degeneracy of the model parameters associated with the operators permuted by the GT.

We can thus exploit generalized symmetries of a discrete dataset to reduce the pool of candidate spin models to those that have the same symmetries.
An invariance of the data under a GT should be reflected in the candidate models as a strong symmetry,
which imposes a degeneracy of the parameters associated with the operators permuted by the GT
28
28

28

For example, take a binary dataset with
3
spins that is invariant under the GT characterized by the inverse transformation
\boldsymbol{T}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
1&1&0\\
0&1&1\end{pmatrix}
. Then the operators
(100),(110),(101),(111)
should be given the same parameters as well as the operators
(011)
and
(010)
for the model to be strongly symmetric under the same transformation (this can be checked by multiplying the matrix
\boldsymbol{T}^{-1}
with each of these operators as in Eq. (
96
)).

: they must either all be absent from the model or all have the same parameter. The model selection procedure should then decide if this degenerate parameter is relevant or if these operators should be all be removed from the model
29
29

29

Note that a symmetry under GT will not reduce the set of models to a specific subset of equivalence classes, but instead it will invalidate candidate models from all classes. A symmetry under a given GT is indeed representation-dependent.
For each transformation under which a model
\boldsymbol{M}
is invariant, there is a corresponding transformation under which another model of the same class is invariant. Consider a transformation
\boldsymbol{T}_{1}
under which
\boldsymbol{M}_{1}
is invariant:
\boldsymbol{T}_{1}\boldsymbol{M}_{1}=\boldsymbol{M}_{1}
. Consider another model
\boldsymbol{M}_{2}
of the same class: there exists a GT
\boldsymbol{T}
such that
\boldsymbol{M}_{2}=\boldsymbol{T}\boldsymbol{M}_{1}
. The model
\boldsymbol{M}_{2}
is then invariant under the GT
\boldsymbol{T}_{2}=\boldsymbol{T}\boldsymbol{T}_{1}\boldsymbol{T}^{-1}
, as it satisfies that
\boldsymbol{T}_{2}\boldsymbol{M}_{2}=\boldsymbol{M}_{2}
. Furthermore, if
\boldsymbol{T}
and
\boldsymbol{T}_{1}
commute, then
\boldsymbol{T}_{2}=\boldsymbol{T}_{1}
, which means that the models
\boldsymbol{M}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{M}_{2}
are invariant under the same GT
\boldsymbol{T}_{1}
.

.

For real data, the probabilities of occurrence of the states are known up to some uncertainty that depends on the size of the data: the larger the number of datapoints, the smaller the uncertainty.
To find all the symmetries of a finite dataset, one should take into account the level of uncertainty (even more so in undersampling regimes).
This can be done by reducing the noise in the empirical probability distribution, for instance, by assigning the same probability to states that have similar frequencies
at the level of precision warranted by the data (e.g., following the procedure of
22
).
A smaller number of different relevant probabilities
in the empirical distribution increases the number of symmetries observed in the data, and therefore reduces the number of parameters in the model.
This is a consequence of the duality between mixture models and models of the exponential family
2
,
which was exploited for instance for model reduction and parameter fitting for small systems in
19
. Such a procedure can significantly reduce the number of possible model parameters. A model selection procedure would then have fewer parameters to select from to identify if they are relevant or not. These parameters would also be associated with larger “macro”-patterns (composed of multiple operators).
Such an approach has the potential to greatly simplify the model selection procedure and lead to more robust model discovery.

5
Limiting the search to Minimally Complex Models (MCMs)

Despite the existence of equivalent models, the space of models is still extremely large, making it difficult to perform model selection. Ref.
15
proposes to perform model selection among a sub-family of spin models that they call Minimally Complex Models (MCMs), for which all the quantities of interest in the context of statistical inference are computationally cheap to calculate, making model selection feasible in practice among these models.

In particular, Ref.
15
introduced MCMs as an alternative to pairwise models that respects the structure of gauge transformations (GTs), because the family of MCMs is invariant under GT (the GT of an MCM is also an MCM). This is not the case for families of spin models restricted to have interactions only up to a certain order, such as pairwise models.
Working with MCMs thus brings a new perspective to statistical modeling, by making it possible to tackle data modeling problems in a way that is independent of the basis in which the data was originally represented.
This is interesting especially for systems where it is not clear if the current representation of the data is the most meaningful, or when one is interested in tackling a modeling problem from the most general perspective, in the absence of any information about the system.

Model selection with MCMs is also efficient thanks to the peculiar interaction structure of MCMs, in which interactions are grouped into independent sub-systems.
More precisely, in some representations, an MCM corresponds to a factorization of the model multivariate probability distribution over subsets of basis variables;
i.e., the model assumes that these subsets are statistically independent from each other (see Fig.
8
).
Performing model selection among MCMs thus aims to uncover the factorization of the model probability distribution that best matches the structure of the data.
Such factorization reduces the dimensionality of the statistical inference problem, while preserving most of the relevant information within the data.
MCMs can thus be used to identify groups of variables that carry most of the multivariate information in the data, or to facilitate the modeling problem for large systems by breaking it down into lower-dimensional ones.
Ref.
1
likens this approach to performing community detection for data (by analogy to community detection in network analysis), giving a simple interpretation to MCMs in terms of community structure.

In this section, we focus on a subset of
q
-state spin models that generalize the framework of minimally complex models
15
to discrete systems.

Figure 8:
(colors online)
Visualization of MCMs as factorization in state space.

Left.
In each row, the left panel shows an MCM based on four discrete variables
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4})
with two ICCs
represented in different colors. The MCM is represented in a preferred basis, which means that each ICC contains all possible operators over the shown subset of
\alpha
-variables.
The value of
q
for the discrete variables is not yet specified and the diagrams can be read for any chosen value of
q
.
For example, for the top row: for
q=2
, the red ICC has the
q^{2}-1=3
operators
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\in\{(1,0),(0,1),(1,1)\}
; for
q=3
, the red ICC has the
q^{2}-1=8
operators
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\in\{(1,0),(2,0),(0,1),(0,2),(1,1),(2,2),(1,2),(2,1)\}
.

Right.
The panel represents the factorization in state space (for
q=2
and
q=3
) that results from the MCM partition of the basis variables
\alpha_{i}
shown in the left panel.
The model probability distribution can be written as the product of joint distributions over lower dimensional subspaces shown in colors (line, surface, or volume). The color (green or red) refers to the corresponding ICC in the left panel and each marker represents a state of the system reduced to these subspaces (e.g., for the top panel and
q=2
, the pair of variables
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
can take the values
\{(0,0),(1,0),(0,1),(1,1)\}
, hence the four markers in the red area).
The probability of the states that are not represented by a colored marker is obtained by products of the probabilities of the sub-states that are represented by a marker.
When written in a preferred basis, the factorization of the state space resulting from the same MCM partition of the basis variables into ICC (as in the left panel) doesn’t depend on the value of the discretization
q
of the variables.
The only difference is the increase of the number of states in each subspace with
q
.

5.1
Definitions

Minimally complex models (MCMs) are defined in the context of binary data as models constructed from the union of independent complete components (ICCs)
15
.
Here, we generalize to the discrete case the definitions of complete components (CCs), independent components (ICs),
and MCMs. We call a “component” of a spin model
\mathcal{M}
any subset of operators of
\mathcal{M}
.

For
q
prime, we can directly extend to
q>2
the definition of complete components from Ref.
15
.

Definition 26
.

Complete Component (CC), for
q
prime.

A complete component is a set of operators
\mathcal{M}_{a}\subseteq[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]^{*}
that verifies that, for any two operators
(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu})\in\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\,2}
,
all the operators obtained by linear combinations of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
and
\boldsymbol{\nu}
in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
are in
\mathcal{M}_{a}\cup\boldsymbol{0}
, i.e.:

\displaystyle\forall(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu})\in\mathcal{M}_{a}^{2}\,,\qquad\forall(c,d)\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{2},\qquad c\,\boldsymbol{\mu}+d\,\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathcal{M}_{a}\cup\boldsymbol{0}\,.

(112)

In other words,
\mathcal{M}_{a}\cup\boldsymbol{0}
is a vector subspace of
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
of dimension
r_{a}
, where
r_{a}
is the cardinality of the largest linearly independent set in
\mathcal{M}_{a}
(namely the rank of
\mathcal{M}_{a}
– see Definition
14
).

In this definition, the set
\mathcal{M}_{a}
of operators can be a subset of operators of a model (hence the use of the term “component” instead of “model”).
We recall that the interaction vector
c\,\boldsymbol{\mu}+d\,\boldsymbol{\nu}
is associated with the spin operator
\phi^{c\,\boldsymbol{\mu}+d\,\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\doteq[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})]^{c}\,[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})]^{d}
.
For
q>2
, because of conjugate operators, the vector
c\,\boldsymbol{\mu}+d\,\boldsymbol{\nu}
can be equal to
\boldsymbol{0}
even for
(c,d)\neq(0,0)
, which is why we added the element
\boldsymbol{0}
to
\mathcal{M}_{a}
in Eq. (
112
).
This doesn’t happen in the binary case
15
.

Extending Definition
26
to non-prime values of
q
would define as being “complete” model components that we don’t want to consider complete in the context of MCMs.
For example, take the component
\mathcal{M}_{a}=\{(2,2)\}
with a single pairwise interaction for
n=2
spins and
q=4
. This component satisfies Eq. (
112
), because the only multiples of the operator
(2,2)
in
(\mathbb{Z}/4\mathbb{Z})^{2}
are
(0,0)
and itself, which are both in
\mathcal{M}_{a}\cup\boldsymbol{0}
.
The component
\mathcal{M}_{a}
is in some sense complete, but it is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
and not to
\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z}
(here
q=4
).
Considering such component as complete is problematic, because an MCM would then correspond to a factorization over “CC” defined for variables of different types (i.e., with different values of
q
).
We would also lose the re-parametrization property of CC (see Property
40
below), which is necessary for the efficient computation of many quantities of interest for modeling data with MCMs (see Sec.
5.4
).
To exclude this possibility, we give the following general definition of complete components, valid for any integer value of
q
.

Definition 27
.

(for all
q
)  Complete Component (CC).

A set of operators is said to be complete if it is equal to the set of all the operators generated by an independent set of operators (see Definition
11
of independent operators).
Formally, consider a set of operators
\mathcal{M}_{a}\subseteq[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]^{*}
and let
I=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{r_{a}}\}
be a maximal independent set of operators in
\mathcal{M}_{a}
.
The model component
\mathcal{M}_{a}
is said complete if its elements are all the operators generated by
I
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{a}=\langle I\rangle_{gen}^{*}=\bigg\{\,\sum_{j=1}^{r_{a}}c_{j}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}\;\Big|\;\,(c_{1},\dots,c_{r_{a}})\in[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{r_{a}}]^{*}\bigg\}\,,

(113)

where we used the notation
*
to indicate that we exclude the null vector (which doesn’t correspond to an operator).
In other words,
\mathcal{M}_{a}\cup\boldsymbol{0}
is a submodule of
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
isomorphic to
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{r_{a}}
, where
r_{a}
is the cardinality of
I
(namely, the rank of
\mathcal{M}_{a}
, denoted
\rank(\M_a)
).

From this definition, it follows that the number of operators in a complete component of rank
r_{a}
is equal to the number of nonzero vectors in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{r_{a}}
, which is
q^{r_{a}}-1
.

Property 30
.

Number of operators in a CC.
The number of operators in a complete component
\mathcal{M}_{a}
is
|\mathcal{M}_{a}|=q^{r_{a}}-1
, where
r_{a}
denotes the rank of
\mathcal{M}_{a}
.

We can then directly extend the definition of independence between complete components from the binary case to the more general discrete case.

Definition 28
.

Independence between CC and Minimally Complex Models (MCMs).

A set of complete components
\{\mathcal{M}_{a}\}_{a\in\mathcal{A}}
is said to be independent if none of the operators of a given component
\mathcal{M}_{a}
can be obtained by linear combinations of operators of the other components (i.e., generated by the set of operators
\cup_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}\backslash a}\mathcal{M}_{a^{\prime}}
).
This then leads to the definition of Minimally Complex Models (MCMs) as models formed by the union of independent complete components (ICCs),
\mathcal{M}=\cup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{M}_{a}
.

In the following, we give a more general definition of independence between components, for components that are not necessarily complete.
Our goal is to build a general formalism that can be used for any
q
-state spin model (not just MCMs) and which allows
for modeling data in a basis independent way.
For general spin models,
the notion of independence between components is directly linked to a factorization property of the model probability distribution (see Property
39
below); this factorization can be in the original representation of the data or in another gauge-transformed basis. In this framework, MCMs are a natural stepping stone towards modeling the data with more complex spin models.
To construct this formalism, we first introduce the definition of
basis of a component
, which we will then use to define independent components.

Definition 29
.

Basis and dimension of a component.

A basis of a component
\mathcal{M}_{a}
, denoted
B_{\mathcal{M}_{a}}
, is a minimal set of
d_{a}
independent operators
30
30

30

The operators of a basis of a component
\mathcal{M}
are not necessarily in
\mathcal{M}
.
that can generate all the operators of
\mathcal{M}_{a}
:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathcal{M}_{a}}=(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{d_{a}})\subseteq[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]^{*}\;\;\textrm{such that}\;\mathcal{M}_{a}\subseteq\langle\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathcal{M}_{a}}\rangle_{gen}\;.

(114)

We call the cardinality
d_{a}
of such basis the dimension of the component and denote it
\dim(\mathcal{M}_{a})
.
By definition, in an
n
-spin system,
\dim(\mathcal{M}_{a})\leq n
for any model component
\mathcal{M}_{a}
.
In the following, we consider the operators of the basis to be ordered, which we indicated in Eq. (
114
) by using parentheses instead of curly brackets in
(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{d_{a}})
and a bold symbol for
\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathcal{M}_{a}}
.

This definition is also valid if the component coincides with the whole model, thus defining the basis and the dimension of a
q
-state spin model.

The basis of a component is not necessarily unique, but its dimension is (by definition).
Note the difference between the rank and the dimension of a component:
the rank of a component is the cardinality of the largest independent set within the component, whereas its dimension is the cardinality of the smallest independent set containing the component.
If
q
is prime, the dimension and the rank of a component are always equal, but this is not necessarily the case if
q
is not prime, for which the dimension can be larger than the rank
31
31

31

For example, consider a two-spin system with
q=4
and the model
\mathcal{M}=\{(2,0)\}
. This model has a dimension
\dim(\mathcal{M})=1
because it can be generated by the single independent operator
(1,0)
(and not less), but it’s rank is
\rank(\M)=0
because
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(2,0)
is not an independent operator over
(\mathbb{Z}/4\mathbb{Z})^{2}
(as
2\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{0}
).
.
However for CC, any maximal independent set of operators in the component can generate the whole component, by definition
27
of completeness. This means that such a set is also a basis of the CC and therefore that the dimension of a CC is equal to its rank for all values of
q
. In general,
q
-state spin models are difficult to work with if
q
is not prime, due to
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})
not being a field,
but thanks to this last property (or more generally, to the completeness of the components), this is not an issue for MCMs.

Property 31
.

Basis, dimension, and rank of a CC.

Any maximal independent set of operators in a CC is also a basis of the component.
Consequently, the dimension and the rank of a CC are equal, for all integer values of
q
(prime or not).
In the following, we will denote by
r_{a}=\rank(\M_a)=\dim(\mathcal{M}_{a})
both the rank and the dimension of a CC
\mathcal{M}_{a}
.

We have already introduced a definition of spin basis in Sec.
4
, in the context of the definition
17
of gauge transformations (GTs). We defined a spin basis for an
n
-spin system as an ordered set of
n
independent operators, denoted
\boldsymbol{b}=(\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}},\cdots,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}})
, and a GT as a change of spin basis of the form
\mathcal{T}:\boldsymbol{s}\to\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{b}(\boldsymbol{s})
.
A basis of a component in Definition
29
also corresponds to a spin basis, but it is reduced to the (minimal) subspace in which the component is defined.
Note that, in Definition
29
and throughout the paper, we used the term “operator” for both the spin operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
(proper use of the term)
and its vector representation
\boldsymbol{\mu}
.
For clarity and coherence of the notations, we will denote a basis of a component
\mathcal{M}_{a}
by
\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathcal{M}_{a}}=(\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}},\cdots,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{d_{a}}})
when written in terms of the spin operators
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
and by
\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathcal{M}_{a}}=(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{d_{a}})
when written in terms of their vector representation
\boldsymbol{\mu}
.

Using the definition of basis of a component, we now define independent components.

Definition 30
.

Independent Components (ICs).

Consider a set of operators
\mathcal{M}\subseteq[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]^{*}
formed by the union of disjoint components
\mathcal{M}_{a}
:
\mathcal{M}=\cup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{M}_{a}
, where
\mathcal{A}
is a set of labels identifying the components.
Consider a basis
\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathcal{M}_{a}}
for each component
\mathcal{M}_{a}
. The set of components
\{\mathcal{M}_{a}\}_{a\in\mathcal{A}}
is said to be independent if, for each component
\mathcal{M}_{a}
, none of the operators generated by
\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathcal{M}_{a}}
can be generated by the combined bases of the other components (except for the null vector):

\displaystyle\forall\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\langle\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathcal{M}_{a}}\rangle_{gen}^{*}\,,\qquad\boldsymbol{\mu}\notin\bigg\langle\displaystyle\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}\\
a^{\prime}\neq a\end{subarray}}\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathcal{M}_{a^{\prime}}}\bigg\rangle_{gen}\;,

(115)

where
\langle S\rangle_{gen}
denotes the set of all the operators generated by a set
S
of operators over
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
.
In other words, the bases
