\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathcal{M}_{a}}
for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
generate independent subspaces of
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
(each isomorphic to
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{d_{a}}
, where
d_{a}=\dim(\mathcal{M}_{a})
), and the operators of each component
\mathcal{M}_{a}
belong only to the subspace defined by
\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathcal{M}_{a}}
.

For example, any choice of decomposition of an independent model (see Definition
16
) into components gives a set of independent components.

As a direct consequence of the independence between the components
\mathcal{M}_{a,\,a\in\mathcal{A}}
, one can construct a basis
\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathcal{M}}
of a model
\mathcal{M}=\cup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{M}_{a}
by combining the elements of the basis
\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathcal{M}_{a}}
of each component.
The resulting set of operators,
\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathcal{M}}=\cup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathcal{M}_{a}}
, is indeed still independent, thanks to Eq. (
115
).
This is generally not the case for random choices of decomposition of
\mathcal{M}
in components
\mathcal{M}_{a}
, as the components can belong to overlapping (minimal) subspaces of
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
.
We recall that a basis was defined as an ordered set; here we assume that the elements in
\mathcal{A}
have an order, and we create the combined basis
\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathcal{M}}
by “gluing” together the operators of the basis-component
\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathcal{M}_{a}}
in that order (same for the notation
\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathcal{M}}=\cup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathcal{M}_{a}}
that will also be used below). In practice, the specific choice of ordering of these basis components doesn’t matter, but it must be consistent throughout the analysis.

Property 32
.

Basis and dimension of ICs.
Consider a model
\mathcal{M}
formed by the union of components,
\mathcal{M}=\cup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{M}_{a}
, and consider a basis
\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathcal{M}_{a}}
for each component. If the components
\mathcal{M}_{a}
are independent, then the set of operators
\boldsymbol{B}=\cup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathcal{M}_{a}}

forms a basis of
\mathcal{M}
, and
the dimension of
\mathcal{M}
is thus equal to the sum of the dimensions of the ICs
\mathcal{M}_{a}
,
\dim(\mathcal{M})=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\dim(\mathcal{M}_{a})
.

Finally, we recover the definition
28
of
minimally complex models by combining the definitions of complete components and independent components
32
32

32

If all the independent components
\mathcal{M}_{a}
in Def.
30
are also complete, then, in Eq. (
115
), the set
\langle\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathcal{M}_{a}}\rangle^{*}
of operators generated by a basis
\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathcal{M}_{a}}
of a component is equal to the component,
\langle\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathcal{M}_{a}}\rangle^{*}=\mathcal{M}_{a}
(by definition of CC), and Def.
30
becomes Def.
28
of independence between CC.
.

Definition 31
.

Minimally Complex Models (MCMs).

A
q
-state spin model is a minimally complex model if its set of operators,
\mathcal{M}\subseteq[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]^{*}
, is formed by the union of
independent complete components (ICCs)

\mathcal{M}_{a}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}=\bigcup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{M}_{a}\,,

(116)

where
\mathcal{A}
is a set of labels identifying the ICCs.
In other words,
\{\mathcal{M}_{a}\}_{a\in\mathcal{A}}
is a set of independent components and, for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
, the component
\mathcal{M}_{a}
is complete.
Each ICC thus completely models an independent subspace of
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
.

Note that contrary to the binary case, an independent model, as defined in Definition
13
, is not an MCM.
This is because each independent subspace of dimension
d_{a}=1
requires
(q-1)
operators to be fully modeled by a
q
-state spin model, while an independent model only has one operator per dimension (which is strictly less than
(q-1)
for
q>2
).
However, we can define a notion of independent MCM in the discrete case, which reduces to that of independent model in the binary case.

Definition 32
.

Independent Minimally Complex Models (IMCMs).

MCMs for which each ICC has rank 1.

These models will be useful for the search algorithms for the best MCM proposed in Sec.
6.1
.

Finally, MCMs inherit all the properties of CCs and ICs mentioned above.

Property 33
.

Number of operators, basis, dimension, and rank of an MCM.

Consider an MCM
\mathcal{M}=\cup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{M}_{a}
with ICCs
\{\mathcal{M}_{a}\}_{a\in\mathcal{A}}
.
As a consequence of the completeness of the components, the dimension and the rank of each component
\mathcal{M}_{a}
are equal (see Property
31
), denoted
r_{a}=\rank(\M_a)=\dim(\mathcal{M}_{a})
,
and the number of operators in
\mathcal{M}
is given by (see Property
30
):

\displaystyle|\mathcal{M}|=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}(q^{r_{a}}-1)\,.

(117)

Thanks to the independence between the components
\mathcal{M}_{a}
, one can obtain a basis of
\mathcal{M}
by combining the bases
B_{\mathcal{M}_{a}}
of its components (see Property
32
):

\displaystyle B_{\mathcal{M}}=\cup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}B_{\mathcal{M}_{a}}\,.

(118)

The dimension of
\mathcal{M}
is thus equal to the sum of the dimensions of its ICCs
\mathcal{M}_{a}
, and so is its rank:
\rank(\M)=\dim(\mathcal{M})=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}r_{a}
.
In the following, we will denote by
r
both the rank and the dimension of an MCM
\mathcal{M}
.

Figure
9
gives four examples of MCMs. Observe that in some representations, the complete components
\mathcal{M}_{a}
of an MCMs can spread over more than
r_{a}=\rank(\M_a)
variables.
For example, the two models in panel (b) both have a single ICC of rank
r_{a}=1
. In the top model, this ICC spreads over two variables (with pairwise operators), whereas in the bottom model, the ICC is compact, based solely on one variable, and we can immediately see that the component is complete and of rank 1.
Similarly, the two models in panel (a) both have two ICCs of rank
r_{a}=1
and
2
respectively.
It is not easy to see that the top model is an MCM, because the ICC of rank 2 is spread over three variables. In contrast in the bottom model, one can clearly identify the two ICCs, with all possible operators based on
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
for one ICC, and all the operators based on
\sigma_{3}
for the other ICC.
In these examples, the top and bottom models of each panel are related by gauge transformations, which means that they are different representations of the same abstract MCM (see sec.
4
).
In the following section, we discuss GTs of MCMs.

Figure 9:
Examples of MCMs and their GTs to a preferred basis.

Each box contains an example of an MCM.
Operators are represented using the same convention as in Fig.
6
. In panel b), the green color is also used to indicate that a spin is raised to the third power in the corresponding operator (e.g., in model
\mathcal{M}
, the green pairwise link represents the operator
s_{1}^{\,3}s_{2}^{\,3}
). For each example, we use the GT
\mathcal{T}
to transform the top model into the bottom one, and the inverse GT
\mathcal{T}^{-1}
to go back to the top model.
The bottom model
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is in a preferred basis, in which each ICC appears in a compact complete form over a subset of spins (circled with gray dashed lines). The matrix representations of these models and of their GTs are available in App.
D.1
.

a)
Example of two equivalent MCMs with
q=3
for
n=3
spins; these models have two ICCs with rank
2
and
1
respectively. For clarity we represented the models using the decomposition
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{1}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}
, where the operators in
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}
are the c.c. of the operators in
\mathcal{M}_{1}
. The transformed model is obtained using
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*\prime}
, where
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}[\mathcal{M}_{1}]
(see Sec.
4.4
).

b)
Example of two equivalent MCMs,
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, with
q=4
for
n=2
spins; these models have a single ICC of rank
1
.

5.2
Gauge transformations of MCMs, equivalence classes, and counting

Similarly to the binary case, the family of MCMs for
q>2
is invariant under gauge transformations (GTs), meaning that the GT of an MCM is also an MCM (see Def.
17
for the definition of GTs).
Indeed, under a GT, a complete component remains complete and with the same rank, and a set of independent components remains independent (see App.
D.2
).
This is an interesting property of MCMs if one wants to tackle model selection of discrete data from a more general perspective, independently of the basis in which the data is originally represented.
In the following, we lay out the foundations to work with MCMs in a basis-independent way.

Property 34
.

Gauge transformation of an MCM.

The gauge transformation of an MCM is also an MCM and has ICCs of the same ranks as the original model.

Figure
9
shows examples of GTs of MCMs.
In particular, it highlights the transformation of MCMs into a
preferred basis
, a useful notion introduced in the binary case
15
.
As noted previously, in some representations, a complete component
\mathcal{M}_{a}
can spread over more than
r_{a}=\rank(\M_a)
basis variables.
For example, the top model for
q=4
in Fig.
9
.b has a single CC
\mathcal{M}=\{(1,1),(2,2),(3,3)\}
with operators of order two despite having only a rank
r=1
.
Using the GT
\sigma_{1}=s_{1}s_{2}
and
\sigma_{2}=s_{1}
,
\mathcal{M}
is transformed into the model
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\{(1,0),(2,0),(3,0)\}
(see App.
D.1
), in which all the operators are based solely on
r=1
variable (see Fig.
9
.b bottom).
Ref.
15
introduced the notion of
preferred bases
of an MCM, which are spin bases in which each ICC
\mathcal{M}_{a}
of the MCM appears in a compact form, based solely on
r_{a}=\rank(\M_a)
basis elements just like for
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
above. The model in Fig.
9
.a bottom is another example of an MCM represented in a preferred basis.

Definition 33
.

Preferred basis of an MCM.

A preferred basis of an MCM is a spin basis,
\boldsymbol{b}^{*}=(\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}},\cdots,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}})
, in which each ICC
\mathcal{M}_{a}
is based solely on
r_{a}
basis elements, where
r_{a}
is the rank (and dimension) of
\mathcal{M}_{a}
. In such basis, each ICC appears in a compact form and the MCM corresponds to a partition of a subset of the basis variables in which each part identifies an ICC (see dashed outlines in Fig.
9
bottom – the variables are fully connected within each part, with operators of all orders, and not connected between the parts).

In other words, a preferred spin basis
\boldsymbol{b}^{*}
for an MCM defines a GT
\mathcal{T}:\boldsymbol{s}\to\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{b}^{*}(\boldsymbol{s})
(see Definition
17
) that transforms the MCM into its compact representation, with each ICC based on a minimal number of basis variables.
More generally, one can define a preferred basis for any set of ICs (for which the components are not necessarily complete) as a basis in which each component
\mathcal{M}_{a}
is only expressed over
d_{a}=\dim(\mathcal{M}_{a})
basis variables.

It is always possible to construct a preferred basis for an MCM
\mathcal{M}=\cup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{M}_{a}
(and more generally for a set of ICs), simply by combining the elements of a basis
\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathcal{M}_{a}}
for each IC
\mathcal{M}_{a}
, thanks to the independence between the ICCs (see Property
32
).
We obtain a spin basis of the form
\boldsymbol{b}^{*}=\cup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\,\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathcal{M}_{a}}\cup\overline{\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathcal{M}}}
, where
\overline{\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathcal{M}}}
is a set of
(n-\dim(\mathcal{M}))
independent operators chosen to complete the spin basis.
In such a basis, the operators of each component
\mathcal{M}_{a}
would be based solely on their respective basis variables
\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathcal{M}_{a}}
(by definition
30
of ICs), which is precisely what we want for a preferred basis.
Moreover, for MCMs, because the component
\mathcal{M}_{a}
are complete, we can choose any set of
r_{a}=\rank(\M_a)
independent operators in
\mathcal{M}_{a}
to define its basis
\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathcal{M}_{a}}=(\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{a}_{1}},\cdots,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{a}_{r_{a}}})
(see Property
31
).

This is precisely the construction we followed in each panel of Fig.
9
to define the GT
\mathcal{T}
(from top to bottom) of the model
\mathcal{M}
into a preferred basis.
In panel b), the top model
\mathcal{M}
has a single CC, which can be fully generated by the operator
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}=(1,1)
. We used this operator to create a preferred basis for
\mathcal{M}
, by defining the first basis element of the GT
\mathcal{T}
as
\sigma_{1}=\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}(\boldsymbol{s})=s_{1}s_{2}
and choosing the second basis element to be independent from
\sigma_{1}
.
In panel a), the model
\mathcal{M}
has two ICCs; we constructed a preferred basis by combining the elements of a basis
\boldsymbol{b}_{a}
for each ICC
\mathcal{M}_{a}
, for which any choice of
r_{a}
independent operators in
\mathcal{M}_{a}
can be used.
The operators
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}=(1,1,0)
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}=(1,0,2)
defining the first two variables of the preferred basis,
\sigma_{1}=\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}(\boldsymbol{s})=s_{1}s_{2}
and
\sigma_{2}=\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}}(\boldsymbol{s})=s_{1}s_{3}^{2}
,
are two independent operators of the rank-
2
ICC of
\mathcal{M}
.
The operator
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{3}=(0,0,1)
, which defines the third basis element
\sigma_{3}=\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{3}}(\boldsymbol{s})=s_{3}
, is an independent operator of the other ICC of
\mathcal{M}
(which is already in a compact form).
Upon the GT
\mathcal{T}
, the model
\mathcal{M}
is transformed into the bottom model
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, which appears in a compact form with the rank-2 ICC based solely on the first two variables.

Property 35
.

Construction of a preferred basis of an MCM.

A preferred basis
\boldsymbol{b}^{*}
of an MCM
\mathcal{M}=\cup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{M}_{a}
is constructed by combining the elements of a basis
\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathcal{M}_{a}}
for each ICC
\mathcal{M}_{a}
:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{b}^{*}=\cup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\,\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathcal{M}_{a}}\cup\overline{\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathcal{M}}}\,,

(119)

where
\overline{\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathcal{M}}}
is a set of
(n-r)
independent operators that complete the spin basis, with
r=\rank(\M)
.
For each component
\mathcal{M}_{a}
, any set of
r_{a}=\rank(\M_a)
independent operators in
\mathcal{M}_{a}
can be chosen to define the basis
\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathcal{M}_{a}}=(\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{a}_{1}},\cdots,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{a}_{r_{a}}})
.
The preferred basis of an MCM is therefore not necessarily unique.

Using the construction above, any MCM can be represented in a preferred basis, in which the MCM corresponds to a partition of the basis variables, where each part of
r_{a}
variables identifies an ICC of rank
r_{a}
.
This implies that MCMs with ICCs of the same ranks are related by a GT, because they can be mapped to the same partition in a preferred basis, and therefore they belong to the same equivalence class. This gives the following property, characterizing equivalence classes of MCMs:

Property 36
.

Equivalence classes of MCMs.

Consider an MCM with
m
ICCs and denote by
m_{r_{a}}
the number of ICCs of rank
r_{a}
in the model, such that
m=\sum_{r_{a}=1}^{n}m_{r_{a}}
. For a given value of
q
and number of spins
n
, all the MCMs with the same sequence of multiplicities
\{m_{r_{a}}\}_{1\leq r_{a}\leq n}
of their ICC ranks are equivalent under gauge transformation, i.e. for any two such model, there exists a GT that can transform one into the other. They all belong to the same equivalence class of MCMs, which is identified by the sequence of multiplicities
\{m_{r_{a}}\}_{1\leq r_{a}\leq n}
.

Proof.

We give a sketch of the proof.
Consider two MCMs
\mathcal{M}_{x}
and
\mathcal{M}_{y}
that have the same sequence of multiplicities
\{m_{r_{a}}\}_{1\leq r_{a}\leq n}
(i.e., the same rank list
\{r_{a}\}_{a\in\mathcal{A}}
).
We specify two GTs,
\mathcal{T}_{x}
for
\mathcal{M}_{x}
and
\mathcal{T}_{y}
for
\mathcal{M}_{y}
, that bring each model into a preferred basis (in which each MCM corresponds to a partition of the basis elements).
We design the two GTs such that they map ICCs of the same rank in each model to the same basis elements (for example, an ICC of rank 2 could be mapped to the variables
\sigma_{3}
and
\sigma_{4}
for both MCMs).
This is possible because the two models have ICCs of the same ranks.
As a result, the transformed models
\mathcal{T}_{x}[\mathcal{M}_{x}]
and
\mathcal{T}_{y}[\mathcal{M}_{y}]
are identical MCMs (they correspond to the same partition of the basis elements), which means that
\mathcal{M}_{x}
and
\mathcal{M}_{y}
are equivalent. Composing the GTs, we can also write that
\mathcal{M}_{y}=\mathcal{T}_{y}^{-1}[\mathcal{T}_{x}[\mathcal{M}_{x}]]
, where
\mathcal{T}_{y}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{T}_{x}
is a GT.
∎

As a consequence, the number of different classes of MCMs with the same rank
r
is obtained by enumerating all possible (unordered) rank list
\{r_{a}\}_{a}
satisfying the constraint that
\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}r_{a}=r
. This corresponds to all possible integer partitions of
r
.

Property 37
.

Number of classes of MCMs of rank
r
.
In a
q
-state spin system with
n
spins, the number of equivalence classes of MCMs of rank
r
(where
r\leq n
) is given by the number of integer partitions of
r
, independently of the value of
q
.

This number is the same as in the binary case
15
,
because MCMs of different classes only differ by the rank of their ICCs, independently of the value of
q
(see Fig.
8
and
11
).
For example, for any integer value of
q
, there are two equivalence classes of MCMs with rank
r=2
(the class of MCMs with two ICCs of rank
1
each, and the class of MCMs with a single ICC of rank
2
), three equivalence classes for
r=3
, and five for
r=4
(see complexity landscape in Fig.
11
).
The value of
q
instead changes the number of MCMs in each equivalence class.

Property 38
.

Number of MCMs in a class.

For a
q
-state spin system with
n
spins, the number of models in the class of MCMs defined by the sequence of multiplicities
\{m_{r_{a}}\}_{1\leq r_{a}\leq n}
(where
m_{r_{a}}
denotes the number of ICCs of rank
r_{a}
in the models of the class) is given by (see App.
D.3
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\rm MCM}(n,\{m_{r_{a}}\}_{1\leq r_{a}\leq n})=\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{N}_{q}(n,r)}{\displaystyle\prod_{r_{a}=1}^{n}(m_{r_{a}}!)\;\,\mathcal{N}_{q}(r_{a},r_{a})^{m_{r_{a}}}}\;.

(120)

Here,
\mathcal{N}_{q}(n,r)
is the number of different ways to choose
r
independent operators in a
q
-state spin system with
n
spins, which is:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{q}(n,r)=\begin{cases}\;\;\displaystyle\prod_{i=0}^{r-1}(q^{n}-q^{i})\,,&\qquad{\rm for}\;q\;\textrm{prime},\\[5.0pt]
\;\;\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{K}p_{k}^{\,(m_{k}-1)n^{2}}\,\prod_{i=0}^{r-1}(p_{k}^{\,n}-p_{k}^{\,i})\,,&\qquad{\rm for}\;q\;\textrm{not prime}.\end{cases}

(121)

The result for
q
prime was obtained using the same reasoning as in the binary case
15
.

For example, for
n=3
and
q=3
, there are
117
MCMs with two ICCs of respective rank
r_{1}=1
and
r_{2}=2
; Fig.
9
.a shows two of them.
The total number of spin models that are MCMs in an
n
-spin system can be obtained by summing the value in Eq. (
120
) over all possible integer partitions of
r
, for all
r
from
1
to
n
.

The invariance of the family of MCMs under gauge transformations allows one to approach model selection from a representation invariant perspective, and ultimately design model selection procedures able to capture patterns of the data (such as symmetries, co-dependencies) in a way that is independent of the basis in which the data is initially observed.
This is not possible for instance if one restricts the selection to
pairwise models, because the family of pairwise spin models is not invariant under gauge transformation. Figure
6
.a gives an example of a GT that transforms a pairwise model into a model with operators of higher order (in this case, there is a
3
-spin operator).

5.3
Statistical properties of MCMs

Just like in the binary case
15
, when represented in a preferred basis, an MCM corresponds to a partition of a subset of the basis variables in which each part identifies an ICC: the variables are fully connected inside the parts, with all operators of all orders, and not connected between the parts (see the models in Fig.
9
bottom).
The MCM thus completely models all the statistical dependencies within each part (each ICC), but no statistical dependencies at all between the parts, as if they were independent (see Fig.
8
, in the right panel, subspaces of different colors are considered statistically independent by the MCM in the left panel).
This peculiar structure of MCMs endows them with properties that make modeling data with MCMs relatively efficient, in comparison to other common modeling approaches.
In particular, the independence between the ICCs allows writing the model probability distribution as a product of marginal distributions over each ICC, when expressed in a preferred basis (see Fig.
8
and
10
).
Moreover, the completeness of the ICCs enables a re-parametrization of the components, with which most of the quantities of interest in the context of statistical inference can be easily computed (see next section
5.4
).
Below, we formalize these two properties.

When written in a preferred basis, there are no dependencies between the basis variables corresponding to different ICCs of an MCM. This means that the multivariate probability distribution of the MCM factorizes over the basis elements of its ICCs.
We recall that a preferred basis
\boldsymbol{b}^{*}
is a spin basis for the
n
spin system, which means that it can be used to define a GT
\mathcal{T}
of the form
\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{b}^{*}(\boldsymbol{s})
(see definition
17
).

Property 39
.

Factorization of MCMs over their ICCs.

As a consequence of the independence between ICCs, the probability distribution of an MCM
\mathcal{M}=\cup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\,\mathcal{M}_{a}
factorizes over the probability distributions of its ICCs
\mathcal{M}_{a}
, when expressed
in terms of the operators

\boldsymbol{b}^{*}(\boldsymbol{s})=\cup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\,\boldsymbol{b}_{a}(\boldsymbol{s})\cup\overline{\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathcal{M}}}(\boldsymbol{s})
of a preferred basis of
\mathcal{M}
(where each
\boldsymbol{b}_{a}
denotes a basis of the ICC
\mathcal{M}_{a}
– see property
35
):

p(\boldsymbol{s}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g},\mathcal{M})=\frac{1}{q^{(n-r)}}\,\prod_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\,p_{a}\big(\boldsymbol{b}_{a}(\boldsymbol{s})\,|\,\boldsymbol{g}_{a}^{\prime},\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\prime}\big)\,.

(122)

Each
\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\prime}
denotes the ICC
\mathcal{M}_{a}
represented in terms of the basis variables
\boldsymbol{b}_{a}(\boldsymbol{s})
, and each
p_{a}
is a probability distribution over these
r_{a}=\rank(\M_a)
spin variables only.
The vector
\boldsymbol{g}_{a}^{\prime}
of parameters of the ICC
\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\prime}
corresponds to a permutation of the parameters of the vector
\boldsymbol{g}_{a}
that results from the gauge transformation
\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{b}^{*}(\boldsymbol{s})
(see Def.
19
).
The prefactor corresponds to a uniform distribution over the
(n-r)

q
-state variables not modeled by
\mathcal{M}
.

In equation (
122
), we used that for a GT, the probability distribution of the model
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}[\mathcal{M}]
in the new basis
\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{b}^{*}(\boldsymbol{s})
is related to that of the model
\mathcal{M}
in the original spin basis
\boldsymbol{s}
by

p(\boldsymbol{s}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g},\mathcal{M})=p(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g}^{\prime},\mathcal{M}^{\prime})
(see Eq. (
108
)).
For each ICC
\mathcal{M}_{a}
, the vector
\boldsymbol{b}_{a}(\boldsymbol{s})
corresponds to a subset of variables of the new basis
\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{b}^{*}(\boldsymbol{s})
; it can be interpreted as the transformed state
\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}
reduced to the subspace modeled by
\mathcal{M}_{a}
.

The GT
\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{b}^{*}(\boldsymbol{s})
can be written equivalently for the corresponding color variables as the vector-matrix product
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,\boldsymbol{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}^{*}}
,
where
\boldsymbol{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}^{*}}=(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\,\dots,\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n})
is an
n\times n
-matrix whose columns are the
n
operators of the preferred basis
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}=\cup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathcal{M}_{a}}\cup\overline{\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathcal{M}}}
(see Property
18
).
To use a notation analogous to that used for the spin variables,
we denote by
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,\boldsymbol{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}^{*}}
the vector resulting from the gauge transformation of
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
into the basis
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}=(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n})
, i.e.
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\dots,\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n})
.
The gauge transformation can thus be written as
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{B}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
. Similarly, the vector
\boldsymbol{B}_{a}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}^{a}_{1},\dots,\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}^{a}_{r_{a}})
denotes the transformed state
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}
reduced to the subspace modeled by the ICC
\mathcal{M}_{a}
.
Equation (
122
) can then be written in terms of the color variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
as:

p(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g},\mathcal{M})=\frac{1}{q^{(n-r)}}\,\prod_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\,p_{a}\big(\boldsymbol{B}_{a}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\,|\,\boldsymbol{g}_{a}^{\prime},\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\prime}\big)\,.

(123)

When written in a preferred basis, each ICC
\mathcal{M}_{a}
of the MCM is a complete model (see Sec.
2
) for the sub-system of
r_{a}
variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{B}_{a}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
, where
\boldsymbol{B}_{a}
is a basis of
\mathcal{M}_{a}
.
This means that
\mathcal{M}_{a}
can model any possible statistical patterns of the discrete variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}^{\prime}
.
The component has
(q^{r_{a}}-1)
parameters
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
that fully parametrize the
r_{a}
-dimensional sub-system and is thus equivalently specified by the
(q^{r_{a}}-1)
state probabilities
p(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}^{\prime})
for all states
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}^{\prime}\in[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{r_{a}}]^{*}
of the sub-system (the last probability being fixed by normalization,
p(\boldsymbol{0})=1-\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}^{\prime}\neq\boldsymbol{0}}p(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}^{\prime})
).
The ICC
\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\prime}
can thus be re-written as the following parametric family of probability distributions:

\displaystyle p_{a}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}^{\prime}\,|\,\,\boldsymbol{\eta}_{a},\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\prime})=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{r_{a}}]^{*}}\,\eta_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\,\delta(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\nu})+\bigg(1-\sum_{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{r_{a}}]^{*}}\eta_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\bigg)\,\delta(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{0})\,,

(124)

where each
\eta_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
is a parameter that represents the probability that the system is in the state
\boldsymbol{\nu}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}^{\prime}
, and
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{a}
is a vector that contains all the
(q^{r_{a}}-1)
parameters
\eta_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
.

Within each ICC, there is therefore a bijection between the
(q^{r_{a}}-1)
parameters
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
’s and
\eta_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
’s (see Sec.
2
).
For any choice of the parameters
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
’s of the ICC, the parameters
\eta_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
’s
correspond to the state probabilities of the system given by the definition of the complete spin model in Eq. (
40
),
\eta_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}=p(\boldsymbol{\nu}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g}_{a}^{\prime},\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\prime})
for each state
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}=\boldsymbol{\nu}
of the sub-system (which corresponds to an inverse discrete Fourier transform). Reciprocally, the parameters
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
can be obtained from the parameters
\eta_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
by the discrete Fourier transform of the log-probabilities, as in Eq. (
45
).

Property 40
.

Equivalent parametrization of ICCs in terms of the state probabilities.

As a consequence of the completeness of the ICCs, the probability distribution of an ICC
\mathcal{M}_{a}
can be equivalently parametrized by the
(q^{r_{a}}-1)
state probabilities

\eta_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}^{\prime}}=p_{a}\big(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}^{\prime}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g}_{a}^{\prime},\,\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\prime}\big)
for all state
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}^{\prime}\in[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{r_{a}}]^{*}

(one of the state probabilities is fixed by normalization), where
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{B}_{a}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
is the transformed states reduced to the
r_{a}
-dimensional sub-system modeled by
\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\prime}
.

Figure 10:
(colors online)
Representation of the model discrete probability distribution in state space for two ICCs of Fig.
9
. Each dot represents a state of the spin system and the color represents the probability of the state. States with the same color have the same probability.

Top panel.
Representation of the probability distribution of the MCM with a single ICC in Fig.
9
.b. In the best basis, defined as the color variables
(\alpha_{1}^{\prime},\alpha_{2}^{\prime})
in the bottom panel of Fig.
9
.b, one can see directly that the model probability distribution factorizes and is uniform along the direction of
\alpha_{2}^{\prime}
, which gives the state space representation in the top left panel. After the GT
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=\mathcal{T}^{-1}[(\alpha_{1}^{\prime},\alpha_{2}^{\prime})]
(top MCM in Fig.
9
.b), the probability distribution remains uniform in the direction of
\alpha_{2}^{\prime}
, which is perpendicular to the direction of
\alpha_{1}^{\prime}=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}
. This is a non-trivial factorization when looking at the system of variables
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
.

Bottom panel.
Representation of the probability distribution of the single ICC based on the spin variables
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
in the bottom panel of Fig.
9
.a. We denote by
(\alpha_{1}^{\prime},\alpha_{2}^{\prime},\alpha_{3}^{\prime})
the corresponding color variables of this basis, which is a preferred basis for the ICC, and by
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})
the basis variables for the top panel of Fig.
9
.a. Independently of the basis representation, the probability distribution for this ICC is uniform in the direction of
\alpha_{3}^{\prime}
(indicated here by the color remaining identical in this direction), which is perpendicular to the plane defined by
\alpha_{1}^{\prime}
and
\alpha_{2}^{\prime}
.
The independencies in the model probability distribution
(here indicated by the uniform direction)
are non-trivial in the basis of the variables
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})
(bottom right panel),
but much easier to see in the basis of the variables
(\alpha_{1}^{\prime},\alpha_{2}^{\prime},\alpha_{3}^{\prime})
(bottom left panel).

The factorization and completeness properties of MCMs mean that, at best fit, an MCM corresponds to an approximation of the data that neglects all statistical dependencies between the variables of different ICCs and preserves all the other statistical patterns.
In the context of model selection, the best MCM for a given dataset therefore corresponds to a factorization of the model probability distribution that best matches the structure of the data, optimally balancing goodness-of-fit of the data against model complexity (methods for this selection are discussed in the next section). This optimal factorization could be in the original basis of the data or in any other gauge-transformed basis (see Fig.
10
).
At best fit, this MCM thus provides a factorization of the empirical distribution that optimally approximates the data, by neglecting statistical dependencies between variables where they are the least relevant (variables assigned to different ICCs) and preserving them where they are the most relevant (within ICCs).

In a given basis, the number of possible factorizations over
r
basis elements corresponds to the number of partitions of these
r
variables into distinct parts, which is equal to the Bell number of
r
, denoted
B_{r}
. The number of different MCMs that factorize in the same preferred basis (denoted
MCM^{*}
) is therefore obtained by summing this number over all possible model ranks, from
r=0
to
n
, and accounting for the possible choices of the
r
basis operators on which the model is based:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{MCM^{*}}(n)=\sum_{r=0}^{n}{n\choose r}B_{r}\,.

(125)

This number doesn’t depend on the value of
q
and is thus identical to the binary case (see Fig.
8
).

5.4
Statistical inference and model selection with MCMs

A significant advantage of Minimally Complex Models over other parametric models for discrete data is that most useful quantities for statistical modeling have a closed-form analytical expression and are thus cheap to compute numerically.
Similarly to the binary case
15
, the factorization property
39
of the model probability distribution implies that the likelihood and the evidence (
7
) of an MCM also factorize over the ICCs when written in a preferred basis, and the equivalent parametrization property
40
makes these quantities easy to compute analytically for each ICC.
Below, we give closed-form expressions for the maximum likelihood, the geometric complexity appearing in the MDL principle Eq. (
8
),
and the model evidence (
7
).
These results (see Appendix
D
for proofs) are direct extensions of the formulae obtained in the binary case
15
to the more general discrete case.

Consider a
q
-state spin system with
n
spins represented by the discrete variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n})\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
and a dataset composed of
N
observations of the system denoted
\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}=(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(1)},\cdots,\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(N)})
.
We assume that the system is stationary and that the observed states are randomly sampled from the same underlying distribution,
p(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g},\,\mathcal{M})
, defined by a parametric model
\mathcal{M}
.
Under this assumption, the probability that the model
\mathcal{M}
with parameters
\boldsymbol{g}
assigns to the data, namely the likelihood of the model, is
P(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g},\mathcal{M})=\prod_{i=1}^{N}p(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(i)}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g},\mathcal{M})
.
In the following, we take the model
\mathcal{M}
to be an MCM
\mathcal{M}=\cup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{M}_{a}
. We consider a preferred basis of
\mathcal{M}
of the form
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}=\cup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\,\boldsymbol{B}_{a}\cup\overline{\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathcal{M}}}
, where each
\boldsymbol{B}_{a}
is a basis of the ICC
\mathcal{M}_{a}
, and denote by
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\cup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\prime}
the representation of
\mathcal{M}
in this basis (i.e.,
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{B}^{*}}[\mathcal{M}]
and
\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{B}^{*}}[\mathcal{M}_{a}]
for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
).
We denote by
r
the rank of
\mathcal{M}
and by
r_{a}
the rank of each ICC
\mathcal{M}_{a}
.

Using the factorization Property
39
, the likelihood of an MCM can be written as the product of the likelihood of each ICC, when expressed in a preferred basis:

P(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g},\,\mathcal{M})\,=P(\boldsymbol{B}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}})\,|\,\boldsymbol{g}^{\prime},\,\mathcal{M}^{\prime})=\frac{1}{q^{N(n-r)}}\prod_{a\in\mathcal{A}}P_{a}\big(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}_{a}^{\prime}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g}_{a}^{\prime},\,\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\prime}\big)\,,

(126)

where, for each ICC
\mathcal{M}_{a}
,
\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}_{a}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{B}_{a}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}})
denotes the dataset
\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}
transformed in the basis
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
(preferred basis of
\mathcal{M}
) and reduced to the basis variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{B}_{a}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
(i.e., reduced to the subspace modeled by the ICC).
Each term
P_{a}\big(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}_{a}^{\prime}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g}_{a}^{\prime},\,\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\prime}\big)=\prod_{i=1}^{N}p_{a}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}^{\prime(i)}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g}_{a}^{\prime},\,\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\prime})
is the likelihood of the ICC
\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\prime}
reduced to the variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}^{\prime}
; it is a function of the
(q^{r_{a}}-1)
parameters in the vector
\boldsymbol{g}_{a}^{\prime}
.
Using Property
40
, this likelihood is equivalently parametrized by the
(q^{r_{a}}-1)
state probabilities
\eta_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}=p_{a}\big(\boldsymbol{\nu}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g}_{a}^{\prime},\,\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\prime}\big)
,
where
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{r_{a}}]^{*}
denotes the state of the basis variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}^{\prime}
. The likelihood of an ICC expressed in terms of the parameters
\eta_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
is then:

\displaystyle P_{a}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}_{a}^{\prime}\,|\,\boldsymbol{\eta}_{a},\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\prod_{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{r_{a}}}\,\eta_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\;\,k_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}_{a}^{\prime})}\,,

(127)

where
k_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}_{a}^{\prime})
is the number of times the state
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\nu}
occurs in the dataset
\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}_{a}^{\prime}
, and
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{a}
denotes the vector of all the parameters
\eta_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
of
\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\prime}
(excluding
\eta_{\boldsymbol{0}}
which is fixed by normalization).
Consequently, the values of the parameters
\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{a}={\rm argmax}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}_{a}}P(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}_{a}^{\prime}\,|\,\boldsymbol{\eta}_{a},\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\prime})
that best fit the data within each ICC are directly equal to the empirical probabilities:

Property 41
.

Maximum likelihood estimates.

For each ICC
\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\prime}
, the best fit
of the parameters
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{a}=\big(\eta_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\big)_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
is given by the empirical probabilities:

\hat{\eta}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}=\frac{k_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}_{a}^{\prime})}{N}\,,\qquad\textrm{for all}\;\boldsymbol{\nu}\in[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{r_{a}}]^{*}\,,

(128)

where
k_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}_{a}^{\prime})
is the number of times the state
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\nu}
occurs in the reduced dataset
\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}_{a}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{B}_{a}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}})
.

The bijection between the parameters
\boldsymbol{g}
and the
\boldsymbol{\eta}
greatly simplifies the inference step, allowing one to compute the maximum likelihood parameters without having to resort to any parameter fitting (e.g., through gradient descent), which are often computational demanding for high-dimensional models.
Replacing these parameter values in the likelihood function (
127
), we obtain a closed-form expression for the maximum likelihood of an MCM:

Property 42
.

Maximum likelihood of MCMs.
The maximum likelihood of an MCM
\mathcal{M}=\cup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{M}_{a}
for a dataset
\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}
is:

P(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}\,|\,\boldsymbol{\hat{g}},\mathcal{M})=\frac{1}{q^{N(n-r)}}\,\prod_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\left[\prod_{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}_{a}^{\prime}}\,\left(\frac{k_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}_{a}^{\prime})}{N}\right)^{\vskip-14.22636ptk_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}_{a}^{\prime})}\right]\,,

(129)

where the second product is over the distinct states
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\nu}
occurring in the reduced dataset
\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}_{a}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{B}_{a}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}})
(written in the basis
\boldsymbol{B}_{a}
of the ICC
\mathcal{M}_{a}
)
and
k_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}_{a}^{\prime})
is
the number of times they occur.
The maximum log-likelihood can then be written as:

\log P(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}\,|\,\boldsymbol{\hat{g}},\mathcal{M})=-N(n-r)\log(q)-N\,\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}S(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}^{\prime}_{a})\,,

(130)

where

S(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}^{\prime}_{a})=-\sum_{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}_{a}^{\prime}}\,\frac{k_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}_{a}^{\prime})}{N}\,\log\left(\frac{k_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}_{a}^{\prime})}{N}\right)

(131)

is the entropy of the dataset
\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}_{a}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{B}_{a}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}})

(which is a reduction of the original data
\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}
to the subspace modeled by the ICC
\mathcal{M}_{a}
).

The maximum log-likelihood in Eq. (
130
) can also be obtained more directly by expressing the log-likelihood of each ICC
\mathcal{M}_{a}
for the system reduced to the variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{B}_{a}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
, as minus
N
times the sum of the entropy of the reduced data
S(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}^{\prime}_{a})
and of the Kullback-Leibler divergence between the ICC and the data. This latter is zero at best fit, because the ICC is complete over the subspace based on the variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}^{\prime}
.

A useful quantity for model selection is the model evidence (or marginal likelihood) defined in Eq. (
7
).
Selecting the model with the largest evidence, instead of the largest maximum likelihood, allows basing the selection of the model not only on the goodness of its fit to the data, but also on its complexity.
Within a family of models, the model with the largest evidence achieves an optimal balance between goodness-of-fit and simplicity.
Here we compute the evidence assuming Jeffreys’ prior
26
over the parameters (see App.
D.9
). Using the factorization property
39
of MCM, Jeffreys’ prior and the model evidence factorize over the ICCs, and, using the completeness of the ICCs, we obtain a closed-form expression for the model evidence (see App.
D.10
):

Property 43
.

Evidence (or marginal likelihood) of MCMs.

The evidence of an MCM
\mathcal{M}=\cup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{M}_{a}
is the product of the evidence of each ICC when expressed in a preferred basis
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\cup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\,\boldsymbol{B}_{a}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\cup\overline{\boldsymbol{B}_{\mathcal{M}}}
of
\mathcal{M}
:

P(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}\,|\,\mathcal{M})=\frac{1}{q^{N(n-r)}}\prod_{a\in\mathcal{A}}P_{a}\big(\boldsymbol{B}_{a}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}})\,|\,\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\prime}\big)\,.

(132)

Using Jeffreys’ prior, the evidence of each ICC
\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\prime}
for the reduced dataset
\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}^{\prime}_{a}=\boldsymbol{B}_{a}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}})
is given by (see proof in App.
D.10
):

\displaystyle P_{a}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}^{\prime}_{a}\,|\,\mathcal{M}_{a}^{\prime})\;=\;\frac{\displaystyle\Gamma\left(\frac{q^{r_{a}}}{2}\right)}{\displaystyle\Gamma\left(N+\frac{q^{r_{a}}}{2}\right)}\;\prod_{\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}_{a}^{\prime}}\left(\frac{\displaystyle\,\Gamma\left(k_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}_{a}^{\prime})+\frac{1}{2}\right)}{\displaystyle\sqrt{\pi}}\right)\;,

(133)

where
\Gamma
denotes the gamma function. The product is over all the distinct states
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\nu}
observed in the reduced dataset
\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}_{a}^{\prime}
and
k_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}_{a}^{\prime})
is the number of times they occur. For
q=2
, we recover the result derived by Ref.
15
in the binary case.

The evidence of an ICC in Eq. (
133
) only depends on its rank
r_{a}
, on the empirical distribution of the reduced dataset
\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}^{\prime}_{a}=\boldsymbol{B}_{a}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}})
, and on the number
N
of datapoints. The evidence of a statistical model is usually expensive to compute from Eq. (
7
), due to the high-dimensional integral. Thanks to the closed-form expression (
133
), the evidence of any MCM can be computed efficiently with minimal computational resources, making it feasible in practice to perform model selection among MCMs on systems of reasonably large sizes (with hundreds of variables). In Sec.
6
, we apply this approach to real data.

Models can also be selected following the minimum description length (MDL) principle
44
;
43
;
21
, for which the best model is the one providing the most compressed representation of the data. Equation (
8
) gives the description length of a spin model for a sufficiently large number
N
of samples (expanded to order
O(1)
in
N
). The last term
c_{\mathcal{M}}
in the expansion is the geometric complexity of the model. It is defined as a high-dimensional integral (
A79
), which can be computed analytically for MCMs (see App.
D.7
).

Property 44
.

Geometric complexity of MCMs.
Thanks to the independence between ICCs, the geometric complexity of an MCM is given by the sum of the complexity of its ICCs:

\displaystyle c_{\mathcal{M}}=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\,c_{\mathcal{M}_{a}}\,,

(134)

where the complexity of an ICC only depends on its rank
r_{a}
and is given by (see proof in App.
D.7
):

\displaystyle c_{\mathcal{M}_{a}}=\frac{q^{r_{a}}}{2}\log\pi-\log\Gamma\left(\frac{q^{r_{a}}}{2}\right)\,,

(135)

where
\Gamma
is the gamma function. For
q=2
, one recovers the result obtained by
9
in the binary case.

Figure
11
shows the complexity landscape of MCMs for different values of
q
.
Observe a shift of the entire landscape towards larger complexities for increasing values of
q
, reflecting the increase in the number of parameters with
q
.
Note the two limit lines, sub-complete models (MCMs with a single ICC) are the simplest among all MCMs with the same number of parameters and rank, while IMCMs are the most complex.
For binary systems, Ref.
15
has hypothesized that classes of MCMs are the classes of lowest complexity among models with the same number of parameters and the same rank (hence their name). Although they verified that this is the case for systems with
n=4
spins or less, this hypothesis has not been proved in the more general case.
However, it is interesting to point out that MCMs likely span a large part of the complexity landscape of all
q
-state spin models, because they cover a large set of combinations of parameter number and rank. Indeed, at fixed number of parameters, it is expected that the higher the rank of the model (number of degrees of freedom of the model), the higher its complexity
9
.
In the binary case, Ref.
9
showed that the sub-complete models are the simplest among all models with the same number of parameters and rank, and the independent models (which are MCMs when
q=2
) are the most complex.

Figure 11:
(colors online)

Complexity
c_{\mathcal{M}}
of all MCMs with
n=4-5
spins for varying values of
q
.

For a given value of
q
, all the models with the same rank
r
and rank sequence
\{r_{a}\}_{a\in\mathcal{A}}
of their ICCs are equivalent. As a consequence, the number of classes of MCMs in an
n
-spin system is the same for all values of
q
. Here, one observes the shift in complexity of these classes as one increases the value of
q
.
The two dashed lines indicate the complexity of IMCMs (red) and the complexity of sub-complete models (black).

For a large number
N
of samples, the expansion of the negative log-evidence assuming Jeffreys’ prior matches the expansion of the description length
L(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}\,|\,\mathcal{M})
in Eq. (
8
)
5
;
38
. In App.
D.11
, we verify that this is indeed the case for the closed-form expression (
133
) of the log-evidence of MCMs and give the next order term of the expansion.
The advantage of using the closed-form expression (
133
) of the model evidence over the description length expansion (
8
) is that it is exact for any value of
N
and it is cheap to compute in practice.
In particular, it provides an accurate measure for comparing models when the number of datapoints is small or, more precisely, in undersampling cases.

5.5
Measuring data complexity with MCMs

The minimum description length (MDL) principle was introduced by Rissanen in the context of measuring the complexity of a noisy dataset. Without compression, a dataset with
n

q
-state discrete variables and
N
datapoints requires
N\times n
qits to be encoded, i.e., one qit per variable per datapoint, where one qit corresponds to a unit of information of
(\log q)
nats (we use the term “qit” for this unit by analogy to bit and trit).
Using a statistical model, redundant patterns in the data can be encoded more concisely (encoding the patterns one time instead of multiple times), which yields a compression of the data.
The resulting number of qits required to encode a dataset
\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}
using a statistical model
\mathcal{M}
is given by the description length
L(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}\,|\,\mathcal{M})
(taking the logarithm in base
q
).
The minimum description length achieved (i.e., maximal compression) within a family of statistical models is interpreted by Rissanen
44
as a measure of stochastic complexity of the data, by analogy with algorithmic definitions of complexity (such as Kolmogorov complexity).
Extending the parallel between the description length and the negative log-evidence to finite values of
N
, we propose to use the negative log-evidence of the best MCM as a measure of stochastic complexity of the data within the family of MCMs. In Sec.
6
, we give an example of the application of this quantity as a measure of data complexity.

Definition 34
.

Complexity of the data.

We propose to use the negative log-evidence of the best MCM (which is the MCM with the largest evidence) as a measure of stochastic complexity of the data within the family of MCMs:

\displaystyle{\rm COMP}_{MCM}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}})=-\max_{\mathcal{M}\in{\rm MCM}}\log P(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}\,|\,\mathcal{M})\,,

(136)

where the evidence
P(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}\,|\,\mathcal{M})
of an MCM is given in Property
43
.

6
Applications to real data

6.1
Search algorithms: optimization of the log-Evidence of MCMs

Despite the advantage provided by the closed-form expression for the evidence in Eq. (
133
), the number of MCMs is still large and in practice searching exhaustively for the best MCM among all remains computationally untractable for systems with more than ten variables.
Fortunately however, finding the best MCM (or near-optimal MCM) among those sharing a common
preferred basis
is computationally accessible, thanks to a direct mapping between these models and partitions of the basis variables.
This one-to-one correspondence with partitions allows for the development of efficient search algorithms beyond exhaustive enumeration.
In a given preferred basis, finding the best MCM corresponds to finding the factorization of the model probability distribution over the basis variables that best matches the structure of the data.
Ref.
1
likens finding such MCM factorization in the original basis of the data to a high-order community detection, and thus is interesting on its own.
To simplify the model selection procedure in the binary case, Ref.
15
also hypothesized that the best MCM overall would be obtained in the basis formed by the best independent model (i.e., the one with the largest evidence) and thus proposed to divide the selection procedure in two steps: first find the best independent model and use it as a new basis for the data, and then find an optimal factorization in this basis.
We follow a similar approach for the discrete case, focusing on two separate steps: searching for an optimal spin basis and searching for the best MCM in a chosen preferred basis.

6.1.1
Search for the optimal MCM in a given preferred basis

In a given
preferred
basis, each MCM maps to a partition of the spin variables into independent parts, independently of the value of
q
. This makes it possible to use the same search algorithms as in the binary case, with the only difference being that the model evidence is now computed using the more general formula (
133
).
Following Ref.
15
, we used two different algorithms: 1) an exhaustive search, going through all possible partitions of the
n
variables using a Gray code
16
;
28
, which can be used for small systems with up to about
15
variables; and 2) a greedy search, that starts from the independent model and successively merges the two ICCs that lead to the largest increase in evidence until the evidence reaches a maximum, which can be used for larger systems (our current implementation
14
can be used with up to 128 variables).
Ref
14
also implements two other search algorithms not used in this paper, a simulated annealing search and a greedy search using divisive hierarchical clustering.

6.1.2
Search for an optimal basis

In the binary case, Ref.
15
proposes to use the best independent model with
n
operators as a preferred basis for the MCM search. The
n
independent operators of this model correspond to the spin basis in which the empirical probability distribution is the most likely to factorize over each variable as if there were independent.
We extend this idea to the more general discrete case, by searching for a spin basis in which the empirical probability distribution is most likely to factorize. In other words, we search for the best IMCM for the data and any basis for that IMCM equally satisfies the requirement above.
Using any of these bases as the preferred basis for the MCM search (in the previous section) will give the same set of MCMs to choose from (because ICCs always include all possible multiples of the basis operators). In the binary case, an IMCM is (also) an independent model, and we recover the approach proposed in
15
.

The best IMCM with
n
ICCs (each of rank
1
) is defined as the one with the largest evidence.
Because all IMCMs with
n
ICCs are in the same equivalence class, the model with the largest evidence among them is also the one that has the largest maximum likelihood. Using Eq. (
130
), the maximum log-likelihood of an IMCM with
n
ICCs based on the spin basis
\boldsymbol{B}=(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\,\dots,\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n})
(where each
\boldsymbol{B}_{a}=(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{a})
is a basis of one of the ICC)
is given by:

\displaystyle\log P(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}\,|\,\boldsymbol{\hat{g}},\mathcal{M}_{\rm imcm})=-N\,\sum_{a=1}^{n}S_{\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}}[\alpha^{\prime}_{a}]\,,\;\;\;{\rm where}\;S_{\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}}[\alpha^{\prime}_{a}]=-\sum_{\alpha_{a}^{\prime}=0}^{q-1}\,\frac{k_{\alpha_{a}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}})}{N}\log\left(\frac{k_{\alpha_{a}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}})}{N}\right)

(137)

is the Shannon entropy of the discrete variable
\alpha_{a}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{a}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}
in the dataset
\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}
.
Here, the entropy of the reduced dataset
S(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}_{a}^{\prime})
appearing in Eq. (
131
) was re-written as
S(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}_{a}^{\prime})=S_{\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}}[\alpha^{\prime}_{a}]
because the vector
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{B}_{a}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=(\alpha_{a}^{\prime})
has only one variable.
We used the
n
independent operators
\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}\}
to define the new basis variables
\alpha_{a}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{a}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}
for all
a\in\{1,\,\dots,\,n\}
(see Definition
17
), and each term
k_{\alpha_{a}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}})
denotes the number of times
\alpha_{a}^{\prime}
takes a given value
\alpha_{a}^{\prime}\in\{0,\,\dots,\,q-1\}
in the dataset
\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}
(or equivalently the number of times the basis variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{B}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=(\alpha_{a}^{\prime})
takes a given value in the transformed and reduced dataset
\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}_{a}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{B}_{a}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}})
).
Because each state of
\alpha_{a}^{\prime}
corresponds to a state of the operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{a}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
,
S_{\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}}[\alpha^{\prime}_{a}]
is also the Shannon entropy of the operator
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{a}
in the data:
S_{\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}}[\alpha^{\prime}_{a}]=S_{\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}}[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{a}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})]
.
Equation (
137
) therefore implies that the most likely IMCM is given by the set of the
n
least entropic independent operators in the data
33
33

33

Note that for
q>2
, finding the most likely IMCM is different from finding the most likely independent model. An independent model with
n
operators can be written as a set of
n
independent components with one operator in each component, but these components are not complete. This means that, for an independent model, the Kullback-Leibler divergence between the single operator and the data within each independent component is not zero (contrary to IMCMs). This term would then have to be taken into account in the computation of the maximum likelihood of the independent model.
. Exactly as in the binary case, finding the most likely IMCM corresponds to finding the set of
n
independent operators with the lowest entropy. The only difference is that now there are multiple bases of operators that correspond to the same IMCM and thus work equivalently as a preferred basis (they identify the same set of MCMs on that basis).

To find these operators for small systems (with
n\lesssim 15
), we extend the exhaustive algorithm of Ref.
15
. We compute the entropy of all the operators of the system (excluding certain operators, listed below) and create an
(n\times K)
-matrix containing in columns the discrete representations
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}
of the
K
least entropic operators, ordered from low to high entropy. The elements of this matrix are in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})
. Identifying the
n
least entropic independent operators then consists of finding the independent columns in this matrix starting from the left-hand side. This can be done using Gaussian elimination in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})
: the columns with leading coefficients identify the set of least entropic independent operators.
For
q
non-prime, some interactions are “not invertible” and are directly excluded from this procedure; these are the interactions
\boldsymbol{\mu}
for which there exists a coefficient
c\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})
such that
c\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{0}
in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
(see Sec.
4.1
).
Moreover for
q>2
, all the considered (invertible) operators
\boldsymbol{\mu}
have a non-identical complex conjugate
-\boldsymbol{\mu}
; conjugate operators have the same empirical entropy and for the preferred basis it doesn’t matter if one uses
\boldsymbol{\mu}
or
-\boldsymbol{\mu}
as a basis element because they identify the same preferred basis
34
34

34

It is reassuring that c.c. operators have the same entropy, because they identify the same IMCM. Different entropies would give different values of maximum likelihood (
137
) to the same IMCM depending on which operator is used in the basis, which would be problematic.

(any partition of the basis elements into ICCs will give the same MCM independently of using
\boldsymbol{\mu}
or
-\boldsymbol{\mu}
as a basis element).
Therefore, only one element from each conjugate pair is included in the procedure (in our implementation
14
, this is done by keeping only the interaction vectors
\boldsymbol{\mu}
whose first entry is smaller or equal to
q/2
).
Similarly, we can also exclude from the selection all multiples of the already selected operators,
because they identify different bases of the same IMCMs
35
35

35

Any partition of the basis elements into ICCs will give the same MCM independently of which operator multiple is used, because ICCs always include all possible multiples of the basis operators.
. This wasn’t implemented, but would improve further the speed of the code.

With this exhaustive approach, the number of operators to consider grows exponentially as
q^{n}
, making this algorithm unusable in practice for systems of more than
25
variables.
For many real systems, it seems reasonable to assume that the least entropic operators will be of low order (i.e., of order 1, 2, 3, or 4), and one could reduce the exhaustive search to consider operators up to a fixed order
k
only.
In the binary case, Ref.
15
also proposes an iterative algorithm that can consider operators of any order while keeping the computational time similar to fixing
k
. This algorithm can be easily extended to larger values of
q
; it is available in
14
but not used in this paper.

Figure 12:
MCM analysis of the US Supreme Court data in its original basis for different embeddings.

The original dataset is binary and is embedded in the larger
q
-state space for this analysis.
