Optimal MCM found by exhaustive search in the original basis of the data for different values of
q
.
The circles represent the 9 justices labeled by their initials: Ruth Bader Ginsburg (RG), John P. Stevens (JS), David Souter (DS), Stephen Breyer (SB), Sandra Day O’Connor (SO), William Rehnquist (WR), Anthony Kennedy (AK), Clarence Thomas (CT), and Antonin Scalia (AS).
MCMs are represented as partitions of the basis variables, with components identified by different colors and delimited by dashed lines. The best MCMs for
q=2
and
q=3
correspond to the same partition. The value of the log-evidence is indicated for each component.
The log-evidence of the MCM (reported in Table
2
) is the sum of the values of its components.

Bottom.

Scatter plot of the log-evidence of MCMs for all possible partitions of the basis variables, for
q=2
(along the horizontal axis) against, respectively,
q=3
,
4
and
5
(along the vertical axis).
Each dot corresponds to one partition, whose color indicates the number of parts, i.e., the number
n_{ICCs}
of ICCs of the corresponding MCMs. In each plot, the partition corresponding to the MCM with the largest log-evidence is identified by a red circle for each
q
; they are the best MCMs shown in the top panel. The grey circles indicate the partitions corresponding to the best MCMs for smaller values of
q
.
The log-evidence of MCMs corresponding to the same partition decreases with
q
, due to the increase in complexity penalty.
In particular, no partition lies above the
(x=y)
-line (indicated by the shaded area) and the vertical distance between this line and a dot corresponds to the increase in penalty in the log-evidence when changing
q
from
2
to, respectively,
3
,
4
, or
5
for the corresponding partition.
MCMs with ICCs of the same sizes are penalized by the same amount and corresponds to aligned dots parallel to the
(x=y)
-line. For the MCMs with
n_{ICCs}=2
(orange dots), we indicated the size of the ICCs near the alignment: for example “
[4,5]
” denotes MCMs with one ICC of size 4 and one of size 5.
Models with fewer ICCs have a larger average ICC size and thus get a stronger penalty with increasing
q
(see orange dots moving faster down than other dots from one panel to the next).

6.2
Code availability and reproducibility

The codes for all three algorithms can be found in our Python package in Ref.
14

(C++ codes with Python bindings).
A notable difference with respect to the codes for binary MCMs provided by Ref.
15
is that datapoints and operators cannot be easily encoded by binary integers anymore. This is because the state vectors
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
and the interaction vectors
\boldsymbol{\mu}
are not necessarily binary, but more generally defined in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
.
To avoid losing efficiency, we encoded these vectors using several 128-bit integers to encode 128
q
-state variables (i.e., each
q
-state variable is encoded by
\log_{2}(q)
bits). This is to compensate for the lack of built-in data structure to encode integers in base
q
in our chosen language (C++) but also in most common programming languages.

As a proof of concept, we revisit two datasets previously analyzed with MCMs in the binary setting
15
.
We study the impact of the choices made for how to discretize the data and of the resolution
q
of discretization on the structure of the resulting MCM.

6.3
Example 1: binary dataset with artificially increased values of
q
, embedded in a larger space

For illustration purposes, we first analyze a binary dataset using MCMs with varying values of
q
, as if the (binary) variables were taking values in
[0,q-1]
. The dataset comes from the US Supreme Court database
49
and consists of the votes of the
n=9
justices on
N=895
cases debated during the second Rehnquist Court.
For
q=2
, voting outcomes were labeled as in Ref.
32
using the political inclination (if known) of each debated case, such that a judge casts a vote
\alpha_{j}=1
if the decision is conservative-oriented, or
\alpha_{j}=0
if it is liberal-oriented. Although the datapoints are binary, one can embed them into the larger state space
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
, effectively increasing the number of possible configurations while keeping the observed states unchanged. Embedding the data into a larger space is common in data analysis. For instance, when using principal component analysis on discrete data, the data is analyzed as if the variables were taking real values even though they are discrete.

6.3.1
Embedding choice

For
q>2
, to reflect the opposite political valence between conservative and liberal oriented votes, we gave opposite values of
\alpha_{j}
to opposite votes, assigning
\alpha_{j}=1
to liberal-oriented votes and
\alpha_{j}=-1\,(\bmod{q})=(q-1)
to conservative-oriented votes. This choice confers conjugate spin values
s_{j}=\exp(2 \I\pi\alpha_j/q)
to these two states. All the other states of
\alpha_{j}
are not observed in the data.
The specific choice of mapping of
\alpha_{j}
from binary to discrete values will not impact the outcome of the best MCM in the original basis of the data (compared to any other permutations of the values of
\alpha_{j}
).
However, it can impact the interactions selected in the best basis (and the interactions of the best spin model overall) and change the interpretation of what a given interaction means (see Sec.
2.4
on interpreting
the interactions).
Intuitively, we expect that labeling the discrete data in a way that best reflects the meaning associated with the values taken by the variables in the studied system would make it easier to interpret the uncovered interactions.
On the other hand, assigning the values at random can make it more difficult to interpret the model.
Overall, the final result from the modeling procedure (i.e., the interpreted model) should be consistent across mapping choices. In other words, the uncovered models for different choices should be related mathematically by the (bijective) transformation of variables associated with the different mapping choices).

In the following, we first analyze the impact of the embedding on the optimal MCM factorization in the original basis of the data, and then on the overall best MCM.

6.3.2
Optimal MCM factorization in the original basis

Figure
12
Top shows the best MCMs found by the exhaustive search in the original basis of the data for varying values of
q
. For
q=3
,
we found the same factorization as in the binary case
15
, with the five conservative-oriented justices grouped in one ICC and the four more liberal-oriented justices grouped in a second ICC.
Further increasing
q
breaks these components into smaller ICCs,
first splitting the conservative group at
q=4
and then the liberal group at
q=5
.
The reduction of the ICC sizes with larger
q
can be understood intuitively. For larger values of
q
, ICCs based on the same number
r_{a}
of variables require more parameters (precisely,
q^{r_{a}}-1
parameters) and are thus more penalized. Because the observed states remain unchanged,
we therefore expect the ICCs of the best MCM to get smaller as we artificially increase
q
.

Formally, consider a dataset with variables taking values
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q_{1}\mathbb{Z})^{n}
,
and consider two MCMs,
\mathcal{M}^{q_{1}}
and
\mathcal{M}^{q_{2}}
, corresponding to the same partition of the
n
basis variables but for two different values of
q
, respectively
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
with
q_{2}>q_{1}
.
For these models, the log-evidence takes the form (using
r=n
in Eq. (
132
) and (
133
)):

\displaystyle\log P(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}\,|\,\mathcal{M})=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\left[\log\frac{\displaystyle\Gamma\left(\frac{q^{r_{a}}}{2}\right)}{\displaystyle\Gamma\left(N+\frac{q^{r_{a}}}{2}\right)}+\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}\in\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}_{a}}\log\left(\frac{\displaystyle\,\Gamma\left(k_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}_{a})+\frac{1}{2}\right)}{\displaystyle\sqrt{\pi}}\right)\right]\;.

(138)

Here,
\mathcal{A}
denotes the common partition identifying both
\mathcal{M}^{q_{1}}
and
\mathcal{M}^{q_{2}}
, such that each element
a\in\mathcal{A}
identifies an ICC in each model based on the same groups of variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}
.
Because the dataset itself remains unchanged (with states
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q_{1}\mathbb{Z})^{n}
) and because the two MCMs correspond to the same partition of the basis variables, the second sum in Eq. (
138
) takes the same value for both models. Only the complexity part (first term within the first sum) changes with
q
, becoming more negative for larger
q
at fixed value of
r_{a}
.
Figure
12
Bottom illustrates how this increase in penalty with
q
impacts different models.
MCMs with ICCs of the same sizes
\{r_{a}\}
(which belong to the same complexity class)
are penalized by the same amount as one increases
q
(see the alignment of the dots parallel to the
(x=y)
-line)
and the penalty increases faster with
q
for MCMs with larger average ICC size (i.e. with a smaller number of ICCs). These effects can also be deduced from the dependence in
\{r_{a}\}
of the complexity term in Eq. (
138
).
As a result, the best MCM can structurally change when increasing
q
, favoring models with smaller ICCs (see changing location of the red circle towards MCMs with smaller ICCs).

6.3.3
Best MCM factorization overall

Figure
13
shows the best MCM found overall for different values of
q
, by performing first an exhaustive search for the optimal basis, and then an exhaustive search for the optimal MCM on that basis.

The nine independent operators of the best basis are represented by squares in the figure. Except for one single spin operator on AS, all these operators are pairwise and identify interactions between the same pairs of justices for all values of
q
. In the binary case,
15
reported that these
9
interactions alone account for
86\%
of the information extracted by a fully connected pairwise model with
45
interactions. A notable difference with the binary case, is that these pairwise operators are of the form
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{ij}=(1,\,q-1)
between two justices
i
and
j
, and not of the form
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{ij}=(1,\,1)
as one could have naively extrapolated from the binary case (where
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{ij}
denotes the interaction vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}
reduced to the
i
-th and
j
-th entries).
This is because the most frequent pattern between these pairs of justices is the symmetric voting pattern
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{j}
, which is a pattern that is best captured by this type of pairwise interaction (see the explanations in Example
7
of Sec.
2.4.2
).
Indeed, the justices SO and SB, whose interaction is the most entropic pairwise interaction in the basis, vote identically in
78\%
of the cases, and AS and CT, whose pairwise interaction is the least entropic interaction among all, vote identically in
93\%
of the cases.
Note that the choice of embedding doesn’t impact the best basis in this case, because the symmetric patterns
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{j}
identified by the best basis remain unchanged under permutation of the state values.
In Fig.
13
, the basis operators are numbered from the least to the most entropic. Observe that this ordering changes slightly from
q=2
to
q=3
(see the operators ranked 7 and 8, and 2 and 3).
This is because for
q=2
the entropy of an operator only depends on the probability of the operator to be even (symmetric state
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{j}
) or odd (antisymmetric states
\alpha_{i}=-\alpha_{j}
), while for
q\geq 3
the entropy of a pairwise operator also depends on the relative occurrence of the non-symmetric states.

The ICCs of the best MCMs found on the optimal basis for each
q
are identified by the triangles in Fig.
13
.
These MCMs have ICCs of smaller sizes (on average) than those found in the original basis for the same value of
q
(shown in Fig.
12
), which means that they correspond to less complex models (with fewer interactions). The log-evidence of these models (see table
2
) is also larger than that of the MCMs based on the original basis. This means that they are better models for the data, which is what we expected from the two-step procedure.
Similarly to the analysis in the original basis, ICCs are smaller for larger
q
, which can be explained by the increase in penalty with
q
while the data patterns remain mostly unchanged. ICCs for larger
q
also seem to lie mostly within the ICCs for smaller
q
, although this doesn’t hold for all ICCs.

Figure 13:
Factor graph representation of the best MCM found by exhaustive search for the US Supreme Court dataset for different values of
q
.

The circles represent the original variables, which correspond to the 9 justices labeled by their initials as in Fig.
12
.
The squares represent the operators of the best basis found by exhaustive search, numbered from low to high entropy.
For each basis operators (squares), the colored lines indicate which “justice” variables (circles) are included in the operator and the color specifies the power to which each variable is raised: one in blue, two in red, three in green, and four in orange. For example, for
q=3
, the basis operator of lowest entropy (square with number “
0
”) is the operator
\phi_{0}(\boldsymbol{s})=s^{\,}_{CT}\,s^{2}_{AS}
.
The triangles represent the ICCs of the best MCM, a line from an operator to a component identify the presence of that operator in the component.

6.4
Example 2:
Choice of discretization and standardization of discrete data.

In this second example, we analyze answers to the Big Five Personality Test
18
. In particular, we discuss possible choices of discretization schemes and their impact on the best MCM factorization in the original basis.

The test consists of
n=50
questions designed to probe the personality of the respondents along five dimensions generally considered as describing the main traits of personality: extraversion, emotional stability, agreeableness, conscientiousness, and openness to experience.
Each trait is measured based on the answers to ten statements on a scale of
x=1
(disagree) to
5
(agree). For example, extraversion is probed by statements such as
“I am the life of the party”
or
“I am quiet around strangers”
.
We obtained a dataset from the Open-Source Psychometrics Project
4
with answers from
N=602\,591
individuals (after cleaning for errors and identical IP addresses).
The dataset was then converted from the original 1-to-5 scale to the desired discrete formats, with respectively
q=2
,
3
, or
5
, using two different discretization schemes.

In the following, we only perform the MCM analysis in the original basis of the data. This is because we are mainly interested in studying the impact of different choices of discretization of the data and the original variables were purposely chosen in the design of the Big Five test.
As a side note, in the binarized version,
15
found that the best MCM in the best basis happened to be is identical to the best MCM found in the original basis.

6.4.1
Discretization Scheme 1: direct mapping

For the first scheme, we re-assign the values of the variables
x_{j}\in\{1,\,2,\,3,\,4,\,5\}
in the original data (where
x_{j}
denotes the answer to the
j
-th question) to new discrete values
\alpha_{j}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})
for
q=2,\,3
and
5
, as described in Table
1
Scheme 1.
To respect the opposite valence between positive and negative answers, we associated the “neutral” answer to
\alpha_{j}=0
and gave opposite values
36
36

36

corresponding to conjugate values of
s_{j}=\exp(2 i \pi\alpha_j/q)

to opposite answers (e.g.,
\alpha_{j}=1
to “agree” and
\alpha_{j}=-1\,({\rm mod}\,q)=(q-1)
to “disagree”).
Choosing this assignment of
\alpha_{j}
doesn’t affect the outcome of the best MCM with respect to selecting any other permutations of the values of
\alpha_{j}
(because permuting the values of
\alpha_{j}
doesn’t change the numbers
k_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}}
of occurrence of the states used in Eq. (
138
)).
However, as mentioned previously, if the final goal is to obtain a more precise high-order spin model for the data (e.g., beyond MCMs), then different choices can lead to different optimal models, and a choice informed by domain knowledge can make it easier to interpret the uncovered model.

Figure
14
shows the best MCMs found in the original basis of the data for Scheme 1.
From
q=2
to
q=3
, the sizes of the ICCs remain large with 9 to 10 variables: the dataset is sufficiently rich at
q=3
for the balance between fitting and complexity to require ICCs of a similar sizes than at
q=2
(contrary to Example 1). In other words, the increase in size of the state space is compensated by an increase in richness of the data.
From
q=3
to
q=5
, the ICCs break down into smaller components.
Although questions are mostly grouped into ICCs corresponding to the same traits, we also find mismatches (especially for larger
q
).
Additionally, we observe that some statements are more biased than others towards agreeing or disagreeing (e.g., “I insult others”), which may give rise to spurious correlations. The next scheme will attempt to reduce this effect by standardizing the data.

6.4.2
Discretization Scheme 2: standardization

In the second scheme, the value of each variable
x_{j}
was re-centered around its empirical mean
\bar{x_{j}}
following Scheme 2 in Table
1
.
Besides, for
q=3
and
5
, we defined small intervals on the values of
x_{j}
of size
2\sigma_{j}\,\epsilon
within which
\alpha_{j}
takes a constant value, where
\sigma_{j}
denotes the standard deviation of
x_{j}
.
The value of
\epsilon
was then adjusted to maximize the entropy of the variables in the data (see Fig.
A2
in Appendix).
Here as well, we reflected the opposite valence of the data between positive and negative answers by giving opposite values of
\alpha_{j}
to opposite intervals in
x_{j}
.
The introduction of Scheme 2 is motivated by the observation that some statements are significantly more biased than others. For example, the answer to the statement “
I insult others
” is more often negative,
with
62\%
of the respondents who disagree and only
21\%
who agree (
\bar{x}_{j}\simeq 2.27
).
However, such patterns are inherent to the choice of the question,
and are not directly informative about personality.
Instead, one may understand personality from the way respondents answer with respect to the average behavior in the population.
In this context, it would be more interesting to model patterns between answers based on their deviation from the population average instead of raw dependencies.
We achieved this in Scheme 2 by standardizing each variable and maximizing their entropy before modeling them, so that individual variables carry as little information as possible.
For example, staying “neutral” for the statement “
I insult others
” (
17\%
of the respondents) tends to be more negative than the majority of people, and recentering the data will then move “neutral” answers to the negative side.
Similarly, the statement “
I spend time reflecting on things
” has the lowest entropy (see Fig.
A2
in Appendix), with
48\%
of the respondents “strongly agreeing“ and
34\%
“agreeing“ (
\bar{x}_{j}\simeq 4.22
); recentering the data will then give “neutral“ answers to respondents “agreeing“ in their response.

Figure
14
shows the best MCMs found in the original basis of the data for Scheme 2. They reveal a clearer separation of the five traits than in Scheme 1, in particular for
q=3
and
5
.
In this example, reducing the bias of the variables allows us to better focus the model on the (high-order) correlation structure of the data,
following a principle similar to using
z
-scored variables in Principal Component Analysis and revealing in this case a clearer separation between the traits.
We note that the standardization of the data is not a requirement of the technique,
but is a specific choice that can be made depending on what type of dependencies one is interested in modeling (i.e., row dependencies or correlations).

7
Discussion

In this work, we introduced a complete family of maximum entropy models designed to model any high-order patterns of discrete data.
This comprehensive framework is capable of capturing the full complexity of multivariate discrete systems. In contrast to the version of the Potts models typically used in statistical inference (see Eq. (
29
)), our models avoid over-parametrization and are able to capture high-order patterns. Focusing strictly on the pairwise case, for example, the maximum number of parameters used corresponds exactly to the number of pairwise patterns that can be fitted to the data. Furthermore, mapping these maximum entropy models to discrete Fourier analysis not only confirms that they represent a complete model family for discrete data, but also allows us to straightforwardly invert the parameters from empirical frequencies via a discrete Fourier transform.

A key analytical result of our framework is the cluster expansion of the partition function of
q
-state spin models, which we express in terms of the loops of the models. In practice, these loops can be extracted easily using the matrix representation of spin models. Furthermore, the cluster expansion of the partition function establishes a direct connection between the statistical properties of the models and the algebraic properties of their matrix representation. This transition to a linear algebraic perspective facilitates the manipulation of spin models and allows for a rigorous characterization of statistical constraints, model complexity, and equivalence classes. In particular, we extended the framework of equivalence classes of spin models by generalizing the notion of gauge transformations (GTs) to the discrete setting. We showed that GTs provide a more general definition of symmetries in discrete data, extending well beyond standard spatial reflections, rotations, or permutations. More importantly, GTs enable the development of model selection procedures that are entirely independent of the original basis representation of the data. This opens the way for equivariant modeling, allowing systems to be analyzed based on their inherent symmetries rather than arbitrary representational choices.

While the family of
q
-state spin models has a super-exponential number of models, we make the framework more widely usable in practice by focusing on a sub-family of models, the minimally complex models (MCMs). MCMs operate essentially as factorization models: they allow us to find a factorization of the multivariate probability distribution that optimally matches the underlying structure of the data by carefully balancing goodness-of-fit against complexity. The benefit of working with MCMs is that all quantities of interest have a closed-form expression allowing for efficient model selection in reasonably large systems. Moreover, the family of MCMs is invariant under GTs enabling equivariant model selection within this family. We demonstrated the efficacy of this approach through real-world applications. In particular, we discussed the importance of the choice of the discretization scheme and illustrated how they affect the resulting models in our examples.
To ensure broad accessibility, we have also released an open-source package
14
capable of analyzing systems with up to
128
discrete variables.

Looking forward, interesting research directions are the development of more efficient search algorithms within the space of MCMs for scaling the analysis to larger systems, as well as extending our general modeling framework to continuous and non-stationary data. In particular, extending the MCM approach will broaden the scope of optimal factorization techniques to an even wider array of complex systems.
Another interesting idea is the use of generalized symmetries of discrete data and spin models (thanks to the definition of gauge transformations) for the development of equivariant model selection.

Appendices

Appendix A
q
-state spin models

A.1
From complex to real Hamiltonian.

Proof of Eq. (
15
).

Let us denote
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=A_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}+iB_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
. We recall that
\phi^{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})]^{*}
. For
p(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g})
in Eq. (
12
) to be real, one needs that, for all
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
:

\displaystyle{\rm Im}\left[g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})+g_{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\phi^{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right]

\displaystyle=0

(A1)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\qquad(B_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}+B_{-\boldsymbol{\mu}})\cos\bigg(\frac{2\pi}{q} \state\cdot\vecmu\bigg)+(A_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}-A_{-\boldsymbol{\mu}})\sin\bigg(\frac{2\pi}{q} \state\cdot\vecmu\bigg)

\displaystyle=0\,.

(A2)

Because
\cos\left(\frac{2\pi}{q}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}\right)
and
\sin\left(\frac{2\pi}{q}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}\right)
are orthogonal vectors of
\mathbb{R}[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]
, this latter equation is equivalent to
A_{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}=A_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
and
B_{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}=-B_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
, which gives
g_{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}=g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}
.
∎

Proof: re-writting Eq. (
12
) under the form of (
16
).

Let us denote
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=A_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}+iB_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=g_{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}
. We have that
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})+i\psi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
, where
\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\cos(2\pi\state\cdot\vecmu/q)
and
\psi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\sin(2\pi\state\cdot\vecmu/q)
, and that
\phi^{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})]^{*}
. Grouping conjugate terms together for all
\boldsymbol{\mu}
such that
-\boldsymbol{\mu}\neq\boldsymbol{\mu}
, the Hamiltonian in Eq. (
12
) takes the form:

\displaystyle H(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}=-\boldsymbol{\mu}}g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})+\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}<-\boldsymbol{\mu}}\left(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})+g^{*}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\phi^{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right)\,,

(A3)

where we used the notation
\boldsymbol{\mu}<-\boldsymbol{\mu}
to ensure that the second sum includes each conjugate pair only once. For
-\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\mu}
,
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
and, to match Eq. (
12
) to Eq. (
16
), we define the parameter
a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=2g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
.
For
-\boldsymbol{\mu}\neq\boldsymbol{\mu}
, we compute the local Hamiltonian:

\displaystyle H^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})+g^{*}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\phi^{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=2A_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})-2B_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\psi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\,,

(A4)

from which we define
a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=2A_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
and
b_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=-2B_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
to match Eq. (
16
), i.e.
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=\frac{a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}-ib_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}{2}
.
∎

A.2
Orthogonality of complex spin operators.

Proof of Eq. (
39
):

for all
(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu})\in[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]^{2}
:

\displaystyle\langle\phi^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\,|\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q^{n}}\,\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\,[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})]^{*}=\frac{1}{q^{n}}\,\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\,\phi^{-\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\,,

(A5)

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\frac{1}{q^{n}}\,\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}-\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\overset{(b)}{=}\frac{1}{q^{n}}\,\times\,\begin{cases}\;\;q^{n}\qquad&{\rm if}\;\;\boldsymbol{\mu}-\boldsymbol{\nu}=\boldsymbol{0}\,,\\
\;\;0\qquad&{\rm otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\langle\phi^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\,|\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\delta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu}}\,.

where
\phi^{-\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})]^{*}
is the inverse of
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
in
\Omega
.
For (a), we used the closure property of
\Omega
in Eq. (
34
), and for (b)
we used the property Eq. (
36
).
∎

A.3
Orthogonality of real spin operators in
\Omega_{\rm R}
.

We denote by
\mathbb{R}[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]
the vector space of real functions on
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
endowed with the following inner product:
for two real functions
f(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
and
h(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
,

\displaystyle\langle\,f\,|\,h\,\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q^{n}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}}f(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\,h(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\,,

(A6)

Applying the scalar products for any two operators of the forms
\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\cos\left(\frac{2\pi}{q}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}\right)
and
\psi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\sin\left(\frac{2\pi}{q}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}\right)
, one gets that:
for all
(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu})\in[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]^{2}
:

\displaystyle\left\langle\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\big|\,\psi^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\right\rangle=0\;,\qquad\left\langle\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\big|\,\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\right\rangle=\frac{\delta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu}}+\delta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},-\boldsymbol{\nu}}}{2}\qquad{\rm and}\qquad\left\langle\psi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\big|\,\psi^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\right\rangle=\frac{\delta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu}}-\delta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},-\boldsymbol{\nu}}}{2}

(A7)

The set
\Omega_{R}
of real spin operators (
42
) is a set of
q^{n}
orthogonal elements of
\mathbb{R}[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]
, which is a vector space of dimension
q^{n}
. The set
\Omega_{R}
thus forms a basis of
\mathbb{R}[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]
.

Proof.

Eq. (
A7
) can be proven easily using trigonometric formulae and Eq. (
37
):

\displaystyle\left\langle\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\big|\,\psi^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q^{n}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}}\cos\left(\frac{2\pi}{q}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}\right)\,\sin\left(\frac{2\pi}{q}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\,,

(A8)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{1}{q^{n}}\bigg[\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}}\sin\left(\frac{2\pi}{q}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot(\boldsymbol{\mu}+\boldsymbol{\nu})\right)-\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}}\sin\left(\frac{2\pi}{q}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot(\boldsymbol{\mu}-\boldsymbol{\nu})\right)\bigg]

(A9)

\displaystyle=0\,.

(A10)

\displaystyle\left\langle\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\big|\,\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q^{n}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}}\cos\left(\frac{2\pi}{q}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}\right)\,\cos\left(\frac{2\pi}{q}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\,,

(A11)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{1}{q^{n}}\,\bigg[\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}}\cos\left(\frac{2\pi}{q}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot(\boldsymbol{\mu}+\boldsymbol{\nu})\right)+\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}}\cos\left(\frac{2\pi}{q}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot(\boldsymbol{\mu}-\boldsymbol{\nu})\right)\bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[\delta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},-\boldsymbol{\nu}}+\delta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu}}\right]\,.

(A12)

The proof can be done the same way for the last identity,
using that
\sin a\sin b=(\cos(a-b)-\cos(a+b))/2
.
∎

A.4
Fourier coefficients and inverse Fourier transform.

Proof of Eq. (
46
):

Starting from Eq. (
40
) for the log-probability:

\displaystyle\log P(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}}g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\;,

(A13)

we take the inner product with the operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
for a given
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
:

\displaystyle\left\langle\phi^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\mid\log P\right\rangle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}}g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\left\langle\phi^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\mid\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right\rangle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}}g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\delta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu}}=g_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\,,

(A14)

where we used that spin operators are orthogonal (
39
).
∎

Proof of Eq. (
47
):

We start from Eq. (
16
) for the log-probability, written in terms of the variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
(with
a_{\boldsymbol{0}}=-\log Z(\boldsymbol{g})
):

\displaystyle\log P(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\leq-\boldsymbol{\mu}}\left(a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})+b_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\psi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right)\,,

(A15)

where the notation
\boldsymbol{\mu}\leq-\boldsymbol{\mu}
prevents including twice the same conjugate pair in the sum, and where
b_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=0
for all
\boldsymbol{\mu}
such that
-\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\mu}
. We take the inner product with the operator
\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
for a given
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
:

\displaystyle\left\langle\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\mid\log P\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\leq-\boldsymbol{\mu}}\bigg[a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\langle\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\,|\,\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\rangle+b_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\big\langle\varphi^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\,|\,\psi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\big\rangle\bigg]=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\leq-\boldsymbol{\mu}}a_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\frac{\delta_{\boldsymbol{\nu},\boldsymbol{\mu}}+\delta_{\boldsymbol{\nu},-\boldsymbol{\mu}}}{2}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\,a_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\qquad\quad&{\rm for\quad}\boldsymbol{\nu}=-\boldsymbol{\nu}\\[2.0pt]
\,a_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}/2\qquad\quad&{\rm for\quad}\boldsymbol{\nu}\neq-\boldsymbol{\nu}\\
\end{cases}

(A16)

where we used the orthogonality between real spin operators (
A7
).
Similarly, the relation for
b_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
in Eq. (
47
) can be obtained by taking the inner product of
\log P(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
with
\psi^{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
.
∎

Proof of Eq. (
48
).

There’s nothing to prove, as this equation directly comes the initial decomposition of
\log P
over the Fourier basis in Eq. (
40
). However, it can be interesting to show how to go back from the Fourier coefficients (
46
) to
\log P
using inner products. Starting from Eq. (
46
) for the coefficients:

\displaystyle G(\boldsymbol{\mu})=g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=\frac{1}{q^{n}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}}\log P(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime})\,[\phi_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\mu})]^{*}\;,

(A17)

where we introduced the functions
\phi_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{\mu})\doteq\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
for all
\boldsymbol{\mu}
and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
. Let’s take the inner product of
G
with the operator
\phi_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
for a given state
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
:

\displaystyle\left\langle G^{*}\mid\phi_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\right\rangle=\frac{1}{q^{n}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}}\log P(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime})\,\left\langle\phi_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}}\mid\phi_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\right\rangle=\frac{1}{q^{n}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}}\log P(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime})\,\delta_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=\frac{1}{q^{n}}\log P(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\,,

where we used the completeness property (
44
) of spin operators.
∎

A.5
Entropy of models with a single interaction

Take a
q
-state spin model for
n
variables with a single interaction
\boldsymbol{\mu}
encoded by the operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
(and its c.c. if
\boldsymbol{\mu}\neq-\boldsymbol{\mu}
). For all states
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, the operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=z_{q}^{\boldsymbol{\mu}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
only takes values in the
q
-th roots of unity (corresponding to
q
locations on the unit circle). Besides, if
q
is prime or if for all integer
c\in[0,q-1]
the vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}
verifies that
c\boldsymbol{\mu}\neq\boldsymbol{0}
in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
, then there are the same number of states at each root (i.e.,
q^{n-1}
states).
For a given
j\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})
, all the states
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
for which
\boldsymbol{\mu}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}=j\,
have the same interaction energy (
51
),
H^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=-2\,\vec{g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}}\cdot\vec{z_{q}}^{j}
, where
\vec{z_{q}}^{j}
is the direction of the root
z_{q}^{j}
in the complex plane, and the same probability:

\displaystyle P(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,|\,\{\boldsymbol{\mu}\},\,g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})=\frac{p_{j}}{q^{n-1}}\,,\qquad{\rm where}\qquad p_{j}=\frac{e^{\,2\,\vec{g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}}\cdot\vec{z_{q}}^{j}}}{Z_{q}}\qquad{\rm and}\qquad Z_{q}=\sum_{j=0}^{q-1}e^{\,2\,\vec{g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}}\cdot\vec{z_{q}}^{j}}

(A18)

is a normalization factor.
The probability that the operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
takes the value
z_{q}^{j}
is then:

\displaystyle P[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=z_{q}^{j}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}:\;\boldsymbol{\mu}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}=j}P(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,|\,\{\boldsymbol{\mu}\},\,g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})=q^{n-1}\frac{p_{j}}{q^{n-1}}=p_{j}\,,

(A19)

because there are exactly
q^{n-1}
states satisfying
\boldsymbol{\mu}\cdot\boldsymbol{\alpha}=j
. Hence, the entropy of the operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
in the model with the single-interaction
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is

\displaystyle S[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}]=-\sum_{j=0}^{q-1}p_{j}\,\log p_{j}\,,

(A20)

and the entropy of the model probability distribution is

\displaystyle S[P(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,|\,\{\boldsymbol{\mu}\},\,g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})]

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}P(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,|\,\{\boldsymbol{\mu}\},\,g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\log P(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,|\,\{\boldsymbol{\mu}\},\,g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})=-q^{n-1}\sum_{j=0}^{q-1}\frac{p_{j}}{q^{n-1}}\log\frac{p_{j}}{q^{n-1}}\,,

\displaystyle=-\sum_{j=0}^{q-1}p_{j}\log p_{j}+(n-1)\log q\,.

(A21)

The first part this last equation corresponds to the entropy of the operator
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
, while the second corresponds to the entropy of
(n-1)
purely random
q
-state variables.

Figure A1:

Contour plot of the entropy (in quits – i.e., we divided the value by
\log q
) of the operator
\phi^{1}(\alpha_{1})=\exp(\frac{2\pi i}{q}\alpha_1)
as a function of the parameters
a_{1}
and
b_{1}
for different values of
q
.
For
q
prime, all the other operators have the same entropy plot as this operator.
For
q
non-prime, the entropy plot can become the one of a smaller
q
if
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is divisible. For instance, the operator
\phi^{2}(\alpha_{1})=\exp(\frac{2\pi i}{4}\,2\alpha_1)=z_{q}^{\,2\alpha_{1}}
for
q=4
is equal to the operator
\phi^{1}(\alpha_{1})=\exp(\frac{2\pi i}{2}\alpha_1)=z_{q}^{\,\alpha_{1}}
for
q=2
, and the entropy plot of this operator
\phi^{2}
becomes the one of a typical operator at
q=2
(instead of
q=4
).

Appendix B
Expansions of the partition function

B.1
Generalized Euler formula

Similarly to the binary case, we re-write the exponential under the sum of
q
hyperbolic functions. In the binary case (
q=2
), Ref.
9
uses that:

\displaystyle e^{g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})}=\cosh(g_{\vecmu})+\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})\sinh(g_{\vecmu})\,,\qquad{\rm for}\;\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})\in\{-1;+1\}\,.

(A22)

For larger values of
q
, the monomials
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
are the
q
-th roots of unity:

\displaystyle\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{2i\pi}{q}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\qquad\forall\boldsymbol{\mu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}\,,\;\;\forall\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}

(A23)

\displaystyle\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})

\displaystyle\in\{e^{\frac{2i\pi}{q}k}\,|\,k\in[\![0,\,q-1]\!]\}=\sqrt[q]{1}\,.

(A24)

One can use the generalized version of Euler’s formula
37
(see proof below):

\displaystyle\boxed{\qquad e^{g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})}=\sum_{r=0}^{q-1}c_{r}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\,\left[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right]^{r}\,,\qquad{\rm where}\;\;c_{r}(x)=\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{x^{kq+r}}{(kq+r)!}\,.\qquad}

(A25)

The functions
c_{r}

are the generalized hyperbolic functions of order
q
:
c_{r}(x)=F_{q,r}^{1}(x)

37
. For
q=2
, one has respectively for
r=0
and
r=1
:

\displaystyle c_{0}(x)=F_{2,0}^{1}(x)=\cosh(x)\qquad{\rm and}\qquad c_{1}(x)=F_{2,1}^{1}(x)=\sinh(x)\,,

(A26)

which gives back Eq. (
A22
).
For
q=3
, one has:

\displaystyle e^{g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})}=F_{3,0}^{1}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})+F_{3,1}^{1}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})+F_{3,2}^{1}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\left[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right]^{2}\,,

(A27)

where

\displaystyle c_{0}(x)=F_{3,0}^{1}(x)=\frac{1}{3}\left[e^{x}+2\,e^{-x/2}\cos\left(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\,x\right)\right]

\displaystyle c_{1}(x)=F_{3,1}^{1}(x)=\frac{1}{3}\left[e^{x}+2\,e^{-x/2}\cos\left(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\,x+\frac{1}{3}\pi\right)\right]

(A28)

\displaystyle c_{2}(x)=F_{3,2}^{1}(x)=\frac{1}{3}\left[e^{x}+2\,e^{-x/2}\cos\left(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\,x-\frac{1}{3}\pi\right)\right]

Proof of Eq. (
A25
).

We start from the power series expansion of the exponential function:

\displaystyle e^{g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})}=\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{\left[g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right]^{j}}{j!}=\sum_{j\geq 0}\,\frac{g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\,j}}{j!}\,\left[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right]^{j\,{\rm mod}\,q}\,,

(A29)

where we used that
[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})]^{q}=1
(
j\,{\rm mod}\,q
denotes
j
modulo
q
).
We then group all the terms that have the same power
r
of
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
(for which
j
is of the form
j=kq+r
for all
k\geq 0
):

\displaystyle e^{g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=0}^{q-1}\;\sum_{k\geq 0}\;\frac{g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\,kq+r}}{(kq+r)!}\,\left[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right]^{r}\,,

(A30)

which gives Eq. (
A25
).
∎

B.2
Euler formula, special case
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}

Proof of Eq. (
65
).

From the definition (
60
), all the elements
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
verify that
2\,\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{0}
in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
and, as a consequence, that
\left[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right]^{2}=1
for all state
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
. This means that for
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
one can write
\exp(g_{\vecmu}\phi^{\vecmu}(\state))
as a sum over two terms only after grouping, respectively, odd and even powers of
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
in the generalized Euler formula (
A25
). One then simply recovers the original Euler formula (
A22
):

\displaystyle\forall\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{0},\qquad e^{g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})}=A_{0}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})+A_{1}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\;\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\,,

(A31)

\displaystyle{\rm where}\;\;\;A_{0}(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{\frac{q}{2}-1}c_{2k}(x)=\cosh(x)\;\;\,{\rm and}\;\;\,A_{1}(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{\frac{q}{2}-1}c_{2k+1}(x)=\sinh(x)\,.

(A32)

We recall that
q
is even (and thus
q/2
is an integer), because
\mathcal{M}_{0}=\emptyset
for odd values of
q
.
This result can also be obtained directly by observing that for all
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
,
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\pm 1
, which immediately gives Eq. (
A22
).
It is however interesting to recover it from the general formula by grouping odd and even powers of
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
, because a similar reasoning can also be used for other prime factors of
q
when
q
is not prime (as in Sec.
3.3.3
).
∎

B.3
Euler-like formula for complex-conjugate terms
(\boldsymbol{\mu},-\boldsymbol{\mu})

Proof of Eq. (
63
).

One can expand each exponential in the following equation using the generalized Euler formula (
A25
):

\displaystyle e^{\;g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})}\,e^{g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}\phi^{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{q-1}\sum_{k=0}^{q-1}c_{m}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})c_{k}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*})\left[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right]^{m-k}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum_{m=0}^{q-1}c_{m}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})c_{m}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*})}_{k=m}+\underbrace{\sum_{m=0}^{q-1}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}c_{m}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})c_{k}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*})\left[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right]^{m-k}}_{k<m}+\underbrace{\sum_{m=0}^{q-1}\sum_{k=m+1}^{q-1}c_{m}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})c_{k}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*})\left[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right]^{q+m-k}}_{k>m}

in which, for the case
k>m
, we used that
[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})]^{q}=1
for all
\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\mu}
. For the double sum with
k<m
, the exponent
(m-k)
is in the interval
[1,q-1]
for all the terms in the sum. In the following, we make the change of variables
r=(m-k)
replacing
k
by
r
.
For the double sum with
k>m
, the exponent
(m-k)
is in the interval
[-(q-1),-1]
for all the terms in the sum, and, by adding
q
, we get that the exponent
(q+m-k)\in[1,q-1]
. In the following, we make the change of variables
r=(q+m-k)
replacing
k
by
r
.

\displaystyle e^{\;g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})+g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}\phi^{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})}=\underbrace{\sum_{m=0}^{q-1}c_{m}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})c_{m}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*})}_{k=m}+\underbrace{\sum_{m=0}^{q-1}\sum_{r=1}^{m}c_{m}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})c_{m-r}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*})\left[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right]^{r}}_{k<m}+\underbrace{\sum_{m=0}^{q-1}\sum_{r=m+1}^{q-1}c_{m}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})c_{q+m-r}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*})\left[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right]^{r}}_{k>m}

Note that the coefficient in front of
[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}]^{r}
in each term can be written under the form:
c_{m}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})c_{(m-r)\bmod{q}}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*})
.
Indeed, in the first double sum, the values taken by
m
and
r
are such that
(m-r)\in[0,q-1]
, and thus
(m-r)\bmod{q}=(m-r)
. In the second double sum, the values taken by
m
and
r
are such that
(m-r)\in[-(q-1),-1]
, and thus
(m-r)\bmod{q}=q+(m-r)
. This gives:

\displaystyle e^{\;g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})+g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*}\phi^{-\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})}=\sum_{m=0}^{q-1}\sum_{r=0}^{q-1}c_{m}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})c_{(m-r)\bmod{q}}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{*})\left[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right]^{r}\,,

(A33)

which finally gives Eq. (
63
) after switching the order of the two sums.
∎

B.4
Loop expansion of the partition function

Proof of Property
9
.

Consider a model
\mathcal{M}\subseteq(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
. We denote by
K=|\mathcal{M}|
the number of operators in the model and arbitrarily index the operators from
1
to
K
:
\mathcal{M}=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\,\cdots,\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{K}\}
.
Using the generalized Euler formula (
A25
) in the expression of the partition function (
21
):

\displaystyle Z_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\prod_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}}\left(\sum_{r=0}^{q-1}c_{r}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\,\left[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right]^{r}\right)\,,\qquad{\rm where}\;\;c_{r}(x)=\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{x^{kq+r}}{(kq+r)!}\,,

(A34)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{r}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{K}}\prod_{k=1}^{K}\big[c_{r_{k}}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}})\,\left[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right]^{r_{k}}\big]\,,

(A35)

in which
r_{k}
denotes the
k
-th element of the vector
\boldsymbol{r}
. Using that
[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}]^{r}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\phi^{r\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
and that
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i}+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
, where the sum and product in the exponents are taken over
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
, one gets:

\displaystyle Z_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{r}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{K}}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{K}c_{r_{k}}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}})\right)\,\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\phi^{\sum_{k=1}^{K}r_{k}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\,,

(A36)

Observing that, due to Property (
2
),

\displaystyle\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\phi^{\sum_{k=1}^{K}r_{k}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\begin{cases}\;\;q^{n}\,,\qquad&{\rm if}\;\;\sum_{k=1}^{K}r_{k}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}=\boldsymbol{0}\qquad\textrm{in }(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}\\
\;\;0\,,\qquad&{\rm else}\,,\end{cases}

(A37)

we define the loops of
\mathcal{M}
as the vectors
\boldsymbol{r}=(r_{1},\,\dots,\,r_{K})
for which this sum is non-zero. The set of loops of a model
\mathcal{M}
is then:

\displaystyle\boxed{\qquad\mathcal{L}_{q}(\mathcal{M})=\bigg\{\boldsymbol{\ell}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{K}\;\Big|\;\sum_{k=1}^{K}\ell_{k}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}=\boldsymbol{0}\bigg\}\;,\qquad}

(A38)

and, the partition function can be computed as a sum over the loops of the model as:

\displaystyle\boxed{\qquad Z_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})=q^{n}\,\sum_{\boldsymbol{\ell}\in\mathcal{L}_{q}(\mathcal{M})}\prod_{k=1}^{K}c_{\ell_{k}}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}})\,,\qquad{\rm where}\;\;c_{\ell}(x)=\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{x^{kq+\ell}}{(kq+\ell)!}\,.\qquad}

(A39)

∎

B.5
Loop expansion without trivial loops from c.c. operators

Proof of Property
10
.

Consider a spin model
\mathcal{M}\subseteq(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
, which we write under the form
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{0}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}
as described in Definition
10
. We denote
K_{0}=|\mathcal{M}_{0}|
and
K_{1}=|\mathcal{M}_{1}|
the number of operators respectively in
\mathcal{M}_{0}
and in
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and arbitrarily index the operators:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0}=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0,1},\cdots,\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0,K_{0}}\}\qquad{\rm and}\qquad\mathcal{M}_{1}=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,1},\cdots,\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,K_{1}}\}\,.

(A40)

Let’s replace Eq. (
63
) and Eq. (
65
) in the expression of
Z_{\mathcal{M}}
from Eq. (
62
):

\displaystyle Z_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\prod_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}}\Big[A_{0}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}})+A_{1}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}})\;\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\Big]\prod_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{1}}\left[\,\sum_{r=0}^{q-1}B_{r}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}})\left[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right]^{r}\,\right]

(A41)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\Bigg[\sum_{\boldsymbol{r}_{0}\in(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{K_{0}}}\prod_{i=1}^{K_{0}}\left(A_{r_{0,i}}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0,i}})\,[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0,i}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})]^{r_{0,i}}\right)\Bigg]\Bigg[\sum_{\boldsymbol{r}_{1}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{K_{1}}}\prod_{j=1}^{K_{1}}\left(B_{r_{1,j}}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,j}})\,[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,j}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})]^{r_{1,j}}\right)\Bigg]\,,

(A42)

where
r_{0,i}
(resp.
r_{1,i}
) denotes the
i
-th element of the vector
\boldsymbol{r}_{0}
(resp.
\boldsymbol{r}_{1}
). We use that
[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}]^{r}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\phi^{r\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
and that
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{i}+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
, where the operations in the exponents are taken over
(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{n}
if
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
and over
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
if
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{1}
. This leads to:

\displaystyle Z_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{r}_{0}\in(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{K_{0}}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{r}_{1}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{K_{1}}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{K_{0}}A_{r_{0,i}}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0,i}})\prod_{j=1}^{K_{1}}B_{r_{1,j}}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,j}})\right]\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\phi^{\sum_{i=0}^{K_{0}}r_{0,i}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0,i}+\sum_{j=0}^{K_{1}}r_{1,j}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,j}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\,.

(A43)

Using that (see Property
2
):

\displaystyle\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\phi^{\sum_{i=0}^{K_{0}}r_{0,i}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0,i}+\sum_{j=0}^{K_{1}}r_{1,j}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,j}}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\begin{cases}\;\;q^{n}\,,\qquad&{\rm if}\;\;\sum_{i=0}^{K_{0}}r_{0,i}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0,i}+\sum_{j=0}^{K_{1}}r_{1,j}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,j}=\boldsymbol{0}\qquad\textrm{in }(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}\\
\;\;0\,,\qquad&{\rm else.}\end{cases}

(A44)

we defined the set of (non-trivial) loops of a model
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{0}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}
as:

\displaystyle\boxed{\qquad\mathcal{L}_{q}^{cc}(\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{1})=\bigg\{(\boldsymbol{r}_{0},\boldsymbol{r}_{1})\in(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{K_{0}}\times(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{K_{1}}\;\Big|\;\sum_{i=1}^{K_{0}}r_{0,i}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0,i}+\sum_{j=1}^{K_{1}}r_{1,j}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,j}=\boldsymbol{0}\bigg\}\;.\qquad}

(A45)

Finally, the partition can be computed as a sum over these loops, as:

\displaystyle\boxed{\qquad Z_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})=q^{n}\,\sum_{(\boldsymbol{r}_{0},\boldsymbol{r}_{1})\in\mathcal{L}_{q}^{cc}(\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{1})}\prod_{i=1}^{K_{0}}A_{r_{0,i}}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0,i}})\prod_{j=1}^{K_{1}}B_{r_{1,j}}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1,j}})\,,\qquad}

(A46)

\displaystyle{\rm where}\qquad A_{0}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})=\sum_{k=0}^{\frac{q}{2}-1}c_{2k}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\qquad{\rm and}\qquad A_{1}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})=\sum_{k=0}^{\frac{q}{2}-1}c_{2k+1}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\,,

(A47)

\displaystyle B_{r}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})=\sum_{m=0}^{q-1}c_{m}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\,c_{(m-r)\bmod{q}}(g^{*}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\,,\qquad{\rm and}\qquad c_{r}(x)=\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{x^{kq+r}}{(kq+r)!}\,.

(A48)

∎

For systems with
q
prime or
q=2\,p
where
p
is prime, the contribution from the independent part of a model
\mathcal{M}
(strong and weak) can be directly identified from this last expression, as the term in the sum for which
(\boldsymbol{\ell}_{0},\boldsymbol{\ell}_{1})=(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{0})
:

\displaystyle Z_{\mathcal{M}_{ind}}(\boldsymbol{g})

\displaystyle=q^{n}\,\prod_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}}A_{0}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}})\,\prod_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{1}}B_{0}(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}})\,,

(A49)

\displaystyle{\rm where}\;A_{0}(x)=\cosh(x)\qquad{\rm and}\qquad B_{0}(x)=\sum_{m=0}^{q-1}c_{m}(x)\,c_{m}(x^{*})\,.

(A50)

Note that
c_{m}(x^{*})=[c_{m}(x)]^{*}
and thus
c_{m}(x)\,c_{m}(x^{*})=\lVert c_{m}(x)\rVert^{2}
.

Appendix C
Gauge transformations

C.1
Counting gauge transformations

We give the main lines of the proof of Eq. (
93
).
The group of gauge transformations
\mathcal{G}_{q}(n)
is given by the general linear group
GL((\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n})
. We can compute the number of gauge transformations using the Chinese Remainder Theorem. Let the prime factorization of
q
be
q=p_{1}^{a_{1}}p_{2}^{a_{2}}\cdots p_{k}^{a_{k}}
. The ring
\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z}
is then isomorphic to the product of rings
\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{Z}/p_{i}^{a_{i}}\mathbb{Z}
. This induces a group isomorphism:

\displaystyle GL((\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n})\cong\prod_{i=1}^{k}GL((\mathbb{Z}/p_{i}^{a_{i}}\mathbb{Z})^{n})\,.

(A51)

It follows that

\displaystyle|GL((\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n})|=\prod_{i=1}^{k}|GL((\mathbb{Z}/p_{i}^{a_{i}}\mathbb{Z})^{n})|\,.

(A52)

It remains to determine the order of
GL((\mathbb{Z}/p^{a}\mathbb{Z})^{n})
for a prime
p
and
a\in\mathbb{N}
.

Consider the canonical ring homomorphism
\rho:GL((\mathbb{Z}/p^{a}\mathbb{Z})^{n})\to GL((\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z})^{n})
which reduces each matrix entry modulo
p
. This map is a surjective group homomorphism. By the first isomorphism theorem for groups, we have:

\displaystyle|GL((\mathbb{Z}/p^{a}\mathbb{Z})^{n})|=|\ker(\rho)|\cdot|GL((\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z})^{n})|\,.

(A53)

The kernel,
\ker(\rho)
, consists of matrices
M\in GL((\mathbb{Z}/p^{a}\mathbb{Z})^{n})
such that
M\equiv I\pmod{p}
, where
I
is the identity matrix. Any such matrix can be written in the form
M=I+pA
, where
A
is an
n\times n
matrix with entries in
\mathbb{Z}/p^{a-1}\mathbb{Z}
. There are
(p^{a-1})^{n^{2}}
possible matrices for
A
. Therefore, the order of the kernel is:

\displaystyle|\ker(\rho)|=(p^{a-1})^{n^{2}}=p^{n^{2}(a-1)}\,.

(A54)

The remaining term,
|GL_{n}(\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z})|
, is the order of the general linear group over the finite field
\mathbb{F}_{p}
. This is a standard result obtained by counting the ways to choose
n
linearly independent column vectors from
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}
. The formula is:

\displaystyle|GL(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n})|=(p^{n}-1)(p^{n}-p)\cdots(p^{n}-p^{n-1})=\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}(p^{n}-p^{k})\,.

(A55)

Combining these results, the order for the prime power case is:

\displaystyle|GL_{n}(\mathbb{Z}/p^{a}\mathbb{Z})|=p^{n^{2}(a-1)}\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}(p^{n}-p^{k})\,.

(A56)

The total number of gauge transformations for an arbitrary
q
is found by substituting this into the product over the prime factors of
q
.

C.2
Gauge transformation of a spin model

Consider a spin model
\mathcal{M}
with parameters
\boldsymbol{g}
.
Under a gauge transformation
\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}
, the energy of a state
\boldsymbol{s}
in the model can be rewritten in terms of the new basis variables
\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}
, by using the inverse GT
\boldsymbol{s}=\boldsymbol{b}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})
:

\displaystyle H(\boldsymbol{s}\mid\boldsymbol{g},\,\mathcal{M})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}}g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{s})=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}}g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{b}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}))=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}}g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}](\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})\,,

(A57)

where we used the definition of gauge transformed operators (
95
) in the last equality.
Because the transformation of a spin operator
\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}]
is still a spin operator (see Definition
18
), the last expression in Eq. (
A57
) corresponds to the Hamiltonian of a spin model with operators
\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}](\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})=\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})
where
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{T}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}
.
The energy of a state
\boldsymbol{s}
under a model
\mathcal{M}
is thus mapped by a GT to the energy of a new state
\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{b}(\boldsymbol{s})
of a new spin model
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle H(\boldsymbol{s}\mid\boldsymbol{g},\,\mathcal{M})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}}\,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})=H(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}\mid\boldsymbol{g}^{\prime},\,\mathcal{M}^{\prime})\,,

(A58)

where
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}_{b}[\mathcal{M}]=\{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{T}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}\mid\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}\}
, and
\boldsymbol{g}^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}_{b}[\boldsymbol{g}]
is a permutation of the parameters in
\boldsymbol{g}
that is such that
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}}=g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
for
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{T}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}
.

Proof: models related by GTs have the same partition function (for Sec.
4.6
).

Consider a
q
-state spin model
\mathcal{M}=\{\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}},\dots,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{K}}\}
and a GT of this model,
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}[\mathcal{M}]=\{\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}_{1}},\dots,\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}_{K}}\}
, with
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}_{k}}=\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}}]
for all
k\in[1,K]
.
We labeled the operators in the same order in both models such that
the
k
-th parameter of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is equal to the
k
-th parameter of
\mathcal{M}
:
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}_{k}}=g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}}
, where
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}^{\prime}=T_{\boldsymbol{b}}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}
are related by the GT.

The partition function of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is given by:

\displaystyle Z_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{g}^{\prime})=\sum_{\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}\in\Omega}\exp\left({\;\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}^{\prime}}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})}\right)\,,

(A59)

where
\Omega
denotes the set of all possible
q^{n}
spin states and

\boldsymbol{g}^{\prime}=(g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}_{1}},\dots,g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}_{K}})
is the vector of parameters of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
. The gauge transformation
\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}
corresponds to the change of spin basis
\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{b}(\boldsymbol{s})
. The gauge transform operators are then related to the original operators by
\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})=\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{b}}[\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}](\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})=\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}}[\boldsymbol{b}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})]
(see Def.
18
), which gives:

\displaystyle Z_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{g}^{\prime})=\sum_{\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}\in\Omega}\exp\left({\;\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}^{\prime}}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}}[\boldsymbol{b}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})]}\right)\,.

(A60)

We use the fact that a GT is an automorphism of the state space
\Omega
(bijection between states in
\Omega
) to perform the change of variable
\boldsymbol{s}=\boldsymbol{b}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})
in the first sum and re-write it as:

\displaystyle Z_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{g}^{\prime})=\sum_{\boldsymbol{s}\in\Omega}\exp\left({\;\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}^{\prime}}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}}(\boldsymbol{s})}\right)=\sum_{\boldsymbol{s}\in\Omega}\exp\left({\;\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}}\phi^{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}}(\boldsymbol{s})}\right)=Z_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{g})\,.

(A61)

Here, in the second equality, we used that
k
-th parameter of
\mathcal{M}
is equal to the
k
-th parameter of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
,
g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}_{k}}=g_{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k}}
, following the labeling choice made above.
∎

C.3
Real-valued spin models and reduced model

Proof of Property
.

Consider two c.c. operators
(\boldsymbol{\mu},-\boldsymbol{\mu})\in[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}]^{2}
. We recall that the notation
-\boldsymbol{\mu}
was defined in Sec
2.1
as

-\boldsymbol{\mu}=(q-\mu_{1},\dots,q-\mu_{n})
. Consider a gauge transformation
\mathcal{T}
of these operators with matrix representation
\boldsymbol{T}
. We denoted by
t_{ij}
the elements of the inverse matrix
\boldsymbol{T}^{-1}
. We then have that:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}[-\boldsymbol{\mu}]=\boldsymbol{T}^{-1}(-\boldsymbol{\mu})=\bigg(\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{n}t_{ij}\,(q-\mu_{j})\Big){\rm mod}\,q\bigg)_{i}=-\boldsymbol{T}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}=-\mathcal{T}[\boldsymbol{\mu}]\,,

where were we used that
(\sum_{j=1}^{n}t_{ij}\,(q-\mu_{j}))({\rm mod}\,q)=-(\sum_{j=1}^{n}t_{ij}\,\mu_{j})({\rm mod}\,q)
.
∎

For the two proofs below, we used the decomposition of Definition
10
for both the spin model
\mathcal{M}
and its transformed model
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}[\mathcal{M}]
under a GT
\mathcal{T}
, i.e. we decompose the models as
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{0}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\mathcal{M}_{0}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\prime*}
. We show that
\mathcal{T}[\mathcal{M}_{0}]=\mathcal{M}_{0}^{\prime}
and that
\mathcal{T}[\mathcal{M}_{1}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}]=\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\prime*}
.

Proof that:

for all
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
,
\mathcal{T}[\boldsymbol{\mu}]\in\mathcal{M}_{0}^{\prime}
.

For all
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
,
-\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\mu}
. This implies that
\mathcal{T}[-\boldsymbol{\mu}]=\mathcal{T}[\boldsymbol{\mu}]=\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}
. Using that under a GT
\mathcal{T}[-\boldsymbol{\mu}]=-\mathcal{T}[\boldsymbol{\mu}]=-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}
(see Property
), one gets that the operator
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}
verifies
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}=-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}
, and thus is an element of
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{\prime}
.
∎

Proof by contradiction that:

for all
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{1}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}
,
\mathcal{T}[\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}]\in\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\prime*}
.
Take an operator
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{1}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}
, by definition
-\boldsymbol{\mu}\neq\boldsymbol{\mu}
in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
.
Suppose that its transformation
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}[\boldsymbol{\mu}]
is an element of
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{\prime}
. By definition of
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{\prime}
, the operator then verifies that
-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}
in
(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
. Using the inverse GT on both side of this equality we get that:
\mathcal{T}^{-1}[-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}]=\mathcal{T}^{-1}[\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}]
, where
\mathcal{T}^{-1}[\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}]=\boldsymbol{\mu}
and
\mathcal{T}^{-1}[-\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}]=-\mathcal{T}^{-1}[\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}]
as
\mathcal{T}^{-1}
is a GT (see Property
). We finally get that
-\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\mu}
, which is in contradiction with the fact that
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}_{1}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}
. This means that
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}[\boldsymbol{\mu}]
is not an element of
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{\prime}
, and is therefore in
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\prime*}
.
∎

Proof of the reduced loop structure.

Consider a real-valued spin model
\mathcal{M}
that can be written under the form
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{1}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}^{*}
with the decomposition of Definition
10
(i.e., for which
\mathcal{M}_{0}=\emptyset
).
This is always the case for a real-valued models with
q
prime and
q>2
(for which all the operators are included by c.c. pairs and verify that
-\boldsymbol{\mu}\neq\boldsymbol{\mu}
).
The reduced set of loops of
\mathcal{M}
, excluding trivial loops due to c.c. operators, are given by Eq. (
68
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{cc}_{q}(\mathcal{M})=\bigg\{\boldsymbol{\ell}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{K_{1}}\;\Big|\;\sum_{j=1}^{K_{1}}\ell_{j}\,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}=\boldsymbol{0}\bigg\}\;,

(A62)

where
K_{1}=|\mathcal{M}_{1}|
is the number of operators in
\mathcal{M}_{1}
. Using the reduced matrix representation
\boldsymbol{M}_{1}=(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{\mu}_{K_{1}})
of
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and viewing
\boldsymbol{\ell}
as a column vector, we can rewrite
\mathcal{L}^{cc}_{q}(\mathcal{M})
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{cc}_{q}(\mathcal{M})=\bigg\{\boldsymbol{\ell}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{K_{1}}\;\Big|\;\boldsymbol{M}_{1}\boldsymbol{\ell}=\boldsymbol{0}\bigg\}=\ker(\boldsymbol{M}_{1})\;.

(A63)

One could have also directly identified in Eq. (
A62
) that
\mathcal{L}^{cc}_{q}(\mathcal{M})=\mathcal{L}_{q}(\mathcal{M}_{1})
, which gives the same result.
∎

Appendix D
Properties of MCMs

The proofs in this section are almost identical to the one derived for the binary case in Ref.
9
(Supplementary Materials) and Ref.
15
, just generalized to the case
q>2
.

and
