_2’=
α
_1
.Thematrix
T
inEq.~\eqref{app:eq:exFig:GT:T}isthematrixrepresentationofthislineartransformation.\par\par

D.2
GTs of MCMs

D.3
Number of MCMs in a class of MCMs

We give the main lines of the proof of the results in Lemma
38
.
Consider a system with
n

q
-state spin variables.
Let us denote
\mathcal{N}_{q}(n,r)
the number of different ways to choose
r
independent operators in this
n
-spin system. For the special case
r=n
, this number corresponds to the number of possible gauge transformations in the
n
spin system,
\mathcal{N}_{q}(n,r)=|\mathcal{G}_{q}(n)|
, given in Eq. (
92
) and (
93
). Using the same approach as the one used to compute
|\mathcal{G}_{q}(n)|
, we obtain more generally for any
r\in\{1,\dots,n\}
that:

\displaystyle\textrm{for}\;q\;\textrm{prime},\;\qquad

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{q}(n,r)=\prod_{i=0}^{r-1}(q^{n}-q^{i})\,;

(A64)

\displaystyle\textrm{for}\;q\;\textrm{not prime},\;\qquad

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{q}(n,r)=\prod_{k=1}^{K}p_{k}^{\,(m_{k}-1)n^{2}}\,\prod_{i=0}^{r-1}(p_{k}^{\,n}-p_{k}^{\,i})\,.

(A65)

From this result, we can compute the number of (strongly) independent models with rank
r
, among all real-valued spin models of
n
spins. This number is given by:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{ind}(n,r)=\begin{cases}&\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{N}_{q}(n,r)}{r!}\,,\qquad{\rm for}\;q=2\,,\\[5.0pt]
&\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{N}_{q}(n,r)}{2^{r}\,r!}\,,\qquad{\rm for}\;q>2\,.\end{cases}

(A66)

This corresponds to the number of ways of choosing
r
independent operators in the set of all possible
q^{n}-1
operators, divided by the number of possible permutations of these
r
elements. For
q>2
, the division by
2^{r}
accounts for the fact that using an operator or its c.c. in the independent set defines the same independent model (because real-valued models contain both conjugate operators, and
because, for
q>2
, operators that can be used in an independent set cannot be their own conjugate).

Take an MCM
\mathcal{M}
with a single complete component of rank
r
.
The number of different MCMs in the same class as
\mathcal{M}
(i.e., with a single C.C. of rank
r
)
is given by the total number of possible GTs of
\mathcal{M}
, divided by the number of these GTs that leaves
\mathcal{M}
invariant. This is equal to the number of ways to choose
r
independent (basis) operators among all possible
(q^{n}-1)
operators, divided by the number of ways to choose
r
independent (basis) operators among the
(q^{r}-1)
operators that are already in the
\mathcal{M}
, which is:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\rm CC}(n,r)=\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{N}_{q}(n,r)}{\mathcal{N}_{q}(r,r)}=\begin{cases}\;\;\displaystyle\prod_{i=0}^{r-1}\,\frac{q^{n}-q^{i}}{q^{r}-q^{i}}\,,&\qquad{\rm for}\;q\;{\rm prime},\\[4.0pt]
\;\;\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{K}p_{k}^{(m_{k}-1)(n^{2}-r^{2})}\prod_{i=0}^{r-1}\frac{p_{k}^{n}-p_{k}^{i}}{p_{k}^{r}-p_{k}^{i}}\,,&\qquad{\rm for}\;q\;\textrm{not prime}.\end{cases}

(A67)

Finally, take an MCM
\mathcal{M}
with
m
ICCs and denote by
m_{r_{a}}
the number of ICCs of rank
r_{a}
in
\mathcal{M}
. The number of different MCMs in the same class as
\mathcal{M}
(i.e., with the same sequence of multiplicity
\{m_{r_{a}}\}_{1\leq r_{a}\leq n}
of their ICCs) can be obtained by combining the two previous arguments:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\rm MCM}(n,\{m_{r_{a}}\}_{1\leq r_{a}\leq n})=\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{N}_{q}(n,r)}{\displaystyle\prod_{r_{a}=1}^{n}(m_{r_{a}}!)\;\,\mathcal{N}_{q}(r_{a},r_{a})^{m_{r_{a}}}}\;,

(A68)

where the factors
1/m_{r_{a}}!
account for permutations among ICCs of the same size.

D.4
Notations for the proofs in the sections below

Throughout the paper, we consider a system of
n
discrete random variables
\alpha_{i}
that can take integer values between
0
and
(q-1)
with
q\geq 2
. A state of the system is denoted
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n})\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{n}
, and
a dataset is composed of
N
observations of the system and is denoted
\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}=(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(1)},\cdots,\,\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(N)})
. We assume the system to be stationary and that the observed states are randomly sampled from the same underlying distribution,
p(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g},\mathcal{M})
, defined by a parametric model
\mathcal{M}
.

In the following, we consider that
\mathcal{M}
is an MCM with a single Independent Complete Component (ICC)
\mathcal{M}_{a}
of rank
r_{a}
(with
r_{a}\leq n
).
For the sake of simplicity of the derivations, we consider that
\mathcal{M}
is already written in a preferred basis and we reduce the system to the
r_{a}
variables modeled by
\mathcal{M}_{a}
. This corresponds to reducing the space to the basis variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}
on which the ICC
\mathcal{M}_{a}
is based, and considering the model distribution
p_{a}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{a}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g}_{a},\mathcal{M}_{a})
, which is a distribution over
r_{a}
variables only (instead of
n
).
To simplify the notation, we then drop the index “
a
” used to label the ICCs. We denote the model
\mathcal{M}_{icc}
and its rank
r
, and use the notation
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{r})\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{r}
for the state of the system reduced to the
r
variables (strictly speaking, these variables can be any subset of the variables
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
introduced in the previous paragraph).

D.5
Alternative parametrization of an Independent Complete Components (ICC).

The ICC
\mathcal{M}_{icc}
is a complete model (see Sec. (
2
)) for the sub-system of
r=\rank(\M_{icc})
variables. The model has
(q^{r}-1)
parameters
\boldsymbol{g}
and can be (equivalently) defined as the following parametric family of probability distributions:

\displaystyle p(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,|\,\boldsymbol{\eta},\mathcal{M}_{icc})=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{r}}\,\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\delta(\boldsymbol{\alpha}-\boldsymbol{\mu})\,,

(A69)

where each
\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
is the probability that the system is in the state
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{\mu}
, here considered as a parameter.
We denote
\boldsymbol{\eta}
the vector of the
(q^{r}-1)
parameters
\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}

for all non-zero
\boldsymbol{\mu}
.
The remaining probability
\eta_{\boldsymbol{0}}=p(\boldsymbol{0})
is defined by the normalization:

\displaystyle\eta_{\boldsymbol{0}}=1-\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}=1}^{q^{r}-1}\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,.

(A70)

In this equation, the sum is over all the non-zero vectors
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{r}]^{*}
. We denoted each vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}
by its integer value in base
q
, which allows ordering the vectors
\boldsymbol{\mu}
from
1
to
(q^{r}-1)
.
For instance, the vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}=1
has its first entry equal to
1
, and all the other entries are equal to
0
. The vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}=(q^{r}-1)
has all its
r
entries equal to
(q-1)
.
Substituting the expression of
\eta_{\boldsymbol{0}}

in Eq. (
A69
), we get:

\displaystyle p(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,|\,\boldsymbol{\eta},\mathcal{M}_{icc})=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}=1}^{q^{r}-1}\,\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,\delta(\boldsymbol{\alpha}-\boldsymbol{\mu})+\left(1-\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}=1}^{q^{r}-1}\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right)\,\delta(\boldsymbol{\alpha}-\boldsymbol{0})\,,

(A71)

where
\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{0}
is the state where all the variables have the value
0
.

D.6
Fisher Information Matrix (FIM) for an ICC.

For an MCM (with multiple ICCs), the Fisher Information Matrix (FIM) in Eq. (
109
) is block diagonal, where each block corresponds to the FIM restricted to a single ICC. This is because the model probability distribution of an MCM factorizes over its ICCs, which also have non-overlapping sets of parameters. Consequently, the determinant of the FIM of an MCM is the product of the determinant of the FIM for each ICC,
i.e., for an MCM
\mathcal{M}=\cup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{M}_{a}
with parameters
\boldsymbol{g}=\cup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\boldsymbol{g}_{a}
:

\displaystyle\det\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{g})=\prod_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\det\mathcal{I}_{a}(\boldsymbol{g}_{a})\,.

(A72)

Let us now compute the FIM
\mathcal{I}_{a}
for a single ICC. For simplicity, we consider a system reduced to a single ICC with the notations specified in Sec.
D.4
(in particular, we drop the index “
a
” used to label ICCs).
For the ICC probability distribution with the new parametrization in Eq. (
A71
),
the Fisher Information Matrix (FIM)
is the square matrix of order
(q^{r}-1)
with elements:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{\eta})

\displaystyle=-\left\langle\partial_{\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\partial_{\eta_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}\log p(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,|\,\boldsymbol{\eta},\mathcal{M}_{icc})\right\rangle\,,\qquad\forall\,\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu}\in[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{r}]^{*}\,,

(A73)

where the average is taken over the model distribution
P(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,|\,\mathcal{M}_{icc},\,\boldsymbol{\eta})
.
Substituting Eq. (
A71
) and taking the two derivatives gives,

\forall\,\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu}\in[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{r}]^{*}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{\eta})

\displaystyle=\left\langle\frac{(\delta(\boldsymbol{\alpha}-\boldsymbol{\mu})-\delta(\boldsymbol{\alpha}-\boldsymbol{0}))(\delta(\boldsymbol{\alpha}-\boldsymbol{\nu})-\delta(\boldsymbol{\alpha}-\boldsymbol{0}))}{\left[P(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,|\,\mathcal{M}_{icc},\,\boldsymbol{\eta})\right]^{2}}\right\rangle

(A74)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{r}}\,\frac{\delta(\boldsymbol{\alpha}-\boldsymbol{\mu})\delta(\boldsymbol{\alpha}-\boldsymbol{\nu})+\delta(\boldsymbol{\alpha}-\boldsymbol{0})}{P(\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,|\,\mathcal{M}_{icc},\,\boldsymbol{\eta})}

(A75)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\boldsymbol{\eta})

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta_{\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\nu}}}{\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}+\frac{1}{\eta_{\boldsymbol{0}}}\,,

(A76)

in which
\eta_{\boldsymbol{0}}
is given by Eq. (
A70
). We recall that
\boldsymbol{\eta}
is a vector of the
(q^{r}-1)
parameters

\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
for all
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{r}]^{*}
.

For the following sections,
it is useful to compute the determinant of
\mathcal{I}
.
To do so, we observe that
\mathcal{I}
can be written under the form
\mathcal{I}=\mathbf{D}+\mathbf{u}\mathbf{v}^{\top}
,
where
\mathbf{D}
is a diagonal matrix with entries

\mathbf{D}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\boldsymbol{\mu}}=1/\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
for
\forall\,\boldsymbol{\mu}\in[(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{r}]^{*}
,
and where
\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{v}
are two
(q^{r}-1)
-dimensional column vectors with, respectively, all entries equal to
1
and all entries equal to
1/\eta_{0}
.
The matrix determinant lemma then
allows to compute the determinant of the FIM:

\displaystyle\det\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\eta})

\displaystyle=(1+\mathbf{v}^{\top}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\mathbf{u})\,\det\mathbf{D}=\left(1+\frac{1}{\eta_{\boldsymbol{0}}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}=1}^{q^{r}-1}\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right)\,\prod_{\boldsymbol{\mu}=1}^{q^{r}-1}\frac{1}{\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}

(A77)

\displaystyle\det\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\eta})

\displaystyle=\prod_{\boldsymbol{\mu}=0}^{q^{r}-1}\;\frac{1}{\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\,,

(A78)

in which
\eta_{\boldsymbol{0}}
is given by Eq. (
A70
).

D.7
Geometric complexity of an ICC.

Assuming Jeffreys’ prior over the model parameters, the geometric complexity of a spin model is given by
9
:

\displaystyle c_{\mathcal{M}}=\log\int\sqrt{\det\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{g})}\,d\boldsymbol{g}\,,

(A79)

where the integral is over all possible values of the parameters.
Using the factorization of
\det\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{g})
in Eq. (
A72
), the geometric complexity of an MCM can be written as the sum of the geometric complexity of its ICCs, as in Eq. (
134
).
In the following, we compute the complexity of a single ICC of rank
r
, which we denote
c_{icc}(r)
.
For simplicity, we consider a system reduced to a single ICC with the notations specified in Sec.
D.4
.

Proof of the geometric complexity of an ICC in Eq. (
135
).

The geometric part of the MDL complexity
43
;
45
is defined as
c_{icc}(r)=\log V_{icc}(r)
, where
V_{icc}(r)
is the volume of the
K=(q^{r}-1)
-dimensional model manifold:

\displaystyle V_{icc}(r)=\int_{[0,1]^{K}}\sqrt{\det\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\eta})}\,d\boldsymbol{\eta}\,.

(A80)

Substituting Eq. (
A78
), one gets:

\displaystyle V_{icc}(r)=\int_{[0,1]^{K}}\eta_{\boldsymbol{0}}^{-1/2}\,\prod_{\boldsymbol{\mu}=1}^{q^{r}-1}\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{-1/2}\,d\boldsymbol{\eta}\,,\qquad{\rm in\;which}\;\;\eta_{\boldsymbol{0}}=1-\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}=1}^{q^{r}-1}\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,.

(A81)

Here one can recognize the multivariate beta function:

\displaystyle V_{icc}(r)

\displaystyle=B\underset{q^{r}\;{\rm terms}}{\underbrace{\left(\frac{1}{2},\,\cdots,\,\frac{1}{2}\right)}}=\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{i=0}^{K}\,\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)}{\displaystyle\Gamma\left(\sum_{i=0}^{K}\frac{1}{2}\right)}=\frac{\displaystyle\pi^{\frac{q^{r}}{2}}}{\displaystyle\Gamma\left(\frac{q^{r}}{2}\right)}\,,

(A82)

which gives the complexity term:

\displaystyle c_{icc}(r)=\frac{q^{r}}{2}\log\pi-\log\Gamma\left(\frac{q^{r}}{2}\right)\,.

(A83)

∎

For
q=2
, one recovers the result obtained in the binary case by Ref.
9
:

\displaystyle c_{icc}(r)=2^{r-1}\log\pi-\log\Gamma(2^{r-1})\,.

(A84)

D.8
Likelihood function for an ICC.

Using the definition of the ICC in Eq. (
A69
), we compute the likelihood of the ICC for the dataset
\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}
:

\displaystyle P(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}\,|\,\boldsymbol{\eta},\mathcal{M}_{icc})

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{N}p(\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{(i)}\,|\,\boldsymbol{\eta},\mathcal{M}_{icc})

(A85)

\displaystyle=\prod_{\boldsymbol{\mu}=0}^{q^{r}-1}\;\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\;k_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}})}

(A86)

in which
\eta_{0}
satisfies Eq. (
A70
) and where
k_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}})
is
the number of times the state
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{\mu}
occurs in the dataset
\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}
.

D.9
Jeffreys’ prior.

Jeffreys’ prior for a parametric model with parameters
\boldsymbol{g}
is defined as
26
:

\displaystyle P_{0}(\boldsymbol{g})\propto\sqrt{\det\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{g})}\,,

(A87)

where
\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{g})
is the FIM [of the model].
Using the factorization of
\det\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{g})
in Eq. (
A72
), Jeffreys’ prior for an MCM factorizes over its ICCs:
P_{0}(\boldsymbol{g})=\prod_{a\in\mathcal{A}}P_{0}(\boldsymbol{g}_{a})
, for an MCM
\mathcal{M}=\cup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{M}_{a}
with parameters
\boldsymbol{g}=\cup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\boldsymbol{g}_{a}
.
The factorization of the prior over the parameters is used for the factorization of the model evidence in Eq. (
132
).

In the following, we obtain a closed-form expression for Jeffreys’ prior for a single ICC.
For simplicity, we consider a system reduced to a single ICC with the notations specified in Sec.
D.4
.
For the ICC distributions (
A69
) parametrized by
\boldsymbol{\eta}
, Jeffreys’ Prior is computed as:

\displaystyle P_{0}(\boldsymbol{\eta})=\frac{1}{V_{icc}}\,\sqrt{\det\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\eta})}\,,

(A88)

where
\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\eta})
is the FIM and the normalization
V_{icc}
is the volume in Eq. (
A81
). Substituting the expression of
\det\mathcal{I}(\boldsymbol{\eta})
from Eq. (
A78
), one gets:

\displaystyle P_{0}(\boldsymbol{\eta})=\frac{1}{V_{icc}}\,\prod_{\boldsymbol{\mu}=0}^{q^{r}-1}\,\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{-1/2}\,,\qquad{\rm in\;which}\;\;\eta_{\boldsymbol{0}}=1-\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}=1}^{q^{r}-1}\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,.

(A89)

Here, one can recognize the symmetric Dirichlet distribution with hyperparameter
1/2
.

D.10
Evidence of an ICC.

Proof of Eq. (
133
).

Combining the expressions in Eq. (
A86
) for the likelihood function and in Eq. (
A89
) for Jeffreys’ prior
P_{0}(\boldsymbol{\eta})
,
the evidence (or marginal likelihood) of the ICC is finally given by:

\displaystyle P(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}\,|\,\mathcal{M}_{icc})

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,1]^{K}}P(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}\,|\,\boldsymbol{\eta},\mathcal{M}_{icc})\,P_{0}(\boldsymbol{\eta})\,d\boldsymbol{\eta}

(A90)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{V_{icc}}\,\int_{[0,1]^{K}}\,\prod_{\boldsymbol{\mu}=0}^{q^{r}-1}\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\,k_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}})}\,\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{-1/2}\,d\eta_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,.

(A91)

where
K=(q^{r}-1)
is the number of parameters in the ICC.
Here we recognize the (multivariate) Beta function of the
q^{r}
variables
(k_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}})+1/2)
:

\displaystyle P(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}\,|\,\mathcal{M}_{icc})\;=\;\frac{\displaystyle B\left(k_{0}+\frac{1}{2},\,\cdots,\,k_{q^{r}-1}+\frac{1}{2}\right)}{\displaystyle B\left(\frac{1}{2},\,\cdots,\,\frac{1}{2}\right)}\;=\;\frac{\displaystyle\Gamma\left(\frac{q^{r}}{2}\right)}{\displaystyle\pi^{\frac{q^{r}}{2}}}\;\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{\boldsymbol{\mu}=0}^{q^{r}-1}\,\Gamma\left(k_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}})+\frac{1}{2}\right)}{\displaystyle\Gamma\left(N+\frac{q^{r}}{2}\right)}\;,

(A92)

where
k_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
is the number of times that the state
\boldsymbol{\mu}
occurs in the dataset
\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}
.
For
q=2
, we recover the results obtained for the binary case in Ref.
15
.
Rearranging the terms:

\displaystyle P(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}\,|\,\mathcal{M}_{icc})\;=\;\frac{\displaystyle\Gamma\left(\frac{q^{r}}{2}\right)}{\displaystyle\Gamma\left(N+\frac{q^{r}}{2}\right)}\;\prod_{\boldsymbol{\mu}=0}^{q^{r}-1}\left[\frac{\displaystyle\,\Gamma\left(k_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}})+\frac{1}{2}\right)}{\displaystyle\sqrt{\pi}}\right]\;,

(A93)

and one can observe that,
for all the states
\boldsymbol{\mu}
that don’t occur in the dataset (
k_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}})=0
), one has
\Gamma(k_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}})+1/2)/\sqrt{\pi}=1
, and therefore the evidence can be re-written as:

\displaystyle P(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}\,|\,\mathcal{M}_{icc})\;=\;\frac{\displaystyle\Gamma\left(\frac{q^{r}}{2}\right)}{\displaystyle\Gamma\left(N+\frac{q^{r}}{2}\right)}\;\prod_{\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}}\left[\frac{\displaystyle\,\Gamma\left(k_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}})+\frac{1}{2}\right)}{\displaystyle\sqrt{\pi}}\right]\;,

(A94)

where the product is now only over the states observed in the dataset
\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}
.
∎

D.11
Expansion of the log-evidence for large
N
and MDL principle.

Bayesian model selection and Minimum Description length principle are already known to be equivalent for large dataset, assuming Jeffreys’ prior over the parameters. For completeness, we re-derive this result in the particular case of MCM. Let us focus on the evidence of the complete model, as the extension to MCM is straightforward from that result. Taking the logarithm of the evidence in Eq. (
A93
) yields:

\displaystyle\hskip-5.69054pt\log P(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}\,|\,\mathcal{M}_{icc})=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{r}}\,\log\Gamma\left(k_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}})\hskip-2.84526pt+\frac{1}{2}\right)-\log\Gamma\left(N+\frac{q^{r}}{2}\right)-\log\frac{\pi^{\frac{q^{r}}{2}}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{q^{r}}{2}\right)}\,,

(A95)

where the last term can be recognized as the geometrical complexity
c_{icc}(r)
of an ICC of rank
r
in Eq. (
A83
).
We assume that the dataset
\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}
is sufficiently well-sampled, such that the empirical probability
p_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=k_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}})/N
is non zero for all state (each state is observed at least a few times).
We then take the expansion of the two log-gamma function for large
N
. This gives the following expansion of the log-evidence to the order of
1/N
:

\displaystyle\hskip-5.69054pt\log P(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}\,|\,\mathcal{M}_{icc})=N\,\sum_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z})^{r}}p_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\,\log p_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}-\frac{K}{2}\log\left(\frac{N}{2\pi}\right)-c_{icc}(r)+O\left(\frac{1}{N}\right)\,,

(A96)

where
K=q^{r}-1
is the number of parameters in the ICC.
The first term in the expansion is the maximum log-likelihood of the ICC (see Property
42
), in which
p_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=k_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}})/N
is the empirical probability of state
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
in the dataset
\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}
.
The second term is the first complexity term in the MDL principle, penalizing models with a large number
K
of parameters.
Comparing Eq. (
A96
) with Eq. (
8
), one can see that selecting the MCM with the largest log-evidence,
\log P(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}\,|\,\mathcal{M})
, is equivalent at large
N
to selecting the MCM with the smallest description length,
L(\boldsymbol{\hat{\alpha}}\,|\,\mathcal{M})
.

Appendix E
Additional Figures for data analysis examples

E.1
Entropy of the variables in the data of the Big Five Personality Test.

Figure A2:

Entropy (in base
q
) of the
50
variables in the Big Five Personality Test data
for the two discretization schemes described in Sec.
6.4
with
q=3
and
q=5
.

The two left panels
give the value of the entropy in base
q
(or “qits”) for each variable for each discretization choice. The color indicates the magnitude of the entropy, which can vary between
0
and
1
qit.
The variables are displayed in rows from left to right in the same order as in the original dataset
4
and each row corresponds to one trait (“Ex” for Extraversion, “ES” for Emotional stability, “A” for Agreeableness, “C” for Conscientiousness, and “OE” for Openness to experience).
For example, in the raw data (i.e., scheme 1 with
q=5
), the previous to last variable is the least entropic and corresponds to the question “
I spend time reflecting on things
”.

The right panel
shows the average entropy (red curve) and minimal entropy (blue curve) obtained as one varies the value of
\epsilon
in Scheme 2. For the analysis in Sec.
6.4
, the value of epsilon was chosen to maximize the entropy of the least entropic variable
(as indicated by the vertical dashed line). This is the same value of
\epsilon
that is used in the middle panel.
